                                                     Abstract

         The vision of an ecosystem of general and highly capable autonomous agents challenges
         accepted principles of secure and privacy-preserving system engineering. Inspired by the
         theory of Contextual Integrity, we argue that in order for agents to act appropriately, their
         behaviors should comply with societal norms and expectations. This manuscript explores
         contextual approaches to engineering agentic systems that embody an understanding of
         societal norms and uphold the appropriateness of actions by design. It presents a list of
         key open research problems in this area to create awareness among the broader research
         community across academia, government, and industry.




                                                          1
Executive Summary

The recent step-change in agentic system capabilities, driven by architectures that leverage Large Language
Models (LLMs) to dynamically generate plans, synthesize code, and invoke actions, is transforming the com-
puting landscape into an ecosystem of highly general, increasingly autonomous agents. As their capabilities
grow, proposed AI agents will interact seamlessly with computing systems, humans, and one another to
accomplish longer-running and more complex tasks. However, this vision of general, autonomous agents
challenges known principles of secure, privacy-preserving system engineering, exposing foundational gaps
and requiring research into novel security and privacy primitives.
This manuscript enumerates the resulting research directions, synthesizing deliberations among more than
30 academic and industry leaders at the Google Contextual Agent Privacy and Security (CAPS) Workshop
(New York City, November 2025) and subsequent follow-up discussions.
The core challenge of controlling AI agents lies in precisely understanding which actions are appropriate
in every context in which the agent operates and enforcing such appropriate behavior. Achieving this in
AI agents through conventional security and privacy engineering techniques is challenging due to three
dimensions:

• Unstructured Interfaces and Input Ambiguity: Agents ingest instructions and system context in
  high-dimensional, unstructured formats (e.g., natural language, images). This unstructured way to define
  control flow makes it difficult to clearly define expected behavior or to secure systems against adversarial
  manipulation (e.g., prompt injection).
• Probabilistic, Dynamic Control Flows: Relying on generative planning and real-time code synthesis
  results in probabilistic execution paths dependent on variable or “jagged” model performance. These
  dynamic, ephemeral control flows complicate traditional testing methodologies and cannot be easily
  secured using established secure software development practices.
• Autonomy and Delegation: As agents handle longer-horizon tasks of larger scope, often delegating
  sub-tasks to other agents, traditional oversight mechanisms become less effective. Current approaches
  rely heavily on user-prompted confirmation for information disclosure and higher-risk actions. In highly
  autonomous multi-agent systems, this inevitably leads to consent fatigue, and, combined with more
  abstract task descriptions, increases the chance of unwanted behavior.

This manuscript examines these challenges in the agentic ecosystem through a contextual lens, broadly
inspired by the theory of Contextual Integrity. We ask how we can develop agents that can reason about
and ensure the appropriateness of their actions for the context in which they operate. Achieving this ability
requires new primitives not only at the model level, but also at the systems level (to impose guardrails and
reduce exposure to adversarial data), and at the user level (to effectively interact with and clarify users’
intent). In terms of privacy, we outline open problems in developing agents that inherently understand
and adhere to (i) the data sharing norms and expectations of specific social environments and (ii) users’
personalized privacy preferences, where relevant. This dynamic adherence is vital to complement static
models of explicit user consent, an approach whose efficacy had already been called into question in the
pre-agentic era. In terms of security, we discuss new directions toward systems that uphold contextual
security: systems capable of dynamically adapting permissions, protections, and guardrails to match the
specific scope, risk profile, and context of a given task in real time.
We further discuss how these complexities are compounded when agents interact in an open ecosystem with
other agents, requiring new mechanisms for assessing the trustworthiness of agents, establishing verifiable
human and non-human identities, orchestrating incident detection and response across multiple agents, and
ensuring accountability, non-repudiation, and recourse in the agentic internet.
Ultimately, this manuscript intends to create awareness and serve as a call to action. We urge the broader
research community across academia, government, and industry to collaborate and develop the contextual
foundations for a safe, secure, and privacy-respecting agentic ecosystem.

                                                                                   SECTION 1: INTRODUCTION




             Part I: AI Agents and the Contextual Lens:
                     The Problem and Framework


1     Introduction

AI agents—LLM-based systems capable of autonomously using tools, interacting with external services, and
acting on behalf of users—increasingly impact a wide range of domains spanning personal assistance, software
development, scientific research, and numerous other enterprise, professional, and personal tasks. Unlike
traditional chatbots, these systems can execute potentially irreversible actions while accessing sensitive user
data. As these agent systems become more capable, they will be entrusted to execute increasingly complex,
long-running tasks with minimal human oversight. Furthermore, agents will increasingly interact with each
other, enabling collaborative and coordinated behavior across multi-party settings.
The growing interest in AI agents is driven by the convergence of three trends: (1) LLM performance
generalizes across a broad variety of complex problems; (2) rich data exists in digital formats that agents
can leverage; and (3) LLM-based agents can perform actions through a variety of interfaces (e.g., human-
computer interfaces or documented APIs like Model Context or Agent-to-Agent protocols).
The success of AI agents ultimately depends on users’ willingness to delegate increasingly important decisions
and actions to agents. That willingness, in turn, depends on users’ trust that agents will act appropriately
in accordance with societal and users’ expectations. Security and privacy failures, in particular, can result in
significant harms, including financial loss, unauthorized disclosure of personal information, and reputational
damage. Acting safely and appropriately is therefore of paramount importance to realize the broader promise
of agentic systems with high autonomy.
AI agents create a fundamental tension between capability and control. To be effective at executing tasks
with minimal supervision, an agent must ostensibly have access to all relevant data and tool actions, which
can include sensitive information and irreversible, high-risk actions like sharing data. To then be effective
across a broad variety of tasks, an agent seemingly needs access to a wide range of data and capabilities—an
approach that appears at odds with established security and privacy engineering principles, such as least
privilege, data minimization, and compartmentalization. While giving AI agents access to user data and
interfaces provides significant opportunities, it also introduces risks that are more difficult to assess and
mitigate than those in classic software or narrow-use AI systems.

1.1   A contextual view on trustworthy agentic systems

In this paper, we examine the challenges and open problems in designing trustworthy agents through a
contextual lens, broadly inspired by Nissenbaum’s theory of Contextual Integrity (CI) (Nissenbaum, 2009).
CI defines privacy as information flows that are appropriate given context-specific norms—information flows
that uphold social ends, purposes, and values—instead of merely secrecy or full user control. We adopt this
framing since it offers a guiding principle for bridging broad permissions and strict protections: trustworthy
agents should share data appropriately (Bagdasarian et al., 2024). Sharing data appropriately still allows
agents to achieve high utility since they are not required to keep all user data secret, but it also protects
users in that agents cannot liberally share any data that the user has given them access to (or that they
may have memorized from a different task). More generally, we expand CI to also cover Contextual Security:
ensuring agents behave according to contextual expectations and social norms (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025).
Acting appropriately thus becomes a central theme for secure and privacy-preserving agents.

Figure 1: Model of a general AI agent interacting with human users, tools, and other agents across contexts.
The agent uses a model to generate actions from instructions, memory, skills, or other provided system
context. These actions are executed by a runtime environment. The appropriateness of these actions is
guided by behavioral expectations including social norms and user preferences.


To formalize this view, we define an AI agent as an entity capable of (i) taking inputs and instructions in
unstructured form; (ii) dynamically generating planned responses through the use of a model (typically a
large language model); and (iii) acting out the responses through invoking tools/APIs, communicating with
other users, or interacting with other AI agents. This is further illustrated in Figure 1. An AI agent often
maintains memory of prior interactions, can leverage custom skills, and may operate within an execution
runtime or sandbox. A general-purpose AI agent fluidly operates across diverse social contexts. We define
an agentic system as any software system using one or more AI agents. Building on these foundations, we
define a trustworthy agentic system as follows:


   A trustworthy agentic system must provide utility while acting appropriately under both benign
   and adversarial conditions. More precisely, it must guarantee three core properties:
   • Utility: performing assigned tasks reliably and accurately.
   • Privacy: maintaining appropriateness of information-sharing actions.
   • Security: ensuring that non-information-sharing actions cause no harm.


Note that trustworthy agentic systems must act appropriately both in the presence of attacks from an adver-
sary (adversarial conditions) and in the absence of them (benign conditions). In terms of privacy, an agent
must avoid leaking inappropriate information both under exfiltration attacks and under normal operation
(e.g., due to hallucinations or misinterpretation of instructions). Likewise, when enacting behaviors beyond
personal information sharing, agentic systems should ensure these behaviors are contextually appropriate in
the presence of either attacks or failures that might occur during routine operations.1
The rest of Part I elaborates on these definitions and the contextual view of trustworthy AI agents. §2
refines the above definitions and core concepts used throughout the paper; §3 discusses the various risks and
threat models for AI agents; §4 introduces fundamental principles of Contextual Integrity and the CI lens
for agentic systems; and §5 defines and describes the implications of Contextual Security as an extension of
CI. Finally, §6 poses questions on how we can build agents that operationalize CI.

  1 While the latter requirement and some of the open research problems intersect with the broader field of AI safety,   a
comprehensive treatment of safety is beyond the scope of this paper.

                                                                                 SECTION 1: INTRODUCTION


1.2   Open problems


In this paper, we seek to identify key open and emerging research challenges in realizing trustworthy agentic
systems, and to inspire the community across academia and industry to join us in tackling them. While
achieving these goals poses significant challenges, the development of frontier models and agentic systems
also creates opportunities for rethinking security and privacy. Specifically, language models have shown some
understanding and reasoning about contexts and social norms (Mireshghallah et al., 2024; Yi et al., 2025b).
Part II of this paper therefore examines the open problems in this field from the perspective of a multi-
pronged approach that combines such model-level improvements with system-level mitigations in the agents’
execution environment and user-level or user-interaction improvements:

• System Level: The execution environment can impose additional guardrails to limit the impact of agent
  or model failures and facilitate detection and response. We discuss dynamically limiting agentic capabil-
  ities, establishing agent identity, and authorizing or revoking access to data based on changes in context.
• Model Level: We examine the challenges of teaching models to choose appropriate actions. For example,
  when these actions involve accessing or sharing data, the model must reason about the appropriateness
  of the data flow when prompts are underspecified or ambiguous.
• User Level: At the user interaction level, the autonomy, non-determinism, and conversational nature of
  AI agents have the potential to fundamentally shift user mental models. As a result, traditional consent
  and control paradigms face challenges, as users cannot meaningfully foresee unpredictable execution
  paths, and rigid settings cannot capture complex privacy decisions. We explore how to address these
  challenges by transitioning to dynamic, contextual, and personalized control mechanisms.

Part III then takes an end-to-end perspective and discusses the challenges of deploying and evaluating
contextual privacy and security, the advances in evaluation necessary to measure success, and the governance
and transparency processes that establish the social norms that govern the appropriate behavior of agents.
In summary, the main challenge of realizing trustworthy agentic systems lies in ensuring the appropriateness
of information and control flows across different contexts, i.e., guaranteeing that actions taken by agents
cause no harm while maintaining utility. This challenge leads to multiple open research questions that we
elaborate on in the remainder of this manuscript. While some of these are classic privacy and security
challenges, the agentic vision recontextualizes them. We highlight and summarize the overarching problems
below (see §14 for a full list).

 Open Problems                                                                                      Section

 agentic systems, particularly when plans are dynamically generated?

 agent operate under ambiguous requests and consider the consequences of its actions?

 sure the appropriateness of complex behavior continuously and over long-horizon tasks?
