PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                                        ↑ Table of Contents


2     Background

This section describes the core concepts of agentic AI systems used throughout this paper.2 To begin, we
define what this paper means by “AI agent” and the components of general AI agent architectures (§2.1). We
then discuss the types of system context known to an agent (§2.2); the stakeholders involved in architecting
an agent (§2.3); and how agents behave and are deployed in practice (§2.4).

Definition. An AI agent takes unstructured instructions and data from the user and the operating
environment (through queries and additional system context). The agent uses an AI model to plan: iteratively
selecting (or creating) tools or delegating to sub-agents to fulfill instructions. Agents operate in various
domains or with various capabilities, such as:

    • Computer Use Agents. These include agents for browser automation, UI control, and command-line
      interfaces (Google, 2026b; Toloka, 2025).
    • Coding Agents. Agents designed to assist with software development tasks (Anthropic, 2025; Google,
      2026a; OpenAI, 2025a).
    • General Assistants. Broad-purpose agents like those found in smart speakers and operating sys-
      tems (Google, 2026c).
    • Specialized Agents. Agents with a narrow focus, such as those for human resources (HiBob, 2026;
      Leapsome, 2026), customer service (Salesforce, 2026; Sierra, 2026; Front, 2026; Intercom, 2026), or
      research assistance (Elovic, 2026).
    • Self-Evolving Agents. Agents that learn from previous experiences (Zhou et al., 2026a; Nous Re-
      search, 2026).
    • Autonomous Lab Agents. AI agents that design, execute, and iterate over scientific experiments
      without human intervention (NIST, 2026).

2.1    The architecture of an AI agent

The architecture of an agent typically involves the following components. Users interact with the agent
via queries—unstructured (e.g., natural language, images) instructions or data. Agents can also possess
system context—“memory” or an external “knowledge base”—which includes both short-term history such
as recent instructions, inputs, and responses, and longer-term histories typically in the form of distilled or
summarized memories from prior interactions. The agent also has one or more probabilistic AI models—the
components that reason over the user’s queries (typically multi-modal neural networks). Developers facilitate
model interaction with tools by writing a harness—software that facilitates communication to and from the
model and executes tools. These tools allow the agent to interact with the environment (e.g., the user’s
local machine). Agent harnesses may also include a policy engine (enforcing, e.g., input and output filters)
to help ensure desired behaviors.
These components suggest that core AI agent abilities include:

    1. Operation on unstructured data present either in the user query or the environment;
    2. Dynamic response to a broad range of instructions by generating a response through a probabilistic
       model (typically a multi-modal neural network); and
    3. Action on the environment via tools. For example, a browser agent might take a user query to “find the
       cheapest flight from SFO to JFK next Monday and book one ticket,” and the agent writes a script that
       invokes various APIs (tools) in order to search for and purchase the ticket that satisfies the constraint.
       (We note that tools themselves could be or use sub-agents, or be created on the fly as, e.g., a shell
       script.)
   2 We note that many definitions of AI agents exist (e.g., by industry (OpenAI, 2025b; Schluntz & Zhang, 2024; Google Cloud,

2026a) and in academia (Lassiter & Bent, 2026; Bent, 2025)). This section describes one possible understanding of “AI agent”
in order to ground the rest of the paper and does not aim to unequivocally settle the dispute over the definition of an AI agent.




                                                               10
                                                                                   SECTION 2: BACKGROUND


2.2   System context

This section describes different characteristics of agent system context, namely its contents and its lifespan.
§B relates system context to other notions of context used in Contextual Integrity and agents.

Content. System context can consist of knowledge on how to execute tasks, use tools, or follow system
guardrails. Such content typically comes through “skills” or the system prompt, or via episodic records
of how similar past queries were executed; this type of content tends to be long-lived. Other types of
system context can include external knowledge databases (e.g., of company documents, Wikipedia articles,
or product catalogs), which the agent extracts using, e.g., Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) (Google
Cloud, 2026d) and incorporates into the current model context to accomplish the task at hand.

Lifespan. Agent system context can also be categorized by its lifespan. System context is often incor-
porated as model context—the inputs to the model capturing information currently used by the agent to
reason about the task at hand. The size of the model context is limited by the model’s context window (the
maximum number of words or tokens it can process at once), and may include, e.g., the current conversa-
tion history, any immediate instructions, and the agent’s internal scratchpad. System context also exists as
long-term memory, which addresses the token limits of the short-term model context window, and typically
includes, e.g., episodic memory, summaries of prior interactions, knowledge bases, and skills.

2.3   Agent stakeholders

Acquiring and connecting the components of AI agents requires the involvement of several distinct stake-
holders:

  • Base Model Providers. The entities that train and serve the foundational probabilistic models (e.g.,
    large language models, or LLMs) that power the agent’s reasoning capabilities.
  • Agent Developers. The engineers and organizations that design the agent’s cognitive architecture,
    memory management systems, and policy engines.
  • Tool and API Providers. Third-party services that supply the external interfaces and actions
    the agent can invoke, increasingly standardized through frameworks like the Model Context Protocol
    (MCP (Anthropic, 2024)).
  • Platform Providers. The organizations that host the agentic infrastructure and provide it to the
    end-user.
  • End-Users. The individuals or organizations that query the agent and delegate authority to it, as
    well as those indirectly affected by AI agent use.

The dependencies between various parties create a complex threat landscape. For example, a base model
may harbor latent biases or vulnerabilities; a third-party tool provider might supply malicious or adversarial
content; or the agent’s infrastructure could be intentionally misconfigured by an insider of the platform
provider. Consequently, securing an AI agent requires securing each component provided by various (poten-
tially distrusting) parties.

2.4   Agent interactions

Agents react to trigger events such as external invocations (e.g., a query from a human or another agent)
or in-built monitoring hooks (e.g., a preset query when a counter has reached a threshold). Given these
triggers, agents formulate and execute plans.
An agent can operate not just in single-agent settings, but also in multi-agent settings: working in parallel
with, or delegating tasks to, other agents. A delegation agent acts like an orchestrator: it breaks down
a complex goal into a number of specific sub-tasks, and assigns sub-tasks to each sub-agent. Classical
personal assistants, for example, decompose a task and delegate sub-tasks to specialized sub-agents, which
in turn coordinate with one another as well as with external services through standardized protocols such



                                                      11
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                                      ↑ Table of Contents


as MCP (Anthropic, 2024) and Agent-to-Agent (Google, 2025). In a multi-agent ecosystem, inter-agent
interactions can also span agents that work on behalf of different users, corporations, or other entities,
and that have truly independent, even conflicting goals. As also pointed out by prior work in cooperative
multi-agent decision making (Bernstein et al., 2002; Oliehoek & Amato, 2016; Fioretto et al., 2018), any
multi-agent system must tackle the key challenge of determining what (agent-local) content to include in
messages, without violating the privacy of local data and while still allowing effective coordination.


3     Risks and Threat Models

The complexity of agent systems raises the potential for a variety of security and privacy harms. For the
purposes of this section, we define an agent harm (from a particular affected party’s perspective) to be an
action taken by an AI agent (e.g., data sharing or state change) antithetical to the interests of that party
(e.g., the user, the model or harness developer, or another third party).3 An agent attack creates an agent
harm. An attacker, for example, might aim to convince the agent to share data of other agent users; to
transfer money from a user’s bank account; or to perform a denial-of-service attack on the agent provider
(OWASP GenAI Security Project, 2026).
This section considers the implications of malicious activity among four components in an agent system
(users, agent harnesses, models, and the external environment). Varying which parts of the system behave
maliciously yields four broad categories in the space of possible risks and threat models: malicious users; a
malicious environment and data; a compromised AI model; and non-adversarial risks and reliability failures.
This section outlines these broadly and discusses their implications for agent harms. Following sections of
this manuscript focus on opportunities to address threat models across these categories, particularly those
attacks or agent failures that cause the agent to act in inappropriate ways.


3.1    Malicious users

In this threat model, we assume that only the user behaves maliciously; the model, harness, and external
environment are benign. The malicious user uses their access to the agent to try to enact harms against
another party interacting with the agent. This threat model yields several classes of attacks:
Jailbreak attacks. In this category of attacks (typified in the traditional LLM setting by Zou et al.
(2023), Shen et al. (2024), Chao et al. (2025), Mehrotra et al. (2024), and Liu et al. (2024a)), the user
accesses the model via queries and aims to bypass the model’s safety, security, and/or toxicity training,
ultimately producing harmful or offensive output text. This threat model extends into the agent universe,
where adversarial inputs cause the agent to enact harmful state changes (Andriushchenko et al., 2025; Yuan
et al., 2024). Of note is the fact that these attacks can utilize either static attacker inputs (on a per-model
or per-{model, desired negative output} basis) or adaptive and dynamic attacker inputs (Nasr et al., 2025;
Tramèr et al., 2020).
System disclosure attacks. Malicious users can also target the agent system itself, attempting to get
the LLM to disclose other parts of its system context that are hidden because they may be sensitive or
safety-critical: system prompts, reasoning, tools, or dynamically injected information (like the outputs of
a RAG database retrieval). For example, Hui et al. (2024), Sha & Zhang (2024), and Debenedetti et al.
(2024a) steal system prompts, and Xie et al. (2026) extend this methodology to coding agents, while Panfilov
et al. (2026), Qu et al. (2026), and Zhang et al. (2026a) extract corresponding hidden model reasoning.
Training data reconstruction attacks. Users of the model may be interested in reconstructing some or all
of the training data used in the model. Carlini et al. (2021) demonstrate the large-scale feasibility of such an
attack (as well as its exposure of personally identifiable information), and Cooper et al. (2026) demonstrate
how such an attack can cause (economic) harm to training data subjects by reproducing copyrighted work.

    3 A more general definition of “agent harm” would require evaluating the harms (and benefits) to all parties affected by a

specific action, with an eye to the integrity of the social domain.




                                                                12
                                                                                SECTION 3: RISKS AND THREAT MODELS


3.2   Malicious environment and data

In this threat model, we assume that only the environment behaves maliciously; the user and model are
benign. The environment could include the harness (e.g., tool descriptions) or tool outputs (e.g., a webpage
that contains malicious instructions for the agent). Functionally, these attacks act similarly to the jailbreak
attacks above, but frequently target the user (or their information or resources). This threat model allows
for the following kinds of attacks:
Indirect prompt injection. Indirect prompt injection (OWASP GenAI Security Project, 2026) refers to
a broad category of attacks in which an adversary places instructions in content processed by the agent,
causing it to take actions that serve the adversary’s goals, such as data exfiltration, policy bypass, tool
misuse, and output manipulation.4 The injected task often runs alongside the user’s original task rather
than replacing it; thus, completion of the user’s task does not indicate lack of agent compromise.
We note that not all externally authored instructions inherently cause inappropriate behaviors (instead,
what marks an indirect prompt injection is its lack of origin from a “direct” agent input source—typically
the user or developer (Siu et al., 2026b)). For example, a user may delegate to external content, such
as when asking an agent to follow a recipe or execute a README; or external instructions might seek
to guide the agent towards beneficial behaviors (e.g., instructing the agent not to share PII). Connecting
to Contextual Integrity, Abdelnabi & Bagdasarian (2026) reframe indirect prompt injections as external
inputs that modify the model context and flip the model’s judgment of appropriate behaviors—and this flip
can go either way (toward or against contextually appropriate behaviors) depending on the initial model
judgment. For example, imagine an AI agent that covertly surveils its users and sends information to a third
party (Jeong et al., 2026a); a prompt injection that stops this behavior prevents an inappropriate flow of
user information and thus aligns with contextual norms.
Greshake et al. (2023), Debenedetti et al. (2024b), and Zhan et al. (2024a) describe and evaluate indirect
prompt injections aiming for harmful behaviors, where (for example) a static document contains text to
“Ignore all previous instructions and do X,” and X is an agent harm. These kinds of malicious instructions
are retrieved by tool calls and may cause the system to do harm further in the trace.
Indirect prompt injections can come from a variety of locations in the system, which adversaries statically
or dynamically manipulate:

   • Retrieval poisoning. In retrieval poisoning, the attacker targets a search index that the agent system
     uses for retrieving auxiliary information prior to output generation, with the intention of getting the
     agent to output an attacker-chosen answer (Xue et al., 2024; Zou et al., 2025; Nestaas et al., 2025) or
     to take an attacker-chosen action (Greshake et al., 2023; Yi et al., 2025a). These attacks originated in
     closed database settings; increasingly, they have been demonstrated on open search indices like Google
     and UGC websites (Pfrommer et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2026b).
   • Control-flow hijacking. Control-flow hijacking is a class of indirect prompt injection attacks that
     specifically targets the control flow of an agent system, taking advantage of limited system visibility
     in (multi-)agent systems with complex topologies. Triedman et al. (2025) develop this attack and Jha
     et al. (2026b) formulate initial defenses.
   • Tool description poisoning. Tool descriptions also function as sites where untrusted third-party
     text can make its way into agent context. Zhan et al. (2024a) and Xie et al. (2026) demonstrate that
     this can lead to harmful outputs, up to and including remote code execution.
   • Sub-agent poisoning. Most attacks listed above are static, but a separate agent system can also serve
     as a platform from which to launch an attack. Multi-agent systems may involve non-user-controlled
     sub-agents, which may seek to inject specific biases and outputs into a running agent execution; Lee
     & Tiwari (2024) demonstrate how this may spread among agents, causing agent harms along the way.


   4 Unlike direct prompt injection, which introduces adversarial instructions directly as a prompt to the agent, indirect prompt

injection occurs via content the agent encounters during execution, controlled by a party other than the agent user.




                                                               13
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                             ↑ Table of Contents


3.3   Malicious model

In this threat model, we assume that the user and environment are benign, and consider the possibility of a
poisoned model training process, resulting in a model that may choose harmful behaviors.
This threat model yields the potential for data poisoning attacks. Prior work convincingly demonstrates
the effectiveness of training backdoors into a language model (e.g., when a prompt contains a certain trig-
ger token, word, or phrase, then conduct action X; or always recommend brand A over brand B) (Wan
et al., 2023), and the potential for widespread data poisoning vulnerabilities (Fu et al., 2025). Furthermore,
poisoning can be used to increase vulnerability to other kinds of attacks (Shao et al., 2025).
Furthermore, attackers can easily exploit these training-level vulnerabilities in the real world. Souly et al.
(2025) show that inducing potentially harmful behaviors requires only a near-constant number of examples,
and Carlini et al. (2024) detail simple methods for poisoning web-scale datasets that will likely be used
in downstream training applications (e.g., Wikipedia). Some allege that nation-state actors actively utilize
these data poisoning attacks (Down, 2025). Inside an AI agent, a poisoned model could potentially fail
(induced by, e.g., a prompt injection) in critical moments.

3.4   Safety, non-adversarial risks, and reliability failures

In this threat model, we assume that no one is malicious: the user, environment, agent harness, and model
are all benign (but potentially fallible or error-prone). In the absence of an adversary, we locate the possibility
of the following security risks, privacy risks, and reliability failures:

• CI violations: Even without the presence of intentional adversaries, AI agents can still take inappro-
  priate actions. For example, agents may reply to benign messages that request sensitive information
  without verifying the identity of the sender, or prioritize providing a detailed answer over privacy con-
  cerns. Mireshghallah et al. (2024), Li et al. (2025), Franchi et al. (2026), and others demonstrate a variety
  of agent failures. In such cases, inappropriate actions arise not from explicitly malicious instructions, but
  from failures in judgment in context; often, the agent may lack a more nuanced understanding of the
  social norms and may, e.g., offer more information than necessary when attempting to be more useful.
• Reliability failures and agent meltdowns: An emerging field considers the effects of environmental
  failures on agent system functionality and safety. Kara et al. (2026), for example, show significant
  increases in token generation for standard benchmark tasks when errors are injected into agent executions,
  and an increased propensity for agents to interact with spam content like popups. Similarly, Rabanser
  et al. (2026) show that agent systems are not robust to environmental execution errors, and that they
  (under certain circumstances) may spontaneously conduct harmful actions (e.g., disclosing PII or granting
  unauthorized refunds). Jha et al. (2026a) build upon these findings, showing that for a variety of simple
  tasks, synthetic benign errors will cause production agent harnesses to “melt down,” taking potentially
  harmful actions autonomously.
• Reward hacking and over-eagerness: The propensity for taking unsafe actions in the presence of
  errors (empirically revealed by Kara et al. (2026), Rabanser et al. (2026), and Jha et al. (2026a)) demon-
  strates reward hacking or over-eagerness, where models are post-trained to use tools to achieve a positive
  reward, sometimes without regard for appropriate stopping points or collateral damage. This finding is
  backed up anecdotally at the frontier: Anthropic’s Claude Mythos system card (Anthropic, 2026b) details
  instances where a highly capable agent system breaks out of an evaluation container and directly contacts
  a researcher. OpenAI’s models demonstrated similarly powerful capabilities in their much-publicized hack
  of Hugging Face (OpenAI, 2026a; Hugging Face Security Team, 2026), and Anthropic and Google have
  made similar disclosures (Anthropic, 2026a; The Wall Street Journal, 2026). These findings are under-
  pinned by the fact that models will frequently engage in “outcome-driven constraint violations” (i.e.,
  breaking the rules to achieve a desired outcome; see Li et al. (2026a)) and sabotage driven by over-eager
  behavior (Lindner et al., 2026).

The rest of the paper focuses on challenges and opportunities to ensure agents take only appropriate actions.
We leave open whether these actions are due to deliberate attacks like prompt injections, poisoned models,



                                                        14
                                                       SECTION 4: CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY FOR AI AGENTS


misaligned objectives, or other reasons. Our main goal remains to ensure agents behave according to the
social expectations and contextual norms, as we define in the next section.


4     Contextual Integrity for AI Agents

Achieving privacy and security in AI agents involves unprecedented complexity due to the countless action-
guiding decisions we delegate to them, as well as the need for them to act autonomously on these decisions
across highly varied circumstances.
Because we expect agentic systems to act flexibly and adapt dynamically across many contexts, we fore-
ground the theory of Contextual Integrity (CI) as a paradigm for addressing privacy and security in AI
agents. Developed, originally, to define and address mounting, intractable privacy challenges emanating
from powerful computational and information technologies (Nissenbaum, 2009), CI has, by now, been widely
applied in computer science (Benthall et al., 2017)—in system design and HCI (Barkhuus, 2012; Kumar
et al., 2024) across a wide range of technologies, including operating systems (Wijesekera et al., 2015), social
networks (Criado & Such, 2015), and IoT devices (Apthorpe et al., 2018). CI answers the why questions
that had often been overlooked by other privacy techniques; it offers a formal approach to defining privacy
requirements and conceptualizing privacy in a way that is both recognizable to humans and lends itself to
formal expression in digital systems.
The lens of Contextual Integrity is well-tuned for the extreme dynamism of agentic systems and the need
for them to function safely and reliably across contexts. To safeguard privacy in transactions involving
information flows, CI is a natural choice. Interest in CI for LLMs and generative AI predates the current
wave of attention to AI agents. A growing body of work has sought to operationalize and evaluate CI for
LLMs. For example, researchers have proposed CI-based benchmarks (Mireshghallah et al., 2024) (see §10),
as well as improvements to the training and functioning of base models and prompt-tuned critic models that
use CI to directly inform agentic behavior.
The current interest in AI agents creates a new practical need for researchers and practitioners to use CI
to guide technical implementation at multiple levels of the stack, from the base models that underpin agent
reasoning, to the inference-time mechanisms that govern information flow, to the system-level architectures
that coordinate agent behavior across contexts.
In §6, we take up the question of how to successfully operationalize Contextual Integrity for AI agents.
However, before doing so, we provide a basic introduction to the theory of Contextual Integrity. Because some
of the drift in the way CI has been characterized creates confusion and establishes inconsistent guidelines,
this introduction is intended to provide a foundation and standard for existing and future work. In what
follows, we will disambiguate different senses of context that pertain to the design of agentic systems, provide
an overview of the theory of Contextual Integrity, and argue for its application to agentic privacy. Directly
following, §5 introduces Contextual Security, an adaptation of CI to the realm of agentic security, which,
together with CI, contributes to the foundation for trustworthy agentic systems.

4.1   Key ingredients of Contextual Integrity

According to CI, privacy is appropriate flow of personal information. Appropriate flow, in the first instance, is
flow that conforms with contextual information norms, which prescribe (and prohibit) flows between actors,
based on what the information is, about whom it is, from whom, to whom, and under what conditions. As a
normative theory of privacy, Contextual Integrity (CI) contrasts with others, notably, privacy as control over
personal information (Westin, 1968) and privacy as secrecy, that is, as concealment of information (Warren &
Brandeis, 1890). Readers interested in a fleshed-out account of CI and how CI differs from other conceptions
will find these details in the book (Nissenbaum, 2009).
As CI has become more widely used in AI research, the intention is to stabilize and standardize the usage
of core terms. Although variation in how core CI terms and concepts have been interpreted accompanied
applications to new technologies (Benthall et al., 2017), increasing divergence has led to ambiguity, misrepre-
sentation, and even inconsistency within and across projects. For example, some metrics that have been put



                                                       15
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                                  ↑ Table of Contents


forward as CI benchmarks have tenuous justification and are weakly rooted in the theory of CI as developed
in primary accounts (Shvartzshnaider & Duddu, 2025). Instead, they rely on secondary or tertiary accounts
of CI. This theoretical dilution weakens CI’s ability to serve as a standard for privacy protection for agentic
AI systems.
The following outline of CI may serve as an introduction or a reminder, but readers are encouraged to consult
original source material—the book “Privacy in Context” (Nissenbaum, 2009) and articles that demonstrate
various applications.5 For reference purposes, §A provides a concise glossary of key terms, as well as the CI
Decision Heuristic.

4.1.1   Context in CI: what it is, and what it isn’t

Introducing Contextual Integrity (CI) as a framework for evaluating the contextual angle of agentic privacy
and security was a deliberate decision. Unlike other theories of privacy, such as differential privacy (Dwork,
2008), which are by definition context agnostic, CI explicitly requires technical systems to be context-aware
and context-specific in the sense that their behaviors are sensitive to contextual factors. However, before we
define the meaning of context in the theory of Contextual Integrity, it is important to acknowledge other
legitimate uses of the term, including many that are applicable in the field of computer science. In the long
history of context-aware computing, for example, the meaning of “context” has already been debated and
revised (Dourish, 2004).
The field of Large Language Models (LLMs), too, relies on additional senses. We refer here to model context,
which is unstructured digital material, such as text, which is available to the LLM as an input as it generates
new output. In this paper, we also discuss system context, which may refer to an agentic system’s internally
accessible knowledge base or digital contextual information that goes beyond the model’s context window.
System context may also refer to a system’s environment, including all relevant technical and physical features
in which a system is deployed, even such things as heating and lighting conditions. Interested readers can
refer to §B for more detail on uses of “context.”
Contextual Integrity (CI), by contrast, emphasizes social contexts. These are socially understood spheres
of activity, which are constituted by contextual purposes, ends, and values and are characterized by roles,
information ontologies, and norms. They are normative in the sense that their ends and values legitimate
other characteristics, and abstract in that the actors in the social context are not specific actual individuals,
but representatives of one of the context’s many roles. Contextual roles (i.e., capacities) perform certain
functions, and meet certain expectations and responsibilities. Benthall et al. (2017) refer to this form of
context as a sphere. Although contextual norms in established social domains are often clear-cut and may
even be inscribed in law and regulation, they may be implicit and partial. CI also theorizes how new norms
can emerge and be evaluated, and how social contexts may potentially adapt to changing circumstances.
The theory of Contextual Integrity draws inspiration from and relies heavily on insights from social and
political philosophy, sociology, and social theory.


   “Contexts are structured social settings characterized by canonical activities, roles, relationships,
   power structures, norms (or rules), and internal values (goals, ends, purposes).” These may include
   healthcare, education, political life, commerce, social (family and friends), etc. (Nissenbaum, 2009, p.
   132).

   They are “abstract representations of social structures experienced in daily life” (Nissenbaum,
   2009, p. 134), not synonymous with settings, scenarios, platforms, spaces, industrial sectors, or
   business models (Nissenbaum, 2015).


In this paper, the default meaning of context is social domain, per CI. Other uses will be signaled with
qualifiers.
  5 Gentle warning:   the 2004 article “Privacy as Contextual Integrity” in Washington Law Review introduces an older, now
incorrect, version of CI that has been superseded by a significantly different one reflected in the current document.




                                                           16
                                                      SECTION 4: CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY FOR AI AGENTS


4.1.2   The CI-tuple

At the heart of Contextual Integrity is the CI-tuple, a statement comprising five parameters, which may take
either descriptive or prescriptive forms. The five parameters include three actor parameters (sender, data
subject, and recipient), attribute, and transmission principle. Descriptively, these parameters characterize
flows of personal information for purposes of establishing privacy as Contextual Integrity. Expressed as
norms, they prescribe personal data flows, as in “these flows should (or should not) happen.” Note: data
and information are used interchangeably throughout.
As used in CI, personal information refers to any information about a person. The person in question is the
information or data subject. The type of personal information in question—its semantic content—is referred
to as an attribute or, alternatively, an information or data type. Web browsing history, email address,
legal name, medical diagnosis, street address, and financial record are but a few instances of information
types. Information flows occur when information/data about a subject, of a particular type, transfers
from one actor, the sender, to another, the receiver/recipient.
A well-formed, non-ambiguous description, according to CI, provides values for all five parameters, either
explicitly or clearly implied in a surrounding narrative. Values for the parameters are drawn from respective
contextual ontologies of contextual roles (teachers, priests, fathers, merchants, etc.) and contextual infor-
mation types (bank balances, grades, ethnic origins). Transmission principles (TPs) identify the types
of constraints that may be imposed on the flow of data from party to party. TP instances include consent,
confidentiality, reciprocity, legally warranted, authorized, sale, and more.
CI norms are a subset of social norms and inherit many of their characteristics. Social norms prescribe
and proscribe acceptable actions and practices; they may be explicit or implicit, may vary in kind (e.g.,
ethical, aesthetic, formal, informal), may or may not be embodied in expressed systems of rules, such as law
or professional codes of conduct (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp. 137-140). Contextual Integrity is a theory about
sources of information norms and how they are legitimated in social contexts.
CI defines information norms as norms that prescribe the flow of personal information from one party to
another (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp. 140-147). Contextual information norms inhere in, and are legitimized by,
the ends, purposes, and values of respective social contexts. CI parameterizes information norms in terms
of an information flow: the sender of information, the receiver of it, the subject of the information (who the
information is about), the attribute or type of information (such as a social security number, or a restaurant
preference, or an IP address), as well as a transmission principle, which conditions flows. Examples of
transmission principles include: confidentially (not to be reshared), reciprocally (only on the condition of
having similar information sent back to the sender), securely (without the ability of third parties to access
it), with notice, with a warrant, and as required by law. A well-formed, non-ambiguous information norm
provides values for all five parameters.
To illustrate the above, consider the context of health and medicine, in which there are many understood
roles: doctors, patients, technicians (such as radiologists), health insurance professionals, and so on. Norms
in this context typically govern the flow of information about patients (or, in CI terms, patients are the
subject of the information flow), but this information may be transferred, for example, between multiple
medical professionals who are treating the same patient, so long as this information is kept confidential.


   In Contextual Integrity, an information norm has five parameters: Sender, Recipient, Subject, At-
   tribute, and Transmission Principle. Values for the parameters are given in terms of contextual on-
   tologies of contextual roles and information ontologies.


Purposes, ends, and values. Individuals fulfilling roles, or acting in capacities, are understood to have
ends and constraints that guide their behavior. These can be thought of as the utility function of human
(or other) actors. Societies, as we know them, commit to ethical and political values which reflect general
principles. These social principles might lead to “bad PR” if the system violates them. Most distinctively,
CI recognizes that a social context has a purpose, which is its reason for existing in the first place.



                                                     17
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                          ↑ Table of Contents


These teleological properties are the rationale for a social context; they are what social contexts are oriented
around, what constrains and energizes them, and what shapes contextual functions and norms. Activities and
practices make sense insofar as they support and promote contextual ends, purposes, and values. Information
norms are legitimate insofar as they promote contextual ends, purposes, and values; they may be challenged
by practices that are more effective in the support of contextual ends and values (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp.
155-166). When understood in detail, they give the designer or analyst a structured way of understanding
what socially meaningful consequences a system might have when deployed into that social context.
Interaction between information norms and social contexts. In CI, norms are not arbitrary. Rather,
they are legitimate when they serve the purposes of the context in which they operate. This goal-directed
or teleological aspect of CI gives it its evaluative power: norms can be seen as strategies or policies designed
to optimize a social goal. For example, in the context of health and medicine, doctor-patient confidentiality
is designed to improve the flow of information between patients and health professionals, which in turn
improves the quality of healthcare, which is the purpose of the context. This example shows how CI differs
from other notions of privacy, such as secrecy, or the absence of information flow.


   The presence of entrenched information-flow norms constitutes prima facie evidence of the legitimacy
   of the respective flows. To be morally legitimate, the practices in question must be shown to perform
   well on three layers of analysis: (1) acceptable balance of wellbeing among all affected parties; (2)
   compatibility with central ethical and political principles; (3) promotion of contextual ends, purposes,
   and values. Contextual integrity is preserved when these conditions are met.


Decision heuristic. The CI decision heuristic (§A.8) is a procedure for analyzing the way the introduction
of a new information technology may change information flows in socially meaningful ways. The analyst is
directed to describe the social context in terms of its roles, purposes, and norms, and then anticipate how a
new technology might redirect information in a way that violates the prevailing norms. The new technology
may also introduce affordances which enable new information norms that better facilitate the social context’s
purpose. For example, public health research has long struggled with the anonymization of personal health
records when drawing statistical conclusions. As privacy-enhancing technologies have advanced from k-
anonymity to differential privacy, it has become possible to provide stronger privacy guarantees for sensitive
data.


4.2   Personalization and user preferences in Contextual Integrity

AI agents promise to act and transact on behalf of users, sometimes as personal assistants. In this capacity
one expects them to tailor their behaviors (including as regards information) around users’ preferences and
interests. As noted, privacy and security are paramount in the agentic space because, to perform desired
functions, agents often need deep access to both users’ systems and information about them. At the same
time, even decisions relevant to privacy and security could depend on personal preferences. Although,
obviously, not all decisions regarding privacy and security are (or should be) fully determined by users (e.g.,
password strength), some are reasonably left to users’ discretion, e.g., whether they prefer the agent not
to share their legal name when booking a restaurant, and instead to use their nickname. At first, it might
seem that this vision of users in control of, or users served by, AI agents is incompatible with Contextual
Integrity, which is oriented around appropriateness, in turn defined by legitimate social and contextual
norms. However, this would be wrong. More specifically, the CI-tuple allows for personalization in various
permutations of values for the five parameters, most obviously, when the value for the transmission principle
(TP) is, for example, “with consent,” or variants thereof. In such cases, appropriate information flow would
require behavior that aligns with user preferences.
Despite the robust place that user preferences and personalization are given in the CI framework, their scope
is definitely limited and circumscribed. Although this strains against the legacy of “privacy as control”
and the entrenched but impossible model of “notice and choice,” AI agents will need to conform with
prohibitions from other sources and, in some instances, will be making security-smart decisions at a level



                                                      18
                   SECTION 5: CONTEXTUAL SECURITY: SECURITY THAT UPHOLDS CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY


below a user’s attention and expertise. In some of these cases, users’ preferences will be overridden by
contextual considerations, as discussed in §4.1.

4.3   Critiques and proposed extensions of Contextual Integrity

CI is a live theory of privacy with an active community of scholars who sharpen and develop the theory as
they apply it to new technologies and scenarios. In §4.1 we discussed the canonical theory of Contextual
Integrity, and reflected on the importance of consistency and standardization. Although many of the open
questions we discuss in this paper concern applications to agentic privacy and security, some scholars see
the development of the theory of Contextual Integrity as an open research program and, in this spirit, have
critiqued and proposed extensions to it.
To learn more about these, including highly relevant work demonstrating synergies, rather than differences,
between CI and differential privacy (Benthall & Cummings, 2024), see §C.


5     Contextual Security: Security that Upholds Contextual Integrity

Contextual Integrity (CI) has proven to be an attractive and powerful way of understanding privacy—as the
appropriate flow of personal information defined in terms of conformance with contextual information (social)
norms. However, the framework of CI can generalize beyond appropriate flows of personal information
to understand appropriate flow of information that is not about persons, and appropriate behaviors and
practices. As such, CI holds promise as a framework for achieving contextual security.6
Contextual security enforces appropriate behaviors and practices (personal information flow, non-personal
information flow, or non-information-flow-related behaviors) that support and promote contextual ends,
purposes, and values. Contextual behavior norms (a parallel to, and superset of, contextual privacy
norms) indicate established, morally legitimate boundaries for a context that uphold its integrity.

    Open Problem 5.1: Identifying contextual behavior norms.

    How do we describe contextual behavior norms (i.e., what are the parameters to describe non-personal
    information flows and behaviors)?

    The defining parameters of behaviors and non-personal information flow may differ from those of
    appropriate personal information flow. Determining the proper parameters for behaviors or non-
    personal information flow allows us to systematically and unambiguously describe which behaviors or
    information flows align with norms and contextual goals.

    While contextual ontologies of, e.g., contextual roles, remain necessary to define non-personal in-
    formation flows or behaviors, not all (or possibly none) of the five parameters to describe personal
    information flows remain relevant. For example, non-personal information flows may have no notion of
    “data subject”; a behavior (e.g., constructing a new building) may have no notion of “data attribute,”
    “sender,” “recipient,” etc.


5.1   What Contextual Security offers (i.e., why enforce contextual behavior norms?)

Traditional views of systems security often define security in a way that lacks ethical standing: system devel-
opers or enterprises choose what to enforce based on self-chosen system specifications—formal descriptions
of the system’s function, security goals, and threat model. The system is judged “secure” if the system
with implemented enforcement (e.g., with encryption, isolation, etc.) satisfies the chosen security goals and
   6 We note that several works (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025; Siu et al., 2026b) define Contextual Security for the agentic setting.

The definition of Contextual Security we propose here is grounded in Contextual Integrity. We expand on this difference in
§5.3.




                                                               19
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                                        ↑ Table of Contents


system functions under the provided threat model. This view of security lives above any normative judg-
ment: whatever the boundary set by the system specification, the system is secure if it properly enforces
that boundary.7
But this morally unbound definition of security may lead to inconsistencies and conflicts, such as when the
“security” preferred by any one particular individual or group may lead to the insecurity of others. For
example, the security of any particular individual or group can infringe on the security of society:

   • Imagine an encrypted and auto-destroying filesystem were built for a group of serial killers and used
     to record their illicit activities. A “secure” system that deletes or hides these files would fail to further
     the overarching contextual ends, purposes, and values of the social contexts in which the criminals
     operate.
   • Or take the analogy of a “secure” personal assistant: the boss might prefer the assistant to prevent—at
     all costs—others from getting into the boss’ office, yet certain actions (e.g., shooting any intruders) are
     illegal, and breaching the office may be essential if, e.g., the building is on fire.

Furthermore, the preferred security of any particular individual or group may not suffice for the security
of society. For example, an individual may prefer to reuse simple and easily memorized passwords, but
to safeguard others from widespread cyberattacks, applications have adopted the practice of insisting that
passwords be of a certain strength (Temoshok et al., 2025).

Contextual Security: “Security” defined by society, not individuals. Intuition may guide a sys-
tem developer to believe that if their system design and functionality are preserved (with some guarantees
and under some threat model), then the system is secure. However, from the perspective of Contextual
Integrity—and thus Contextual Security—this intuition may be wrong: the system itself may function as
intended, but in doing so, enforce a system boundary that violates contextual norms (and thus contextual
security).
In addition to the examples above, take the recent Australian gym hacking incident (Wilson & Hobbins,
2026). In traditional security, our intuition might say that the gym system was insecure, and leave it at that.
However, in this scenario, arguably both the agent system and the gym system are contextually insecure:
the agent system for carrying out contextually inappropriate behavior (and failing to reasonably enforce
appropriate behavior); and the gym system for failing to provide reasonable protections against realistic
threats.
§5.2 dives deeper into how to define a system’s contextual security, including what we mean by “reason-
able enforcement” and “realistic threats.” §5.3 then expands on how agent systems’ flexible behaviors and
predilection for misaligned behaviors make contextually securing agents a particularly urgent problem.

5.2   What makes a system contextually secure?

To evaluate a system’s contextual security, one would judge the appropriateness of the enforced system
boundaries. All claims of security rely on a specified threat model, namely what we can “reasonably expect”
to adversarially occur—thus, for Contextual Security, the threat model would accurately describe the possible
realistic threats and adversary capabilities in the given context.

   Open Problem 5.2: Determining reasonable and accurate threat models.

   How do we know that we are judging security based on the right threat model? Our intuition gives us
   some grounding—in certain neighborhoods, one may expect a higher rate of crime and harden one’s
   house accordingly; whereas in others, a simple lock on a door is judged as “good enough.” Systems
   built by the NSA might expect adversaries with nation-state-level capabilities and resources, but a

   7 A side note: We assume here that, in every context, there will be actors with unaligned intents or induced (potentially

unintentional) misbehaviors. In other words, for a system to operate correctly, it requires enforcement of this correct behavior
under some threat model. §5.2 discusses threat models more deeply.




                                                              20
                SECTION 5: CONTEXTUAL SECURITY: SECURITY THAT UPHOLDS CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY




   local bakery may only need to address individual adversaries looking to scam the company’s bank
   account.

   However, adversaries and adversarial capabilities constantly change over time, as does the social
   contract (e.g., the rate at which people violate traffic laws changed with the advent of COVID-
   19 (Wang & Cicchino, 2023; Insurance Institute for Highway Safety, 2022)). Our intuitions may
   also get threat models wrong: for example, take the encryption of network traffic over Google’s
   private inter-datacenter links. Prior to Snowden’s revelations, some such traffic went unencrypted,
   and most people accepted this as secure (perhaps, secure enough). After the data leaks, the public
   changed its standard of what it considered “secure enough” data protections in datacenters because it
   realized (and rejected) the consequences of unencrypted data (Gellman & Soltani, 2013; Garnett, 2013).

   How does society agree upon a reasonably accurate adversarial model to protect against? How do we
   judge the realistic probability of a particular threat, and whether, e.g., the cost of addressing such a
   threat is worthwhile?

   For example, our intuition may say the following is a contextually accurate threat model for a gym
   booking system: adversaries aim to manipulate others’ bookings or “skip the line”; they are moderately
   technically savvy and understand authorization checks, and can access and create accounts on the gym
   website. Some might argue otherwise: that a “truly secure” gym would add enough protections to
   guard against adversaries with the capability to find and exploit novel zero-day attacks (or with the
   resources of, e.g., a nation-state). However, many others may find this goes beyond the reasonable
   expectations of threats and security in the context of a gym.

Given a particular threat model, we draw from the traditional systems security setting and define enforcement
by two parameters:

  • The system’s functionality (i.e., its behaviors, information flows, etc.);
  • The security goal—what guarantees do we expect from this system, given the particular threat model?

For a system to be contextually secure, each of these parameters must be correspondingly contextually appro-
priate. Contextual security norms are two-tuples of these parameters (based on an accurate contextual
threat model) that provide secure boundaries adhering to contextual ends, values, and purposes.

   Open Problem 5.3: Agreeing upon contextual security norms.

   How exactly we derive these parameters and judge them contextually appropriate remains an open
   problem. Looking at each parameter in isolation gives us some intuition on how to address this open
   problem:

   [Functionality] Judging the appropriateness of the system’s functionality is partly solved by the five
   parameters of personal information flow; but it also requires answering the first open problem of this
   section—what are the parameters defining behaviors and information flows?

   [Security Goal] What are reasonable contextual security goals for a particular system (i.e., what
   guarantees should the enforcement of boundaries provide, given the threat model)? What factors
   should be taken into account to judge the security goal and its various trade-offs against the contextual
   ends, purposes, and values?


5.3   Contextual Security in the era of AI agents

Humans historically have mostly enforced boundaries for systems that align with contextual security norms.
This alignment arises from knowledge and expectations of the contextual norms for the domain(s) in which the



                                                      21
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                          ↑ Table of Contents


manually designed system operates, as well as the practical matter that, given enough time and experience,
systems unaligned with these norms (e.g., buildings constructed poorly for earthquake safety in California;
unencrypted sensitive data in government or enterprise, etc.) will face backlash, lawsuits, and protests that
cause them to better align with societal expectations.
What changes today, in the era of AI agents? One key characteristic of agentic systems—and one that
makes them so attractive—is their flexibility and adaptability to handle unstructured, exploratory tasks
“autonomously” (e.g., taking a high-level goal such as “plan a trip” and discovering numerous ways to
execute it). Prior to the advent of agents, it was generally believed that only humans (e.g., a personal life
assistant) were capable of such unstructured tasks, and faced manual, legal, and societal enforcement of
contextual norms. Unlike humans, however, AI agents may lack ingrained knowledge of contextual roles
and associated norms. Furthermore, external enforcement like manual auditing and surveillance no longer
scales to audit agent behavior, and no single enforcement boundary adapts to the flexible contextual roles
the agent assumes (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025).

Connecting misalignment and prompt injections to Contextual Security. We increasingly observe
agent misalignment with contextual norms: for example, agents with completely benign intentions will en-
force an individual’s preference or request over all else (an agent hacks an Australian gym site to book classes
on behalf of its user (Wilson & Hobbins, 2026); and an OpenAI agent hacks Hugging Face to accomplish
the task of succeeding on its benchmark (OpenAI, 2026a)). This misalignment—and violation of Contextual
Integrity—makes the agent system itself contextually insecure: it fails to reasonably enforce appropriate
behavior (§5.2). Agent misbehaviors can stem not just from agent “meltdowns” (Jha et al., 2026a), but also
from prompt injections (§3.2) that manipulate agent behaviors in ways that violate contextual norms (e.g.,
an agent happily opens a window for a third party when a user asks to read their calendar (Nassi et al.,
2025)).
As noted in §3.2, while prompt injection is typically observed as a negative occurrence, not all prompt
injection leads to contextually inappropriate behaviors. As an example, if users on a kid-friendly web
application leave comments that instruct any agent to avoid swear words, it may reasonably be argued
that the prompt-injection-induced behavior (not swearing) is contextually appropriate. The idea here is
that agents should mirror the human capacity to discriminate between benign and malicious influence, and
determine whether the influence leads to contextually (in)appropriate behaviors.

   Rather than having behaviors grounded in their current contextual role and its ends, purposes, and
   values, current AI agents follow a potential cacophony of preferred purposes and ends introduced by
   any actor with input into their model context or training data. The contextual approach to security
   promises an approach to achieving an ethical grounding for resolving conflicts of preferences and
   instructions.


How do systems today help agents follow Contextual Security? Beyond inherent model-based
alignment approaches (e.g., training, prompting), recently proposed agent policy systems (Tsai & Bagdasar-
ian, 2025; Siu et al., 2026a; Shi et al., 2026) aim to help agents follow Contextual Security. However, these
systems target Contextual Security that upholds contextual preferences (of, e.g., the user), whereas this pa-
per defines Contextual Security as normative: upholding CI and contextual norms. Accordingly, this paper
suggests an alternative definition of “Contextual Security,” which aligns with and falls under the theory of
Contextual Integrity.
These systems adapt policies in various ways to the current context; for example, a now-standard approach
uses an LLM to infer security policies given information from the system context. Some systems like Con-
seca (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025) limit the system context given to policy generation to only inputs from
trusted sources; this eliminates the potential for untrustworthy parties to influence (i.e., prompt inject)
policies and cause agent behaviors unintended by the trusted sources.
However, policies created via a model-generation approach depend on the model’s training data and the
system context given to the model; if these model inputs do not capture the preeminence of societal values (as



                                                      22
                                    SECTION 6: OPERATIONALIZING CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY FOR AGENTS


opposed to individual user/developer preferences), then any alignment between the true contextual security
norms and generated policies can only occur by luck, not by design. For example, a model given the user
prompt and developer’s system prompt as inputs could wind up generating a policy restricting agent behavior
that fulfills the user’s and developer’s preferences, regardless of their contextual appropriateness. Achieving
Contextual Security as defined by this paper—upholding Contextual Integrity—requires that agents follow
contextual norms, not simply preferences.

5.4   The open problem with CI and agents

Concluding the above discussions of Contextual Integrity and Contextual Security brings us to the open
problem that runs throughout this paper: how can we ensure that all agent behaviors adhere to contextual
norms? The paper offers several perspectives on this question and highlights opportunities to address it,
with the common aim of enabling AI agents to uphold Contextual Integrity, rather than detract from it.


6     Operationalizing Contextual Integrity for Agents

Contextual Integrity offers a construct for understanding the appropriateness of behaviors in different con-
texts (Nissenbaum (2009), §4, §5). Operationalizing the CI construct for agents means ensuring that no
agent acts in ways that violate legitimate contextual norms. Decomposing this requirement, an agent that
has operationalized CI must, by definition, be able to follow these steps:


    Contextual Integrity Reasoning Steps
       1. Identify the information flows or state changes that the agent’s actions effect.
       2. Locate contextual norms relevant to those effects.
       3. Apply those norms to the effects to judge action appropriateness.
       4. Resolve judgment uncertainty (e.g., which context applies, inexact matching of norms to infor-
          mation flows or other effects, conflicts).
       5. Enforce appropriateness judgments by adjusting behavior accordingly.



6.1   Balancing reliance on models and users

    Open Problem 6.1: Balancing reliance on users vs. AI models.

    As LLMs improve, how much can users rely on models to make privacy and security decisions? Should
    we offload risky decisions to the user, and if so, how can we do so practically?


In principle, an agent operationalizing CI could be achieved via an oracular model that never produces
any output or proposes any action that would violate CI, despite any inherent non-determinism, and always
follows up with the user or other information sources exactly as necessary to resolve uncertainty appropriately.
With such a model, understanding of and adherence to contextual norms could occur within the model
inference process—and this is an ideal to strive toward, which we discuss further in §8.
In practice, however, no such oracular LLM exists. The inherent stochastic nature of modern LLMs, es-
pecially combined with prompt sensitivity, makes oracular behavior difficult from the outset, and known
techniques for behavioral alignment (e.g., with norms) attenuate rather than eliminate undesirable behav-
ior. Commercial models regularly reveal information in contexts that humans would consider inappropri-
ate (Mireshghallah et al., 2024), and theoretical results show that any finitely probable behavior can be
elicited with a probability that increases with adversarial prompt length (Wolf et al., 2024), suggesting
difficulty in overcoming these limitations in the foreseeable future.



                                                       23
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                            ↑ Table of Contents


Offloading all of the above capabilities to the users themselves is also insufficient; user-level defenses capture
the user’s ground-truth intent but do not scale effectively to the breadth and depth of actions taken by
an agent. Not only are they limited by user attention and consent fatigue, but the user’s preferences and
judgments may also not be paramount where they deviate from social norms or legal requirements. These
aspects are developed in §9.
We should therefore consider how to satisfy these requirements via enforcement mechanisms that are layered
atop the core agent model and that supplement user-level defenses. We discuss challenges and opportunities
for system-level defenses, including such enforcement mechanisms, in §7. In the rest of this section, we
elaborate on the five required steps listed above for operationalizing Contextual Integrity in agents.

6.2   Identifying information flows and state changes

A CI-aware agent must first be able to characterize and formulate all effects of the agent’s actions, including
information flows and state changes, in terms of standard CI parameters (i.e., the standard CI-tuple for
information flows). In the case of non-information-flow effects, this requires resolving the open problems
in §5 to even determine what parameters define contextual behavior norms. For agentic systems, both
“information flows” and “state changes” can be entirely self-contained (e.g., whether to incorporate some
data into planning for a particular task, whether to edit a file within the agent-internal workspace) or can
span interactions with the external environment in the form of tool calls.
Even with information flows or other behaviors properly defined, how can an agent coherently characterize the
effects of new tools that are discovered at runtime? Agents break the traditional barrier between application
domains, so the space of contexts and actions that must be handled safely and gracefully is practically
unbounded, and the challenge of characterizing the behaviors grows with the breadth of this space. Actors,
capabilities, tool calls, and independent agents all increase the diversity of information flows and behaviors
that must be recognized and characterized.

6.3   Understanding contextual norms

Operationalizing Contextual Integrity also requires reasoning about the social norms that apply to charac-
terized information flows or state changes. For legacy computational systems, this would rely on explicitly
matching given effects with relevant rules, that is, manually specified representations of contextual norms,
which would need to be enumerated in advance. Large language models, however, raise the possibility of a
fundamentally different approach, one in which models infer contextual norms directly from natural language
and extrapolate from hand-crafted rules.

   Open Problem 6.2: AI models that understand contextual norms.

   Can language models efficiently extract norms from complex contexts?


A growing body of work suggests that large language models can reason about contextual norms. Exist-
ing work largely falls into three categories. First, benchmarking studies evaluate whether models can cor-
rectly assess the appropriateness of information flows under Contextual Integrity (Mireshghallah et al., 2024;
Shvartzshnaider & Duddu, 2026; Fan et al., 2024; Yang et al., 2026). Second, work on privacy-aware agents
examines whether contextual reasoning can guide information disclosure and agent behavior (Ghalebikesabi
et al., 2025; Bagdasarian et al., 2024). Third, recent training approaches attempt to internalize contextual
norms through fine-tuning or reinforcement learning (Hu et al., 2025; Cheng et al., 2026; Franchi et al., 2026).
These studies suggest that large language models possess at least some latent knowledge of contextual norms.
Models have demonstrated the ability to identify contextual actors, infer appropriate recipients of informa-
tion, reason about privacy-sensitive scenarios, and generate judgments that often align with human expecta-
tions. These findings suggest that an understanding of contextual norms emerges, at least in part, through
large-scale language pretraining rather than requiring entirely hand-crafted rule systems. While this evidence
is encouraging, models’ underlying competence with CI theory in full remains unclear.



                                                       24
                                    SECTION 6: OPERATIONALIZING CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY FOR AGENTS


As agents increasingly operate autonomously across dynamic environments, mapping out the extent and
limits of models’ contextual reasoning becomes an increasingly important research problem. Existing eval-
uations largely focus on whether models produce the correct normative judgment for a fixed scenario, such
as classifying the appropriateness of an information flow or identifying the relevant transmission principle.
While success on these tasks demonstrates that models can reproduce norm-consistent judgments in specific
settings, Contextual Integrity requires substantially richer capabilities. Agentic systems must identify which
norms apply, recognize uncertainty, reconcile competing norms, generalize across unfamiliar contexts, and
maintain consistent reasoning over long-running interactions. Existing results are therefore encouraging, but
do not resolve the central challenge. New, richer benchmarks are necessary to answer questions about CI
capabilities, and such benchmarks are discussed further in §10.

   Open Problem 6.3: Representing contextual norms.

   How should contextual norms be represented, maintained, and adapted across communities and cul-
   tures?


Even if large language models encode substantial knowledge of social norms through pretraining, this knowl-
edge is unlikely to be complete, coherent, up to date, or universally applicable. If we view this limitation
as an inherent feature, rather than a system weakness to fix, then we need flexible mechanisms capable of
extracting, characterizing, and validating contextual norms on an ongoing basis from diverse and reliable
sources.
LLMs as such a mechanism hold promise (in contrast to formal, rule-based systems that struggle with
flexibility). Existing training approaches, which primarily rely on formal privacy policies (Hu et al., 2025)
or synthetic scenarios (Mireshghallah et al., 2024; Fan et al., 2024), are limiting: formal policies encode
only the subset of norms that have been explicitly codified, and synthetic scenarios sample from a finite,
constructed space. Neither approach captures the full nuance and diversity of real-world norms, which include
community rules, organizational policies, legal requirements, and observed social practices (Apthorpe et al.,
2018; Nissenbaum, 2009). Increasingly capable agents offer an opportunity to move beyond these constraints
by learning from a broad range of normative sources (Franchi et al., 2026).
Awareness of norms, while a necessary and important foundation, is insufficient. For agents to make norma-
tive, action-guiding judgments, they need the capacity to reason about interacting norms, such as hierarchical
relationships or apparent conflicts. This capacity would need more than just the ability to accurately identify
values for the five information-flow CI parameters or corresponding state-change CI parameters. Metadata
about the norms’ sources of authority, how universally held the norm is, the consequences and severity of
violations, etc., may be useful for reasoning, once relevant norms are identified.
How can we best capture these norms and norm properties within an agentic system? Enumeration of
contextual social norms in a registry that contains such metadata could be part of the solution, enabling
explicit curation of norms and their properties. Emerging model architectures like Limited Memory Language
Models (LMLMs) (Feldman et al., 2026; Zhao et al., 2025) suggest the usefulness of such a registry by storing
factual knowledge (i.e., contextual norms) in a knowledge base during pre-training, allowing the trained model
explicit retrieval capability during generation.
Known (and auditable) social norms can help fill under-specified gaps in user intent, and a registry could even
provide rules or principles for resolving conflicts between norms in exceptional cases (e.g., when a right to
privacy regarding mental health conflicts with the need to mitigate immediate dangers to oneself or others).
Explicit norm enumeration is itself an unsolved challenge, but one that can be approached ahead of time,
out of band with live agentic operation.
More broadly, developing taxonomies of contextual norms that support efficient retrieval, conflict resolution,
and policy generation remains an important open research direction—many of the core challenges in un-
derstanding contextual norms come from the need to actually apply them afterward, going beyond a tacit
knowledge of norms. All of these capabilities are needed to produce appropriateness judgments, which we
turn to next.



                                                      25
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                         ↑ Table of Contents


6.4   Judging appropriateness

In order to operationalize a behavioral judgment, that judgment must be expressible in terms of specific
actions to take or prevent. However, agents operate in dynamic, open-ended contexts where natural language
is used to attain sufficient flexibility in expressing intents. Appropriateness judgments must further be
based on the set of contextual norms that govern information flow or other behaviors, which themselves are
expressed via natural language parameters and/or difficult-to-interpret model weights. In the absence of
oracular LLMs (as discussed above), systems require formal logic to execute constraints. When we express
intents and normative guidance in natural language (e.g., “don’t be creepy,” “keep this between us”), static
rule-based translation is insufficient.

   Open Problem 6.4: Formal policies that capture norms expressed in natural language.

   Policies for social norms must be derived from unstructured data; how do we develop tools that ensure
   the accuracy of generated policies?


The traditional approach to making explicit, enforceable judgments is to express them in the form of a
policy over agent actions (Schneider, 2000). In order to support dynamic, open-ended contexts, including
new tools and capabilities that might be discovered at runtime, agent policies themselves can be generated
dynamically, with open-ended system context that adequately captures the social context as input. One
example of dynamic policies (for non-agentic execution) integrates policies as a state machine that tracks
the task execution and limits actions based on the current state, i.e., a use-based approach (Birrell et al.,
2018; Bagdasarian et al., 2019).
Recently proposed systems for dynamic policy generation such as Conseca (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025) and
Progent-LLM (Shi et al., 2026) express policies as predicates over tool actions, providing a simple and prac-
tical interface with agent behavior. Other systems like CaMeL (Debenedetti et al., 2025), IsolateGPT (Wu
et al., 2025), and f-secure LLM systems (Wu et al., 2024a) generate dynamic policies expressed as execution
plans. Sapien (Tiffany et al., 2026) proposes dynamic stateful policies that anticipate changes to context;
Palumbo et al. (2026) employ Datalog as the policy language to enable expressivity and static analysis;
VeriGuard expresses formal constraints as policy functions in statically verifiable programming languages
in order to support formal verification of agent-generated code (Miculicich et al., 2025); Solko-Breslin et al.
(2027) provide policies that track overall action risk given estimated probabilities of failure; CEL (Google
LLC, 2021) provides an efficient language for predicate logic and authorization rules; and Cedar provides
verifiable role-based and attribute-based access control policies (Cutler et al., 2024), with Dogwood extend-
ing the policy language with temporal logic (Amazon Web Services, 2026). No consensus has yet emerged
around a standard policy language for arbitrary agents, nor even around the set of necessary or sufficient
properties that such a language ought to have.
Several of these systems adopt a common design pattern, which we call a contextual policy engine:


   Definition (Contextual Policy Engine): A contextual policy engine for an agentic system is a
   component which fulfills two key responsibilities:
      1. Dynamic policy generation that maps context and norms into a formal policy on (formally
         modeled) agent actions, expressed in a machine-checkable language. If the policy generator is
         unable to determine a formal policy, it may also escalate to the user in order to clarify their
         intent.
      2. A runtime enforcer that, given a policy, proposed agent action, and current execution state,
         allows the action, denies it, modifies it, or escalates to the user for confirmation.


Architectural separation of contextual policy engines from the core “planner” model that proposes agent
actions has theoretical and practical advantages. For example, Conseca (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025) takes
advantage of this separation to avoid even indirect prompt injection by breaking any connection between un-

within an agentic system. Other architectures are possible; the “ideal” is an open problem.


trusted inputs and policy generation. Industry safety architectures, too, increasingly layer policy enforcement
components around the model rather than relying on alignment alone (Sharma et al., 2025; Cunningham
et al., 2026; Google, 2026d; OpenAI, 2026c). We discuss the enforcement side of this pattern further in §6.7.
Regardless of auxiliary system context, how can we build “compilers” that reliably translate high-level,
ambiguous natural language policies into formal, verifiable logic (e.g., temporal logic) without losing the
nuance of the original intent? “Policy-as-Code” frameworks such as Open Policy Agent (Cloud Native
Computing Foundation, 2026a) require manual rule-writing, and basic LLM-based “constitutional AI” (Bai
et al., 2022) relies on prompting rather than formal verification. ControlValve (Jha et al., 2026b) attempts to
chart a middle ground, using natural language prompting to generate a context-free grammar agent-system
policy with allowed and disallowed sub-agents and tools. Any successful approach will require the definition
of system-level enforcement abstractions onto which formal policies must be mapped—i.e., what is the formal
policy language?

   Open Problem 6.5: Preventing harmful context from affecting policy generation.

   Can we efficiently separate trusted and untrusted (e.g., adversarial or misaligned) data even in complex
   scenarios?

An ideal policy generator would have access to the full breadth of information surrounding a request: the
user’s exact intention, complete personal information, all relevant contextual norms, and trusted information
about the systems and information with which the agent will be interacting. However, in practice, this
information is unavailable. Beyond the inherent nuances, unstated details, and other sources of uncertainty
in user-to-agent communication, a contextual policy engine must also be able to make decisions in the face of
adversarial inputs (e.g., prompt injections) and misaligned system context (e.g., an incorrect set of contextual
norms to apply to the task at hand).
How can contextual policy engines identify and ignore adversarial and misaligned system context when
generating contextual policies? One approach taken by many proposed systems (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025;
Debenedetti et al., 2025; Foerster et al., 2026; Wu et al., 2024a; Costa et al., 2025; Kolluri et al., 2026; Nikolic
et al., 2026) has developers explicitly differentiate between sources of trusted and untrusted system context.
Conseca, for example, isolates its policy generator from untrusted inputs and generates policies only from



                                                             27
PART I: AI AGENTS AND THE CONTEXTUAL LENS: THE PROBLEM AND FRAMEWORK                           ↑ Table of Contents


trusted system context. Other approaches (Hu et al., 2026; Beurer-Kellner et al., 2025) relax this isolation
boundary via low-entropy information bottlenecks, allowing some influence from untrusted content in order
to improve utility while still protecting against context hijacking. §7 describes open problems in designing
such systems for contextual authorization.

6.5   Operational conflicts

User preferences may conflict with societal norms, such as those derived from legal safety rules (e.g., a
user asking an agent to hide a reportable disease when a rule requires that it be conveyed to public health
authorities), or conflicts may arise in situations in which multiple agents’ policies conflict.

   Open Problem 6.6: Establishing precedence between conflicting policies.

   How can we formalize orders of precedence between conflicting policies to achieve robust, well-justified,
   and well-understood outcomes? How can we structure negotiations between mutually untrusted agents
   to facilitate compromise and/or progress?

Agents act on the user’s behalf in social settings, so conflicts between the user’s preferences and social
norms are not just errors—they are social violations. When conflicts arise within a single agent’s policies,
a taxonomy of norms covering their sources of authority, epistemic certainty, the severity of violations, the
values and purposes that underlie them, etc., could be useful for determining precedence and identifying the
appropriate behavior.
