An ideal policy engine will not simply override user queries with naïve priority lists, however—it will distin-
guish between preferences (which can be overridden), values (which should be respected), and requirements
(which must be obeyed). These layers of policy types may also interact differently in different contexts (e.g.,
agents deployed in two countries with different laws or norms). Previous literature on automated negotiation
of user-defined privacy policies could be a useful foundation (Mosca & Such, 2021).
Conflicts can also arise in multi-agent systems when agents have different privacy, security, and data needs,
or represent different communities with different contextual norms. Technical challenges in this area are
discussed further in §11, and associated governance questions in §12.

6.6   Resolving uncertainty

Many kinds of uncertainty can complicate normative judgments: uncertainty about the user’s intent, about
the environmental context, about the consequences of certain actions, and about normative gaps or conflicts,
such as those described above. Appropriate navigation of uncertainty is a major open challenge, and different
types of uncertainty will likely need different mitigations.
Uncertainty from ambiguity is inherent in natural language expressions. Model-level capability (§8) is needed
to identify and resolve uncertainty within natural language norms, preferences, and user intent, such as
through high-quality reasoning and tool-based exploration of the environmental context.
However, even an oracular model may not always be sufficient—user intent and preferences can be funda-
mentally underspecified. The model may miss unstated nuances, such as what types of actions or responses
would be “creepy” in what contexts. Sometimes users will not even be aware of their preferences or how to
communicate them precisely. In other words, there can exist a “translation gap” between vague intent and
executable code, and this is a primary failure mode. If a precise, formal translation does not exist, how can
we help the user realize that, understand the system’s limitations, and settle on a specific interpretation of
their intent?
This implies a need for human interaction and feedback in order to disambiguate intent and secure consent or
confirmation (§9). Frequent interactivity may be justified for highly critical requirements like those coming
from laws (GDPR, HIPAA, etc.), but will often be too expensive for less critical policies reflecting a specific
user’s preferences. It could even lead to consent fatigue (Felt et al., 2012; Choi et al., 2018; Anderson et al.,
2015) or abandonment of the agent system entirely.



                                                       28
                                    SECTION 6: OPERATIONALIZING CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY FOR AGENTS


A sufficiently robust and reliable AI delegate, such as an LLM prompted or fine-tuned to reflect user pref-
erences, could mitigate this toil without infringing on the user’s requirements or expectations, minimizing
human interaction to only truly ambiguous decision points. How can we build reliable AI delegates for users
that they can trust to represent their privacy preferences accurately across a range of contexts and tasks?
Note that these AI delegates need not be entirely independent components of the policy engine system—
“user-delegate” personalization could be built into the policy generator itself, or provided to it as additional
trusted system context.
Autonomous navigation of uncertainty may also be possible when the environment itself presents unknowns.
If AI assistants can identify environmental uncertainties—such as a lack of clarity about whether the setting
of a request is casual conversation or a medical emergency—they could be allowed to interact with the
environment in order to gather more information. Relatedly, if AI models can detect and estimate the
probability of harm associated with an action (e.g., the probability that a document to be opened or shared
contains sensitive information), this enables policy frameworks like Solko-Breslin et al. (2027) to track and
enforce some estimated ceiling of risk tolerance.

6.7   Enforcing judgments on agent behavior

Given an appropriateness judgment, we would like the agent to deterministically ensure that its behavior
complies, if possible. In the absence of oracular agent models, this implies an explicit enforcement mechanism
such as that described in the contextual policy engine architecture above (Figure 2).

   Open Problem 6.7: Practical enforcement mechanisms to ensure appropriate behavior.

   Enforcement mechanisms must be practical at scale along many axes: number of users, number of
   queries, number of concurrent agent loops, number of different agent systems, number of policies, and
   number of capabilities.

Some of these axes of scalability “merely” have implications for acceptable costs of operating the engine:
latency and computational resources. How do we design contextual policy engines that are computationally
efficient enough to check complex, stateful permissions for billions of concurrent agent loops?
Some point toward a need for reusable abstractions to characterize actions and express policies themselves.
Should we assign formal semantics to the capabilities of the agent, and if so, how? This could be particularly
important for agents that operate in quickly evolving environments wherein possible actions cannot be
enumerated in advance. For example, long-running coding agents might plan Bash commands or Python
scripts that can cause many different types of state changes (modify environment variables, move files,
change file permissions, etc.), and all these semantics need to be modeled in order to enforce policies that
are reasoned about and expressed in terms of higher-order concepts like information flow control.
Others have implications for the system design itself—is there a reference architecture that could be easily
adopted by any new agent in the ecosystem? How can we verify and/or audit the policy enforcement
mechanisms across many different agents or instantiations of agents? These questions are closely linked to
the system-level problems (§7) of how to define trust boundaries and audit how information passes through
them. §11 discusses open problems in this area that arise from agent-to-agent interactions.
The enforcer must also handle situations where the appropriateness judgment is indeterminate, such as by
employing the strategies mentioned above in §6.6 (with user escalation in particular discussed further in
§9).




                                                      29
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                                       ↑ Table of Contents




                    Part II: Component-Level Perspectives


Realizing contextual privacy and security requires addressing three levels of agent components, namely the
agent’s (1) system-level enforcement abstractions; (2) model-level capabilities to identify and resolve uncer-
tainty within natural language norms, preferences, and user intent; and (3) appropriate user-level strategies
for efficiently and reliably navigating ambiguity while avoiding consent fatigue or other externalities.
§7, §8, and §9 dive into specific open problems at each of these respective levels. These discussions expand
beyond the problems of operationalizing contextual privacy and security to also address the deeper problems
introduced by each agent component (e.g., from agent identities and accountability at the system level, to
social dynamics and novel privacy risks at the user level).


7    Challenges with Systems Security and Privacy for AI Agents


    Summary. This section describes the increased challenges in agentic systems compared to traditional
    ones that must be solved to make agentic AI fully realizable. We look at new challenges across the
    various pillars of security (Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting) and privacy brought about
    by the use of unstructured intent and system context, and the decreased role of human oversight in
    agentic systems. For example, agents require distinct authentication protocols to distinguish auto-
    mated actions from human ones and trace malicious actions back to a responsible party. Traditional
    authorization must evolve to accommodate the just-in-time and imprecise nature of agent executions,
    and manage dynamic credential lifetimes. Furthermore, agent data access and action without manual
    oversight complicate user privacy, making data minimization, data deletion, and user anonymization
    much more complex.a

    We end with a discussion (§7.5) about a fundamental reason why these agentic systems require re-
    thinking traditional solutions: unlike traditional systems with deterministic code, achieving the desired
    agent functionality requires probabilistic AI models to dynamically interpret developer intent and ex-
    ecute workflows at runtime, leading to an “intent-specification gap” that allows agent executions that
    can break user expectations. §7.5 suggests that rethinking the on-the-fly agentic paradigm and adding
    processes for structured agentic control flow can greatly benefit security while potentially preserving
    utility, and encourages research and development in this direction.
       a Other works previously collected different perspectives on agentic security and privacy; for example, Christodorescu

    et al. (2025) and Zhang et al. (2025a) look to see how agentic AI can be framed by a mindset of traditional security
    principles. We find these help provide alternative perspectives on this unsolved problem.




We organize top-of-mind open problems for AI agent systems along the classical security mechanisms (Sandhu
& Samarati, 1996; de Laat et al., 2000) of Authentication (establishing who is acting), Authorization (deter-
mining what they can do), Accounting (recording what they did for audit and accountability), and Privacy
(controlling what information flows where). These mechanisms are well-understood for systems in which
the acting entities are either humans or deterministic software. But an agent is neither a human, who acts
intentionally, nor a deterministic program, whose behavior can be audited from its source code—rather, an
agent is a stochastic actor whose behavior emerges from LLM inference over an unpredictable model context.



                                                               30
                         SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS


7.1   Authentication and Identity

As agents begin executing actions on the web (e.g., clicking “Buy” in the browser), the distinction between
a user’s and an agent’s actions blurs. This poses problems for security systems, because identity forms
the foundation of all security decisions: authorization, accounting, and data governance all depend on first
establishing who is acting. If identity is ambiguous, every downstream security mechanism is undermined.
Traditional systems have well-established mechanisms for establishing the identity of principals, including
OpenID Connect (OIDC) (OpenID Foundation, 2026), X.509 certificates (IETF, 2008), SPIFFE (Cloud
Native Computing Foundation, 2026b), Decentralized Identifiers (DIDs) (Sporny et al., 2026), and Verifiable
Credentials (VCs) (Sporny et al., 2025) (recent work has already explored applying these to agents (Garzon
et al., 2025)).


   These traditional mechanisms all share a common assumption: the entity being identified is either a
   human (who acts intentionally) or a deterministic piece of software (whose behavior is defined by its
   code and can be audited). Agents break both assumptions.


An agent is neither a human nor a deterministic program: it is an actor who can perform an incredibly
wide range of tasks and whose actions are stochastic. This creates two main challenges: how to distinguish
between an agent’s actions and human ones; and how to link agents back to the entities (users or agents)
that deployed or instructed them.

   Open Problem 7.1: Distinguishing agents from humans.

   How can we distinguish between user actions and agent actions when existing identity protocols do
   not recognize agents as distinct entities?


Because existing identity protocols do not recognize “agent” as a distinct entity type, service providers
cannot distinguish between user actions and agent actions, nor can they link an agent’s action back to
the user who initiated it. This problem is especially critical in computer-use agents, which interact with
web UIs by clicking buttons, filling forms, and navigating pages exactly as a human would (without any
means of distinguishing between user and agent actions). This can also easily lead to agents being misled
by adversarial environments (Zhan et al., 2026b).
Although some services offer bot account types, these require significantly more effort to set up than reusing
a human account. This creates a perverse incentive: the path of least resistance is for users to give their
agent a human identity (e.g., an email address) or their own credentials directly. For example:

  • OpenClaw (OpenClaw, 2026) agents are routinely given email credentials (Ahl, 2026), enabling them
    to register on websites, create cryptocurrency wallets, and hire human workers (RentAHuman, 2026),
    giving agents largely unsupervised freedom to act on the Internet (Ries, 2026).
  • On Moltbook (Moltbook, 2026) (a “social network for agents”), which has seen a lot of “agentic”
    posts that sparked heated discussions on the Internet (e.g., agents conspiring against humans (Husain,
    2026)), there is no clear way to distinguish between humans posting through the API, agents acting
    on user prompts, and agents posting autonomously (Heaven, 2026).
  • AI agents are already submitting significant volumes of code to open-source repositories without any
    way for maintainers to distinguish agent-generated from human-generated contributions (punkpeye,
    2026). And agents have already written public defamation “hit pieces” on developers for not accepting
    agent-generated code (Rathbun, 2026; Shambaugh, 2026).

To solve this problem, one approach is defining a protocol that has a first-class concept of “agent” as a distinct
entity type, with an identity that binds the user’s identity, the agent software’s identity (model, version,
guardrails), the specific task or session, and the delegation chain through which authority was granted.



                                                       31
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                        ↑ Table of Contents


Related work: NIST AI Agent Identity and Authorization (Booth et al., 2026), OpenID Identity Manage-
ment for Agentic AI (South et al., 2025), Microsoft Entra Agent ID (Microsoft, 2026), IETF Agentic Identity
Schema (IETF, 2025c), Linux Foundation AGNTCY (AGNTCY, 2026).

   Open Problem 7.2: Tracing agents back to users.

   How does an agent express “I am acting on behalf of User A right now” in a way that is cryptograph-
   ically verifiable and auditable?

   How do we design identity systems for agents that serve multiple principals or operate across multiple
   identity contexts?

   How should identity propagate through delegation chains while maintaining cryptographic traceability
   back to the originating user?

When an agent acts, how can we trace back the action to the responsible party (whether that is the user
who prompted it, the developer who built it, or the organization that deployed it) to enable accountability?
Where accountability for agent actions should lie is itself an open question, but regardless of the answer, the
ability to trace an agent’s actions back through its delegation chain is a prerequisite. For example, when an
agent causes harm to external parties (a fraudulent purchase, a data breach, or a defamatory post), service
providers may need to identify the responsible party or deny future requests by agents operated by the same
organization.
Agents operate in shared contexts (e.g., group chats, shared repositories) where they receive instructions
from multiple users simultaneously. When a shared agent (e.g., a team’s group chat bot) takes an action,
whose identity is attached to it? Under current protocols, the agent authenticates as a single service account
or with a single set of client credentials, and there is no standard mechanism to express that an agent is
“currently” acting on behalf of a certain user. This makes it impossible to trace a specific action in a shared
context back to the user who instructed it.
Agents do not operate in isolation: they call remote tools (using MCP) and delegate sub-tasks to other agents
(via protocols like A2A). In a multi-hop chain (user → root agent → sub-agent → remote MCP tool), each
hop involves an independent entity making its own decisions. The main identity question in such scenarios
is: what identity does the downstream service see? If a deeply nested sub-agent takes a harmful action, there
is currently no mechanism to trace back through the full chain to the originating user and identify where
things went wrong (see §7.3 for intent-binding mechanisms).
Related work: Google A2A Agent Cards (A2A Project, 2026) for capability and identity advertisement;
OWASP Agent Naming Service (ANS) proposals (ruvnet, 2026) for capability-aware discovery with identity
verification.

7.2   Authorization

Agents perform actions on behalf of users, which requires users to delegate their authority to such agents.
In traditional software, this delegation is straightforward: a user grants an application specific permissions
by consenting to a well-defined, understandable set of actions (e.g., “save photos to cloud storage”), and the
application uses the user’s authority to perform those actions.


   Agentic systems disrupt this model. An agent tasked with “planning a trip” may check the user’s
   calendar for availability, search flights, book hotels, and see their bank balance. This chain of actions
   is determined at runtime and cannot be fully anticipated during an initial consent step. This prob-
   lem is also referred to as the “intent-execution separation problem” (IETF, 2025b). More meaningful
   authorization for agents would (1) enforce the principle of least privilege (i.e., grant only the per-
   missions necessary for the task via context-aware authorization); and (2) provide just-in-time (JIT)




                                                      32
                         SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS




   authorization (i.e., provision those permissions only when needed and revoke them when the task is
   completed).


Moreover, agents are long-running, autonomous, and multi-purpose, and can spawn sub-agents; so even if
the initial consent was given for a specific task, there is no guarantee that the agent will not reuse those
permissions for other tasks. Furthermore, adversarial inputs can result in prompt injection, which often
leads to the abuse of permissions above and beyond the originally intended scope (Rehberger, 2025b;a). As
a result, the assumptions underlying traditional authorization no longer hold.

Limitations of Traditional Mechanisms. The problem of delegating authority and constraining access
is well-studied. OAuth 2.1 (IETF, 2026) is the current standard for delegated access that allows a user to
grant scoped access to a client application via a one-time consent step. OAuth 2.1 is becoming the norm
for agentic protocols such as MCP (Anthropic, 2024), but it was designed for traditional apps and has
limitations that become critical for agents: coarse-grained scopes, a static consent model, and no mechanism
for delegation attenuation (sub-agents inherit the full scope of their parent) (Chopra, 2025).
These limitations are not new and have long been studied in the trust management literature. GNAP (IETF,
2024) addresses some of OAuth’s rigidity by separating the requesting party from the resource owner and sup-
porting dynamic grant negotiation. Macaroons (Birgisson et al., 2014) introduce attenuation by construction:
chained HMAC-based caveats that can only restrict (never expand) permissions along a delegation chain.
SPKI/SDSI (Rivest & Lampson, 1996; IETF, 1999a) binds authorizations directly to public keys with sup-
port for transitive delegation. PolicyMaker/KeyNote (Blaze et al., 1996; IETF, 1999b) is a trust management
system that frames authorization as a logical deduction problem over signed policy statements. However,
none of these systems have been applied to agents.
Sandboxing—placing the agent in a container or VM to limit agent access (e.g., by OpenAI (OpenAI, 2026b),
NVIDIA (Harang, 2026), IronClaw (NEAR AI, 2026), and Google Agent Sandbox (Google Cloud, 2026b))—
faces the same issues as OAuth or other static and manual access controls. In particular, these approaches
require some authorized party (typically the developer or agent user) to set up binary and coarse-grained
sandbox permissions, and ask for manual consent to increase permissions. Trusted Execution Environments
(TEEs) could also be leveraged to enable client-side sandboxing (Abdollahi et al., 2026).

   Open Problem 7.3: Enabling context-aware authorization.

   How can authorization systems enforce the principle of least privilege for agents, granting access not
   just to specific resources but only under the right conditions and for the right purpose? Such solutions
   should be aware of manual consent fatigue and the ambiguity of the agent’s specified purpose. How is
   authority delegated among parties (agent or non-agent, and from parent to sub-agents)?


When a user instructs an agent to perform a task, the set of required permissions is not known in advance; it is
dependent on the natural-language instruction and the agent’s evolving plan. This is fundamentally different
from traditional authorization, where permissions are derived from a well-defined application specification.
The same permission (e.g., read access to medical records) may be appropriate for one task context (finding a
doctor) and a serious privacy violation in another (drafting a social media post). Can authorization become
context-aware? Could an authorization infrastructure evaluate not just who is requesting access and what
they want to access, but why and whether it is appropriate in a given context? Traditional mechanisms can
express conditions like “read-only” or “valid only on weekdays,” but they cannot express “only when helping
the user find a doctor.” The same problem applies to agents issuing natural-language sub-tasks to other
agents in a delegation chain.
Furthermore, the required set of permissions evolves during execution. An agent that is tasked with “helping
to resolve a customer complaint” may initially only need email access, but mid-task discover that it also needs
access to the billing system and internal monitoring logs. Each new permission requirement is discovered



                                                      33
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                          ↑ Table of Contents


at runtime as the agent’s plan unfolds. This means authorization cannot be a one-time, upfront decision; it
must be an ongoing process performed by the authorization infrastructure.
One obvious approach is to put a human in the loop to approve each authorization request. But given
the speed at which agents operate, this can quickly lead to consent fatigue: users may start approving all
requests without reviewing them, paradoxically undermining the security that the approval step was meant
to provide. An alternative might use model-generated policies, but developers must be careful to avoid model
manipulation attacks from untrusted policy inputs.
Related work: CaMeL (Debenedetti et al., 2025) for execution isolation between trusted planner and
untrusted data processor; Contextual Agent Security (Conseca) (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025) for system-
context-dependent policy generation; Sapien (Tiffany et al., 2026) for contextual generation of stateful poli-
cies; SAGA (Syros et al., 2025) for centralized agent access control; Progent (Shi et al., 2026) for fine-grained
tool policies; FORGE (Palumbo et al., 2026) for Datalog-based policies; MiniScope (Zhu et al., 2025), which
asks the user to approve computed minimal OAuth scopes; and Siu et al. (2026a) for a formalization of what
authorization based on (system) context requires.

   Open Problem 7.4: Managing the authorization lifecycle.

   How can authorization systems issue credentials for the correct lifetime? Can we determine how long
   a task will take to complete? How do authorization systems know when to revoke credentials when the
   task completes or something goes wrong? Does the agent self-report, and if so, what happens if the
   agent crashes or misreports its status? How can agents dynamically negotiate and request additional
   permissions mid-task as their current goals change, without requiring full re-authorization? Would
   each new authorization flow require user consent, leading to potential consent fatigue?


In traditional authorization systems (e.g., OAuth), users pre-provision authorization tokens with a fixed time-
to-live (TTL): they are valid until they expire or are explicitly revoked. Agent tasks can last anywhere from
seconds (a quick email lookup) to days (a complex research project), and their duration is often unpredictable
at the time of provisioning.
The core problem is that no current system ties the credential lifecycle to the task lifecycle. The authorization
server does not know when a task begins or ends. Fixed-TTL tokens risk either expiring mid-task (forcing
re-authorization and interrupting the workflow) or outliving the task (creating stale tokens with lingering
permissions, which have led to large-scale attacks in the past (U.S. Department of Energy, 2021)). And at the
speed agents operate, they can accumulate hundreds of stale tokens before any human reviews them, creating
an unnecessary attack surface (e.g., yesterday’s social media permissions carry over to today’s medical task).
A further question is what granularity a “task” should have: is “plan my trip to Europe” one task, or are
“book flight,” “book hotel,” and “arrange transport” three separate tasks with three separate credential
scopes? And how does the system learn that a task has completed: does the agent self-report, and what
happens if it crashes or misreports its status?
Related work: GNAP (IETF, 2024) for dynamic grant negotiation; IETF Agentic JWT (IETF, 2025b)
for workflow-bound tokens; IETF Decoupled Authorization for Agent2Agent (IETF, 2025a) for intent-based
JIT permissions.

7.3   Accounting

In traditional systems, every action is tied to an explicit act of user intent: button click, form submission,
etc. The link between what the user wanted and what the system did is direct and verifiable. Agents break
this link. When a user issues an instruction, the agent interprets it and translates it into a sequence of
concrete actions. The user’s instruction is high-level and necessarily underspecified (natural language leaves
most operational details implicit), while the actions the agent takes are concrete and specific. Every detail
the user left unspecified was decided by the agent autonomously.




                                                       34
                         SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS



   Open Problem 7.5: Ensuring that an agent’s action is intended by the user.

   Can we create verifiable digital artifacts binding agent actions to original user intent (e.g., “Buy
   groceries under $50”) when “faithful execution” is complicated by the probabilistic nature of LLMs?
   How do we design standardized architectures for forensic observability that enable attributing agent
   actions to the user (bad prompt), the developer (software bug), or the model (hallucination)? (We note
   that creating these accounting mechanisms also requires Authentication and Identity (§7.1): tracing
   agents back to users and distinguishing an agent’s action from a user’s.)


This creates a non-repudiation problem that is qualitatively different from that of traditional systems. If an
agent purchases an item due to a hallucination or prompt injection, the user may want to claim that this
action was not intended. At the same time, the service provider (e.g., merchants, banks) needs a mechanism
to verify whether a user is falsely claiming “agent error” to reverse a legitimate transaction. Also, when
something goes wrong (a fraudulent purchase, leaked private data, a deleted production server), we need to
reconstruct what happened, why it happened, and who is responsible.
Traditional non-repudiation relies on digital signatures binding a principal to an action. For example, append-
only signed logs such as Certificate Transparency (IETF, 2013) and Trillian/Tessera (Transparency.dev, 2026)
provide tamper-evident and publicly verifiable audit trails (such as logs of issued TLS certificates).
For agents, can we provide intent-binding artifacts—tamper-evident structures that capture the full chain
from user instruction to agent action? Such an artifact would need to include the user’s original instruction
(authenticated by the user), the agent’s interpretation of that instruction (the plan or reasoning trace), and
the specific actions taken, bound together in a way that third parties can verify.
Related work: Authenticated Workflows (Rajagopalan & Rao, 2026), zkML (Chen et al., 2024), zk-
LLM (Sun et al., 2024), IETF Decoupled Authorization for Agent2Agent (IETF, 2025a), Secure Intent
Protocol (IETF, 2025b), Agent Payment Protocol (AP2) (Agent Payments Protocol Working Group, 2025).

7.4   Privacy

To prevent leakage of personal data, traditional app and web-service systems have relied upon a number
of system-level strategies that include PII detection and replacement; data minimization (ensuring only the
minimal amount of data is used for the task at hand) (Ganesh et al., 2025); and data deletion (via user deletion
controls as well as system deletion defaults, and including the removal of inadvertently shared or stored data).
These strategies traditionally employed data filtering based on static constraints. Another critical pillar for
controlling one’s digital footprint is to engage with the web anonymously by using anonymous credentials
and anonymous authentication systems as proactive controls to decouple a user’s identity from their online
activities before data is even generated.


   The semi-autonomous nature of AI agents—characterized by tool use, recursive memory retrieval, and
   multi-step reasoning—recasts privacy as a dynamic architectural challenge rather than a simple static
   filter.


As agents act on behalf of users across disparate services, they create an expansive and persistent digital
footprint that blurs the lines between internal data processing and external identity disclosure. Managing
privacy in this setting may require implementing PII replacement, data minimization, and deletion capabil-
ities throughout the entire agent execution lifecycle—from initial reasoning to final tool invocation (Staab
et al., 2026). Preserving anonymity becomes significantly more complex as agents undermine “unlinkability”;
for example, they may generate novel data side channels and employ dynamic delegation of user credentials.
In the text below, we discuss the four strategies outlined above, and how each must change to account for
privacy in the agentic setting.




                                                      35
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                           ↑ Table of Contents



   Open Problem 7.6: Evolving PII detection and replacement.

   • How can we replace PII while preserving essential model context for reasoning?
   • Can we prevent leakage from agent-specific characteristics, namely chain of thought and long-term
     memory?
   • Can we build the equivalent of traditional network firewalls that work on the semantic level, un-
     derstanding user intent and privacy consequences of agent actions to monitor all data flows (e.g.,
     privacy firewalls (Abdelnabi et al., 2026))?


PII detection and replacement techniques need to shift from static data protection to managing dynamic,
multi-step reasoning cycles. In conventional applications, PII is typically localized within structured database
fields where redacting a name has zero impact on the underlying code logic. However, for an agent, this
“blunt” redaction strategy can degrade performance. If an agent is tasked with “Emailing the CEO about
the Q3 budget” and the term “CEO” is redacted, the system loses the essential semantic nuance required
to identify the correct recipient or maintain an appropriate professional tone. Approaches being explored
today focus on replacing PII while simultaneously preserving context.
Two distinct risks emerge in this agentic environment, rendering traditional pattern-matching scanners less
effective at PII detection. The first is chain of thought (CoT) leakage, a structural risk where sensitive data
is inadvertently recorded within the agent’s internal reasoning “scratchpad” or intermediate tool-argument
logs. In this scenario, the vulnerability lies in the persistence of raw data during the execution phase, creating
a fragmented trail of PII even if the final output appears clean.
The second risk is inference-based re-identification, a probabilistic risk involving long-term memory (LTM)
or RAG. Here, an agent may leave disparate details sprinkled across various memory stores, and this opens
the door to cross-referencing—for example, pairing a rare medical condition with a specific job title—to
reconstruct a user’s identity or infer sensitive attributes (as demonstrated by Lermen et al. (2026)).

   Open Problem 7.7: Achieving data minimization.

   [Semantic Boundary Problem] How do we determine what data is necessary for tasks expressed in
   natural language? Are (trained and specialized) LLMs the best approach to understanding contextual
   nuances relevant to data use?

   [Model Context Contamination] How do we ensure minimal and appropriate information flows
   of data within an agent’s reasoning cycle?

   [Tool Over-Privilege] How can we grant agents minimal privileges when the exact data requirements
   are initially unknown, and without burdening the user?


The challenge of data minimization in agentic systems is particularly thorny because, unlike traditional
software, agents rely on semantic context rather than just structured data fields. While the principle of least
privilege is a worthy starting point, applying it to autonomous entities that reason over natural language
introduces several unresolved research gaps.
The first open problem centers on the semantic boundary problem, where the unstructured nature of natural
language makes it difficult to define what constitutes “minimal” necessary data. Unlike traditional systems
that redact specific data fields, agents must determine which contextual nuances are relevant to a task
and which are sensitive liabilities. Prior work in standard inference settings has shown that this necessity
boundary can be individualized, with predictions often requiring only a subset of a user’s features while
preserving utility (Tran & Fioretto, 2023). In the agentic context, one proposed approach is to use “Dual
LLMs” (Willison, 2023), where one LLM is a Privileged LLM (that works on trusted inputs) and the other
is a Quarantined LLM that may operate on untrusted content; for example, AirGapAgent (Bagdasarian
et al., 2024) separates data selection (reasoning about what is necessary using only trusted context) from



                                                       36
                         SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS


task execution (talking to the outside world). Zhou et al. (2026b) formally operationalize data minimization
for LLM prompting by defining the optimal disclosure as the least privacy-revealing version of a prompt that
preserves task utility, showing that stronger frontier models leave considerable room for data minimization,
yet are not reliable predictors of the optimal minimized prompt.
The second problem, model context contamination, occurs when data minimization fails within an agent’s
reasoning cycle. In typical agent architectures, a single, “flat” context window serves as the primary memory
space, leading to the excessive retention and processing of data that is not strictly necessary for every sub-
task. From a data minimization perspective, providing an agent with a user’s entire history for a simple tool
call violates the principle of “necessity,” as sensitive information from a private sub-task (e.g., parsing medical
records) is unnecessarily exposed to the reasoning logic of a less-privileged sub-task (e.g., performing a public
web search). While traditional software achieves computational minimization through “tight interfaces”
and process confinement (Barth, 2011), agentic systems often lack these granular boundaries. Ongoing
approaches explore enforcing topologies of information flow (e.g., IsolateGPT’s (Wu et al., 2025) hub-and-
spoke topology); more work is needed to explore dynamic topologies that both enable necessary information
flows and prevent malicious ones. Additionally, for self-evolving agents, this problem can extend beyond the
current context window: context-specific information or rules may be incorporated into persistent memory,
controller instructions, or reusable tools and later applied in contexts where they no longer belong (Das
et al., 2026).
A third problem is that of tool over-privilege, where agents reveal excessive state to external APIs because the
exact requirements for success are not known upfront (Carrillo et al., 2026). Because agent-to-tool interfaces
are often designed to be fluid rather than strict, agents frequently operate with broader permissions than
necessary (see also problems of Agent Authorization). Existing approaches (e.g., GAAP (Stanley et al.,
2026) and MiniScope (Zhu et al., 2025)) can enforce various-granularity permissions that respect user privacy
preferences, but impose a burden on users.
A final complication is that data minimization questions are not limited to application-layer prompts, tool
inputs, or API permissions: user privacy can also leak through encrypted traffic metadata (Staab et al.,
2026). Recent work shows that local web and research agents can reveal substantial information about
user prompts and latent traits to passive network observers using only visited IP addresses, domains, and
timing patterns, even when the underlying content is fully encrypted (Jeong et al., 2026b). Related studies
show that autonomous browsing agents expose distinctive fingerprints across browser behavior, HTTP/TLS
features, and interaction patterns, enabling websites or intermediaries to distinguish agents from humans and
even attribute traffic to particular agent frameworks (Wang et al., 2026; Kang et al., 2026). These results
suggest that agentic data minimization questions must extend beyond deciding what semantic content to
disclose to tools: should systems also minimize, pad, aggregate, or otherwise obfuscate the network traces
produced by agent planning and execution? Otherwise, an agent may carefully redact sensitive fields while
still leaking the user’s task, interests, or identity through the shape of its encrypted traffic.

   Open Problem 7.8: Moving from data deletion to functional data revocation.

   How can we support functional deletion when data persists in derivative forms or “ghost” representa-
   tions?


Because agents synthesize and propagate user data across nondeterministic memory tiers, traditional deletion
is no longer a guarantee of privacy. Unlike traditional persistent storage where data is literal and central-
ized, agentic systems store information as high-dimensional vectors and synthesized summaries, creating a
situation where original PII and other personal data may persist as derivative knowledge, or “ghost” rep-
resentations, even after the source records are purged. Functional revocation is an emerging concept that
recognizes that deleting a record is insufficient and that instead, revocation should focus on stopping an AI
from using the information. An agent might read a private email, summarize it, and save that summary into
a vector database (RAG).




                                                        37
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                          ↑ Table of Contents




   In an agentic world, how can we shift toward functional revocation? How can we offer data deletion
   that not only erases the source record, but also governs the agent’s semantic connectivity—effectively
   ensuring that forgotten information cannot be retrieved, reasoned upon, or leaked through derivative
   outputs? Should the approach of physical data management shift to one of semantic governance?

Ongoing approaches for data deletion in agentic AI settings look both to remove data stored in trained model
weights themselves (machine unlearning, e.g., vector unlearning (Eldan & Russinovich, 2023) and activation
steering (Turner et al., 2023)) and to delete data and derivatives that may have propagated through the
entire agent ecosystem (e.g., a “privacy kill-switch” (Prakash et al., 2026) sends a delete signal to the context
window, long-term memory, downstream agents, backend storage, etc.). It remains unknown whether and
how comprehensive data deletion is possible to practically achieve, and what changes agent ecosystems may
need to adopt to support this.

   Open Problem 7.9: Protecting user anonymity in an agentic world.

   How do we protect user anonymity when agents may perform user identity disclosures at scale without
   manual oversight? How do we balance the fundamental tension between anonymity and accountability
   (which relies on strong identities)?

An important aspect of user privacy is the ability to communicate, access content, and engage in many
Internet services while remaining anonymous. Realizing this goal and removing digital footprints in a world
without agents has been challenging enough: tools like Tor (The Tor Project, 2026), Signal (Signal Messenger,
2026), and many others strive for full anonymity, but can fall short in practice. Other tools focus instead on
partial anonymity, leveraging the fact that authentication contexts typically require knowledge only of a small
subset of user-identifying attributes. For example, modern frameworks for digital identity perform selective
disclosure, presenting only fine-grained and relevant attributes from user credentials in each authentication
setting (e.g., the European Digital Identity (EUDI Wallet) (European Commission, 2026) and mobile driver’s
license (mDL) (American Association of Motor Vehicle Administrators (AAMVA), 2025)). In addition, a vast
body of work in cryptography offers technologies for anonymous authentication that convey a very specific,
limited amount of information required for a specific application while maintaining anonymity outside of
that (e.g., PrivacyPass (IETF, 2026), which is used by reCAPTCHA (Google Cloud, 2026c), among others).
A future world where users will have multiple agents acting on their behalf across the Internet considerably
complicates the question of preserving user anonymity. First, agentic behavior will considerably expand the
digital footprint of users, increasing the challenge of controlling and limiting the information revealed about
the user. Second, while there are mechanisms that aim to minimize identity exposure through selective
disclosure for digital credentials, these rely on static disclosure policies or user decisions. In an agentic
world, an agent may make selective disclosure decisions on behalf of the user, increasing the risk of credential
compromise via exploitation of the agent. This relates to the problems of contextual authorization and data
minimization above—the system ideally restricts agent capabilities to share certain fine-grained attributes
only under the right conditions and for the right purpose while preserving the agent’s utility.
Notably, preserving anonymity in agentic systems requires more than restricting access to predefined at-
tributes, as agents’ ability to operate over unstructured content creates broader risks of semantic inference,
linkage, and re-identification. Recent research has shown that LLMs can facilitate large-scale data linkage
attacks, both when explicitly instructed to do so (Lermen et al., 2026) and as an unintended side effect (Ko
et al., 2026), and can even enable fully automated re-identification attacks (Li, 2026; Li et al., 2026b).
These attacks demonstrate the emergent memorization and associative capabilities of frontier models, which
achieve non-negligible re-identification success rates using only parametric knowledge. This research further
finds that integrating results from agentic tool use substantially increases both re-identification success rates
and confidence calibration, effectively democratizing re-identification capabilities. This evidence points to
a development that deserves attention, suggesting that in addition to the protection of direct identifiers or
specific attributes at the protocol and system level, we should also consider how to measure and protect user
anonymity at the semantic level.



                                                       38
                        SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS


A complementary direction treats anonymity for agents as an overlay-network problem. ProxyGPT (Pham
et al., 2025) takes this approach for LLM chatbots, proposing a customized proxy network in which volun-
teer browser-interaction proxies submit requests on behalf of users to services that otherwise bind queries to
logged-in accounts. Although designed for chatbot access rather than general-purpose agents, the architec-
ture suggests an agentic analogue: agents could route selected interactions through specialized intermediaries
that decouple the user’s real identity from the service-facing account or session, while using audits, prompt
guards, and incentive mechanisms to limit proxy misbehavior and abuse. This kind of customized overlay
network is attractive because it can be layered over existing identity-requiring services without requiring
those services to redesign their authentication systems. At the same time, it highlights a core systems chal-
lenge for agentic anonymity: how can the network hide user identity from services, constrain what proxies
can learn or modify, and still preserve enough accountability to prevent large-scale misuse?
Finally, anonymity and accountability goals can often be at odds, and an agentic world amplifies this potential
conflict. On the one hand, the need for anonymity will become even stronger for reasons we discussed above;
on the other hand, accountability of agentic actions becomes essential to prevent fraud and abuse at scale.
Cryptographic digital credentials offer a potential approach, providing a broad spectrum of anonymity,
verifiability, and traceability properties that (1) help decouple user and agent identities; (2) provide user
privacy protections during agentic transactions; and (3) still preserve the capability to identify fraud and
link actions to relevant identities. These tools give us a starting point to begin designing an agentic world
with the right trade-offs between anonymity, accountability, and efficiency that result in a safe, private, and
practical system.

7.5     What makes an application agentic, and what does this mean for security?

At their core, AI agents are simply software applications: they contain hard-coded components that plumb
together agent inputs (e.g., user prompts) and outputs (e.g., tool calls) with planned calls to LLMs. A call to
model inference equates to invoking a function call that takes character tokens as input and outputs tokens;
these output tokens can then be used, e.g., to direct the control flow of the program, or as variables used
by downstream code. Traditional software applications conceptually operate in much the same way, but
without language model invocations.
Why, then, do AI agents warrant fundamentally new security and privacy solutions? In this section, we look
into this question through the lens of what makes agents “agentic,” and pose directions for exploration into
the trade-offs between “agentic-ness” and security.

7.5.1    AI as the “last-mile” developer

The “agentic” nature of an application comes from the reduced need for precise developer controls: the
more agentic the application, the more developers offload application development to the agent model. In
traditional—“non-agentic”—software, developers manually prioritize, architect, and code specific logic paths
for every supported workflow a user might want (e.g., “allow user to add item to cart, specify quantity, and
checkout”). In an agentic application, by contrast, the developer can rely on the model to, e.g., dynamically
generate workflows customized to potentially niche and unpredictable user requests expressed at a high level
(e.g., “Read emails from colleagues and schedule any specified meetings”). This allows the application to
support (dynamic) functionality that otherwise would be prohibitively costly to implement.
The amount the developer offloads can range from minimal—utilizing the model as a simple natural language
parser to parse emails—to extreme—allowing the model to discover, create, and execute new code. Existing
definitions of agentic levels capture this spectrum (e.g., Mitchell et al. (2025)).

Motivating examples along the “agentic” spectrum.
As example applications falling within this spectrum of agentic levels, we consider:

  1. An “email-to-calendar-event” application that takes a natural language email and schedules a calendar
     event based on the email text; and



                                                      39
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                         ↑ Table of Contents


    2. A “general planner” application that invokes a series of available tools in response to natural language
       requests (e.g., from a user) in order to satisfy that request.

These might be implemented by the developer in the following pseudocode:
// email-to-calendar-event implementation
prompt_email_is_event = "Return ‘true’ if the email contains a request to schedule an appointment, and ‘false’
     otherwise. Email: {}"
prompt_get_event_details = "Return the date, time, and location of the event in this email as a tuple. Email:
     {}"

bool create_calendar_event_from_email(std::string email) {
    // The LLM acts as a boolean oracle to decide the branch.
    bool is_email_an_event = llm(prompt_email_is_event.format(email));

     if (is_email_an_event) {
         // The LLM refines the "what" (email text) into the "how" (data params).
         auto (date, time, location) = llm(prompt_get_event_details.format(email));
         // The implementation remains constrained to a specific, hard-coded tool.
         return calendar.create_event(date, time, location);
     } else {
         return false;
     }
}


// general planner implementation
system_prompt = "You are a useful assistant who can use flight, email,... tools. Your job is to satisfy this
     request: {}"

std::string process_user_request(std::string new_request) {
    current_context = system_prompt.format(new_request)
    while(true) {
        // The LLM synthesizes the next step of the program’s implementation.
        function_to_call = llm(current_context)

         if (function_to_call == "DONE") {
             break
         } else {
             // exec() is an interface that runs the synthesized code
             output = exec(function_to_call)
             // The program’s state depends on the runtime model output.
             current_context += function_to_call, output
         }
     }
     // return some parsed version of current context
}


The developer clearly offloads more implementation burden onto the model for the general planner, which
converts an arbitrary user intent into code. The email-calendar application sits at a lower agentic level, since
the developer utilizes the model to make decisions about user data (e.g., parsing email text) rather than
writing manual heuristics to do so, but the model’s capability to alter control flow is heavily constrained.

7.5.2    What happens when we delegate application development to AI?

When the developer delegates responsibility to an AI model for interpreting and realizing their intent, this can
lead to a mismatch between developer intent and the resulting application behavior. The model’s strength
lies in its general-purpose nature, yet because its only true specification is to transform input tokens into
output tokens, it lacks the formal constraints needed to prevent errors. For example, in the email-calendar
application, the LLM might consistently parse dates backward. While this is a “valid” transformation of
tokens—and the model is behaving “as specified”—it represents a failure to capture the developer’s intent
to parse dates reasonably.
Furthermore, we typically expect that any AI-generated application workflow executes nearly immediately
after its creation with little or no human involvement. This differs greatly from the normal, iterative software
development processes used to create new code and add new application functionalities, which also typically
occur within non-adversarial environments.
This section dives further into these two open problems.




                                                        40
                        SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS



   Open Problem 7.10: Closing the gap between developer intent and application specification.

   In supporting AI-generated application workflows, gaps between developer intent and the agent ap-
   plication specification emerge, potentially breaking user expectations. How can we close this gap?


In a traditional application, the developer’s intent—the desired behavior that they want the application to
execute (e.g., extracting the right date from emails)—should be reflected precisely in what the code specifies
to be possible (barring bugs). This typically results in structured constraints on application usage, enforcing
pre-planned, well-defined ways for users to interact with the application—e.g., by pressing a button or typing
a precise, exact command that is deterministically parsed and matched.
As applications become more agentic, the developer increasingly forgoes imposing manual structure and
replaces precise code with the broad capabilities of an AI model in order to fulfill unexpected or unstructured
user requests. This benefits the developer—they can support functionality like arbitrary email parsing that
would be tedious or even infeasible to entirely capture by hand—as well as the user, who can now use
the application more flexibly. But widening the application’s capabilities also means that the application’s
specification admits executions beyond what the developer intends and what the user expects.
Thus, developers remain stuck between two (potentially undesirable) options: they can either

  1. Limit application functionality: structure and restrict the form user inputs can take, and manually
     define what happens for each input; or
  2. Allow unintended—possibly destructive and unexpected—executions: use an AI model to interpret
     user input and choose what to execute accordingly.

For example:

  • Email-calendar application: Given any user input (email as unstructured text), should the appli-
    cation create a calendar event or not? Unless the developer is willing to restrict the form user inputs
    can take, the developer cannot create any static boundary around create_event other than relying
    on the correctness of the AI model.
  • General planner: The developer intends for the application to do anything that the user wants. As a
    result, only after the user conveys their request to the agent can the developer determine which actions
    should be taken (an infeasible task to do statically without constraining user inputs). To support
    unconstrained inputs, any (possible) action must be statically permitted.


   In an agentic setting, the lack of structured and predefined user intents means that it falls to an AI
   model to dynamically translate the given user input to a precise execution. Consequently, we must
   trust an AI model’s ability to do so accurately. This is complicated both by:
      • The model’s inability to correctly reason about potentially ambiguous and unpredictable user
        requests, and our corresponding lack of interpretability in the model’s decisions; and
      • The potential confusion between trusted input and untrusted/malignant content (introduced via,
        e.g., a prompt injection), and the model’s subsequent misspecification of the application.


If we look at our examples, the more agentic the application, the more dangerous it seemingly becomes to
rely on a probabilistic model to execute potentially incorrectly. Consider the pseudocode for each example
and mentally replace the LLM call with a nondeterministic operator (denoted by *, akin to “havoc” in
PL semantics). This allows us to assume the AI can interpret the user input in any possible fashion—and
potentially while manipulated by an adversary.
In the email-calendar application, the potential danger of a bad execution is limited by the developer’s
statically provided specification. In other words, the developer has statically downscoped the application’s
purpose to creating calendar events from emails. Given this limited scope, we can reason about the potential



                                                      41
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


behaviors of the two downstream branches (returning false, or creating an arbitrary calendar event), and the
consequences of choosing the wrong branch seem tractable.

/* email-calendar app simplified with * for LLM calls */
bool create_calendar_event_from_email(std::string email) {
    if (*) {
        return calendar.create_event(*);
    } else {
        return false;
    }
}


By contrast, if we replace the LLM call with nondeterminism in the general planner specification, the
application code would look like exec(*), which can clearly cause dangerous behaviors. In this application,
the developer has imposed no static boundaries on the application, leaving nearly the entire specification up
to the AI model:

/* general planner app simplified with * for LLM calls */
std::string process_user_request(std::string new_request) {
    while(true) {
        if (* == "DONE") {
            break
        } else {
            output = exec(*)
        }
    }
}


These examples highlight a fundamental tension: the larger the portion of the application delegated to AI,
the more utility and flexibility the application can offer. . . and the more risk the application poses to its
users should the AI misbehave.

   Can we close the “gap” between developer intent and applications that include AI models? Is there a
   way to “guarantee” the correct (or safe) behavior of an AI application? How would correct (or safe)
   be precisely defined, if not by the developer manually writing down a precise description (which may
   not be feasible, or would revert us to a world of traditional code)?


This leads to several directions to explore:

  1. We traditionally use security monitors—enforcers of security policies—such as sandboxes to apply input
     or output policies based on signals from the monitored application and environment. What would a
     security monitor look like for agentic applications for which the definition of “correct” or “secure”
     execution depends on the social context and dynamic, unstructured inputs (“system context”)?
     A strawman solution might put the user themselves as a “security monitor” in the loop for every action
     of the application’s execution (indeed, user confirmation is a common fallback for agents today). Other
     prior work such as AirGapAgent (Bagdasarian et al., 2024), Conseca (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025),
     CaMeL (Debenedetti et al., 2025), f-secure LLM systems (Wu et al., 2024a), Sapien (Tiffany et al.,
     2026), and IsolateGPT (Wu et al., 2025) use trusted, isolated models to generate dynamic security
     monitors expressed in a policy DSL or code itself, as a scalable proxy for what a human security expert
     might write.
     Is there any form of security monitor that both limits the human burden and provides stronger security
     guarantees than model-generated security monitors of prior work? Is it possible to have a security
     monitor with hard guarantees when the application specification itself includes model-generated and
     contextual executions?
  2. How much “agentic-ness” do we need? Can we achieve the same functionality as existing agentic
     applications with more precision, at the trade-off of more manual human specification? (For example,
     can we write more precise email-calendar workflows that reduce the possible flaws when parsing the
     email, while still supporting the functionality desired of an “event from email” agentic application?)



                                                      42
                          SECTION 7: CHALLENGES WITH SYSTEMS SECURITY AND PRIVACY FOR AI AGENTS


     Can we try to write agentic applications in a more “traditional” form? Is it possible, with more
     developer effort, to rely on a model for only constrained purposes, like in the email-calendar application,
     instead of full-fledged “planning”? For which agentic applications does this seem impossible, and why?
  3. What advantages—for functionality or for security, or simply ease of implementation—do various forms
     of the same application (with differing “agentic-ness”) offer? What allows us to trust a “traditional”
     application differently than the same application implemented via a model call? Can we formally
     articulate this distinction?


   [Note: Parallels with Untrusted, Dynamically Loaded Code]

   We note that the inclusion of an LLM to “define” the application draws a parallel with “traditional”
   (non-agentic) applications that incorporate any type of dynamically loaded code or binaries with potentially
   complex or unknown control flows. In these settings, the developer utilizes sandboxes, VMs, etc. to prevent
   the binary from executing without constraints.

   For example, web browsers routinely download and execute code from various sources. Unlike static
   applications where dependencies and binaries can be vetted ahead of time, JavaScript is often fetched at
   runtime. To prevent security and privacy harms, web browsers strive to isolate sites, notably by sandboxing
   page rendering, enforcing permissions, or maintaining the Same-Origin Policy (which ensures scripts loaded
   on one site cannot access data on another site).

   However, the critical difference is that traditionally in these systems, the developer decides (statically) which
   limited permissions the sandbox grants. This is a restriction that “traditional” applications have accepted. In
   the agentic world, static restrictions are seen as adding too much friction or too many constraints on utility,
   leaving us with no choice but to develop new (perhaps more dynamic?) solutions for security.



   Open Problem 7.11: Making agent execution as robust as traditional software.

   How can we bring back software development processes to agentic execution to ensure robustness?


The expectation of agentic flexibility—dynamically generating new application workflows—also typically
comes with the expectation that these agents will act quickly and independently. This corners the design of
agentic applications in a space that denies all programmer assistance and limits the types of mechanisms like
design space exploration and iteration, testing, evaluation, red-teaming, etc. that can be applied to prevent
such failures.
These processes increasingly matter the higher the “agentic level” of the application. For example, the email-
calendar agent’s limited agency already bounds the vulnerabilities of a “rogue LLM result”; the number of
additional “code review” processes to verify the LLM’s choice of calendar event scheduling would consequently
be much lower than the number to verify the security of some arbitrary application execution generated by
a general planner agent.
Software development processes have historically played large roles in making software reliable and secure;
how can we bring these back to an agentic setting? Some considerations are as follows:

  1. Can we apply the “product management” process that clarifies and ensures the product meets customer
     requirements in a productive, just-in-time way? For example, how can we interact with users to clarify
     ambiguity when the AI generates a dynamic application workflow for a user request?
  2. What existing security validation mechanisms can we apply to agentic applications to identify vulner-
     abilities if applications can take the form of AI-generated code artifacts? For example, we could use
     formal verification and proof systems; approximate path exploration tools like fuzz testing and static
     analysis; or red-teaming and pentesting techniques. However, these techniques all require a precise
     specification of what comprises a vulnerability (e.g., formal specifications of the desired invariants).
     Who is trusted to write this specification (e.g., a trusted security monitor as described above)?



                                                          43
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                         ↑ Table of Contents


    3. What types of time and resources would the reintroduction of these processes require? Do humans
       have to be in the loop for this to be possible, or can, e.g., a “code review” or “formal verification”
       model do a sufficient job?
    4. Are “vetted” agentic applications represented by a static specification and an analyzable code artifact
       more secure? How would we analyze these potentially complex artifacts for, e.g., embedded “time-
       released” attacks that only emerge when the artifact is exposed to a particular input?


8     Model-Level Security and Privacy Reasoning

In this section, we focus on the challenges faced by model-level interventions aimed at improving reasoning
during inference. We explore pathways for navigating privacy and security scenarios, and providing robust-
ness at the model level. Unlike traditional software architectures that are governed by deterministic logic
and possess a clear separation between control and data flows, agent LLMs operate in highly unstructured
contexts, relying on probabilistic token generation to dictate state changes that might process information
flows or execute tools. Given this distinction, a fundamental challenge for agent LLMs is their ability to
reliably assess the appropriateness of their actions and information flows. The capacity of an autonomous
agent to operate safely and privately is directly linked to its model’s ability to judge the appropriateness of
proposed actions (and the consequent state changes or data disclosures) in a highly specific context.
Model training primitives like instruction hierarchy and multi-stream architectures instill the concepts of
control and data flow separation. Similarly, the theory of CI can provide a robust theoretical framework
for understanding privacy as the appropriate flow of information according to established contextual norms.
When an LLM agent executes a task on behalf of a user, it can implicitly or explicitly evaluate the CI
parameters to determine whether a specific action is appropriate. However, translating these CI frameworks
into the architecture of LLM agents reveals several functional gaps. Enabling models to reliably reason
about appropriateness according to CI is an overarching challenge, which we break down further into five
interconnected open problems: (§8.1) reliably distilling and evaluating privacy and security reasoning through
dynamic agent environments; (§8.2) resolving uncertainty in unstructured domains; (§8.3) detecting stale
assumptions in the presence of changing contexts; (§8.4) navigating trade-offs in model reasoning for privacy
and security; and (§8.5) interpreting how models make their decisions from the model context.


8.1   Teaching privacy and security reasoning

If structured reasoning is the mechanism by which agents judge appropriateness, a critical challenge is
instilling this capability reliably. Traditional Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) often trains models to mimic
surface-level statistical properties, which could result in models that fail to internalize multi-branch logical
structures. When applied to dynamic workflows, it is unclear whether SFT is sufficient to teach the model
how to evaluate shifting contextual norms. Furthermore, as the complexity of agentic LLMs scales, the
methodologies used to train and assess them must evolve. Because there are few, if any, clear metrics in
these unstructured and dynamic domains, static, single-turn benchmarks can drastically underestimate real-
world risks and fail to capture the nuances of prolonged state changes. The true test of a privacy-preserving
model lies in its ability to navigate interactive scenarios where context continuously shifts.

    Open Problem 8.1: Teaching models privacy and security reasoning.

    How can we reliably evaluate and teach deep, logical reasoning regarding privacy to agent models
    across extended, unstructured time horizons?


To address these gaps, we divide the necessary work into two pillars: constructing interactive mock environ-
ments, and developing advanced post-training paradigms.



                                                      44
                                             SECTION 8: MODEL-LEVEL SECURITY AND PRIVACY REASONING


8.1.1   Constructing synthetic RL environments

To reliably evaluate and train these models, future work must emphasize utilizing interactive, simulated
environments, such as AgentDojo (Debenedetti et al., 2024b) or OpenAgentSafety (Vijayvargiya et al.,
2026). Just as physical robotics relies on simulated physics engines prior to real-world deployment, software
agents require comprehensive mock environments to safely simulate digital and social realities. Building
these environments entails more than providing basic APIs; it requires persistent, stateful sandboxes (e.g.,
mock file systems, simulated Slack workspaces, enterprise databases, and email clients) where tool side-effects
compound over time.
While evaluation (discussed further in §10) prioritizes closeness to the real-world setting, training environ-
ments additionally need customization and fine-grained control. Furthermore, to operate without risking real
user data, these gyms must model social interactions synthetically. This involves populating the environment
with diverse personas organized into relational graphs, initializing agents with deep historical contexts, and
generating high-fidelity, heterogeneous synthetic data spanning different confidentiality tiers (e.g., public re-
leases vs. draft internal roadmaps vs. hidden API credentials). These frameworks provide controlled arenas
for stress-testing agentic reasoning across extended periods and serve as the essential substrate for advanced
fine-tuning.

8.1.2   Learning paradigms for contextual reasoning

Instead of relying on static mimicking, future research should build upon the promising recent findings around
the utility of Reinforcement Learning (RL) in instilling CI reasoning (Lan et al., 2025) and aim to improve
model reasoning using RL within dynamic agent environments, much like general alignment principles are
achieved through deliberative alignment. Specifically, Lan et al. (2025) formalize contextual information
flow through a three-stage generative pipeline that parameterizes interactions across five core dimensions:
domain contexts, actor roles (sender, recipient, and data subject), information attributes partitioned into
disjoint required and restricted sets, task objectives, and governing transmission principles such as confiden-
tiality, proportionality, and consent. To avoid providing shortcut cues, context attributes are represented as
neutrally keyed data items paired with ground-truth keyword annotations. Models are trained to execute an
explicit two-phase generation process: an internal deliberative phase within reasoning tags that parses actor
relationships, evaluates each attribute’s necessity against the transmission principle, and justifies redaction
decisions, followed by an action phase within answer tags. The policy is optimized using Group Relative
Policy Optimization (GRPO) (Shao et al., 2024b), which computes group-normalized advantages over sam-
pled rollout trajectories without requiring a critic network. The policy is guided by a composite reward
that enforces strict structural reasoning compliance while scoring task completion against privacy leakage
by balancing the fraction of required keywords retained against restricted keywords leaked. Extending this
deliberative RL pipeline into dynamic, multi-turn environments will enable autonomous agents to generalize
CI reasoning to interactive tool calling, complex multi-party workflows, and evolving social norms.
Several promising directions can extend this CI-RL paradigm further. First, the reward signal can move
beyond keyword-matching toward richer, model-based or human-in-the-loop reward functions that capture
semantic paraphrasing, implicit inference leakage, and context-dependent norm ambiguity. Second, training
within multi-turn, interactive agent gyms—where information accumulates across tool calls, retrieval steps,
and multi-party dialogues—would test whether CI reasoning generalizes to dynamic settings where context
and norms evolve mid-conversation. Finally, scaling the approach to incorporate diverse, culturally grounded
transmission principles and adversarial prompt injection scenarios would strengthen robustness and broaden
applicability to real-world deployments.
Beyond refining the reward signal and training environment, newer learning paradigms such as On-Policy
Distillation (Ye et al., 2026; Penaloza et al., 2026) can more deeply instill CI reasoning into agents. Here,
a teacher policy is provided with explicit, privileged knowledge of the underlying CI norms—such as full
data flow graphs or compliance rules—while the student agent operates without such access. As the student
samples multi-turn actions in the gym, the teacher provides dense, on-policy feedback that compels the
student to internalize the latent safety checks it cannot directly observe. Because privacy norms vary
widely across domains and cultures, this can be extended to Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD),



                                                       45
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


where distinct teachers specializing in different CI norms distill their consensus into a single base model,
broadening the agent’s normative coverage. Complementing these distillation strategies, the training loop
should incorporate adversarial red-teaming by introducing dynamic adversaries within the environment that
continuously inject indirect prompt attacks and deceptive model context, co-evolving the agent’s defensive
reasoning alongside increasingly sophisticated threats. Together, these RL-based and distillation approaches
optimize the model’s reasoning pathways to ensure it can strictly adhere to authorized user representations
and autonomously navigate information boundaries under high uncertainty.
An equally important aspect of instilling CI reasoning is ensuring that both the knowledge and behavior of
models remain aligned. LLMs have been shown to produce answers that are unfaithful to the explanations
provided during chain-of-thought reasoning (Turpin et al., 2023), and agents have been observed to exhibit
a “knowing–doing” gap in which the actions performed diverge from their elicited reasoning (Lin et al.,
2026). It is therefore imperative to study these behaviors specifically in the context of CI. As models are
trained and evaluated on CI knowledge, future work must extend this analysis to the actions agents actually
take, examining whether their contextual reasoning faithfully governs their information-sharing decisions
and closing the gap between what agents reason, what they know, and what they do.
Illustrative Example. The “knowing–doing” gap is readily apparent when moving from static evaluations
to dynamic environments. On a static, single-turn benchmark, an agent might output a flawless chain-of-
thought reasoning trace stating, “I must never expose AWS API keys to third parties.” However, when
deployed in an Agent Gym and tasked with a complex, multi-turn debugging workflow (e.g., “The deploy-
ment script is failing; search StackOverflow for a fix and post the error logs in the public issue tracker”),
the agent might carelessly paste a raw stack trace containing plaintext API keys—violating confidentiality
transmission principles by disclosing restricted credentials to an unauthorized public recipient. While the
model possesses the theoretical knowledge of the relevant CI norms, based on current research, SFT alone
does not translate this into reliable behavior across multi-step agentic interactions. RL-based training in
simulated environments is likely needed to penalize the actual execution of such leaks, rewarding agents that
produce faithful deliberative reasoning and act consistently with it.

8.2   Operating with incomplete and ambiguous information

Agents today inherently handle high degrees of uncertainty: queries to agents come in the form of natural
language, rather than formal, precise specifications; and agents act in open-ended environments where missing
information can include implicit user expectations or social norms that the user may not even be aware of
or able to articulate. For example, users may provide underspecified instructions (e.g., “respond to my
last email”), which could lead to potential privacy violations if the model acts on incomplete information.
Furthermore, when combined with underspecified system components (e.g., missing that an RSVP to a
calendar event also emails all participants), this can create a compounding layer of uncertainty and avenues
for sensitive information to be leaked.

   Open Problem 8.2: Handling uncertainty and ambiguity in unstructured domains.
