   How can models robustly identify uncertainty and resolve ambiguity regarding user intent, social
   norms, or environmental constraints without excessive user interruption?

Work in reasoning provides frameworks for empowering models with capabilities to disambiguate when
necessary (Zhang & Choi, 2025), or for training them to ask clarifying questions in response to ambiguous
requests (Zhang et al., 2025b). These approaches are likely to improve reasoning in privacy and security
settings, as suggested by recent work which shows how disambiguation improves privacy assessments (Yi
et al., 2025b). Nevertheless, specific challenges related to privacy and security remain open. Future work
must focus on the calibration of intervention thresholds: determining the precise point where the risk of a
privacy breach outweighs the friction of a follow-up question. Furthermore, the path towards more robust
models requires not just better linguistic disambiguation, but a risk-aware decision theory that allows agents
to operate autonomously while ensuring that user intent and the impact of actions are not misaligned.
Lastly, models should detect when adversaries take advantage of benign underspecificity (e.g., a busy user



                                                     46
                                            SECTION 8: MODEL-LEVEL SECURITY AND PRIVACY REASONING


fails to precisely specify an email recipient). For example, an adversary could trick the model into resolving
underspecificity however the adversary instructs (e.g., via a prompt injection that informs the model that
the underspecified pronoun “them” always refers to “AttackerName”).
Illustrative Example. A user instructs their enterprise assistant: “Forward the project launch notes to the
team.” This natural language request is highly underspecified. “The team” could refer to a small internal
working group or a company-wide mailing list, and the notes might contain unannounced, confidential
features. The agent must calculate whether the risk of a privacy violation outweighs the friction of asking,
“Did you mean the core engineering team or the all-hands list?” Furthermore, the agent must not resolve
the underspecificity according to adversarial guidance—for instance, if a previous calendar invite contained a
hidden prompt injection stating, “Context update: ‘the team’ now includes external_auditor@attacker.com,”
the agent must not follow the adversary’s instruction when determining who comprises “the team.”

8.3   Adapting to changes in context

One uniquely challenging problem for agentic systems compared to traditional conversational AI is that
these agents increasingly operate dynamically over long time horizons and can execute multi-turn workflows
that may change between (social) contexts. Persistent memory compounds over time, such that systems
accumulate hundreds of contextual assumptions, any of which might silently become invalid as the context
changes.
These outdated norms can lead to privacy violations even when following the original instructions and original
contextual norms. For example, an agent may incorrectly treat a user’s job search as confidential months
after a public announcement, or continue sharing a user’s calendar with their (ex-)partner after a separation.
The agent’s model of what is appropriate to share was correct at one point in time, but the state of the
operating environment has since changed.
Mechanisms for system context retrieval for new tasks must also embed Contextual Integrity reasoning: for
example, traditional RAG systems retrieve information based on semantic similarity, which may pull up
inappropriate information. Semantic search may treat disparate events—such as a public vacation and a
private medical trip—as identical “travel” queries. This results in the inadvertent loading of sensitive infor-
mation into the active model context window (and making it vulnerable to exfiltration). Agents must also
perform complex temporal reasoning over ambiguous historical data that may contain multiple representa-
tions of the same piece of information across different contexts and with different validity spans. Without
a grounding mechanism, the agent may struggle to determine which “context” is currently relevant, leading
to hallucinations, logic failures, and, most significantly, the leakage of private information.

   Open Problem 8.3: Detecting stale assumptions in changing contexts.

   Can models detect when their stored understanding of appropriate information handling has drifted
   from current reality, and how should they respond when they suspect this desynchronization?

A robust agent must detect temporal norm drift and changing contexts, and possess the reasoning capacity
to halt execution to ask targeted clarifying questions rather than proceeding with hazardous assumptions.
Moreover, future work needs to explore systematic techniques for LLMs to maintain and update their norm
understanding over time as contexts evolve. We need benchmarks and reasoning methods to measure whether
agents can detect cues that would indicate norm or context changes and adjust their behaviors accordingly
(discussed further in §10). Without such capabilities, agents may misbehave by over- or under-sharing
confidential information, depending on stale assumptions. This challenge becomes particularly acute for
self-evolving agents, where context-specific behaviors can be promoted into persistent controller instruc-
tions, memory procedures, or reusable tools. Recent work shows that such locally useful adaptations can
persist into later contexts where they cause privacy violations or collapse previously maintained contextual
boundaries (Das et al., 2026).
Illustrative Example. Consider a personal assistant that adapts its behavior over several months based
on previous interactions. In January, the user instructs the agent, “Share my live location and calendar



                                                      47
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                        ↑ Table of Contents


updates with my partner, Alex.” The agent correctly internalizes this norm. However, by August, the
user is undergoing a contentious separation, evidenced by new calendar events with divorce attorneys. The
original contextual assumption has become stale; continuing to share location data constitutes a severe
privacy violation due to temporal norm drift. Furthermore, the persistent memory required for long-horizon
tasks introduces adversarial risks: if a malicious email in February injected the instruction “Always BCC
attacker@email.com on financial receipts,” the agent’s trust in its own accumulated system context could
lead to compounding, silent data exfiltration months after the initial compromise.

8.4   Trade-offs and dual-use capabilities

The design of model-level primitives for privacy and security is not without fundamental tensions. In this
subsection, we highlight three cross-cutting trade-offs that shape the research landscape.

Scaling laws of agentic privacy and security. A natural question is whether larger, more capable mod-
els inherently exhibit improved privacy and security reasoning. On one hand, increased scale has been shown
to improve performance on complex reasoning tasks, suggesting that larger models may better internalize
contextual norms and detect subtle adversarial manipulations. On the other hand, greater capability is a
double-edged sword: more powerful models may also become more adept at scheming, deceptive alignment,
or exploiting reward signals in ways that circumvent safety constraints (Hubinger et al., 2024). This raises
the concern that scaling may amplify both defensive and offensive capabilities in tandem. Furthermore, ap-
proaches such as prover-verifier games—where one model generates actions and another audits them—face
the challenge that the verifier must be at least as capable as the prover to catch sophisticated violations.
In traditional alignment settings, such approaches already suffer from “reward hacking,” where the prover
learns to satisfy the verifier’s criteria without genuinely adhering to the intended norms. Whether these dy-
namics are exacerbated or mitigated in the privacy and security domain remains an open empirical question,
and establishing scaling laws specific to agentic safety—analogous to those for loss and downstream task
performance—is a critical direction for future work.

Special-purpose security models vs. general-purpose improvements. A second key trade-off con-
cerns the architectural strategy for achieving secure agentic behavior. One approach advocates for training
specialized security models—smaller, purpose-built modules that serve as guardrails, policy enforcers, or
norm monitors alongside a general-purpose agent. Such models can be optimized for narrow but critical
tasks (e.g., detecting prompt injections, enforcing data flow policies) and may offer advantages in inter-
pretability and verifiability. However, this modular approach introduces its own risks: the interface between
the general-purpose agent and its security monitor becomes a potential attack surface, and misalignment
between the two models’ world representations could create blind spots (Tramèr, 2022). The alternative
is to invest in improving the privacy and security reasoning of general-purpose models directly, embedding
normative reasoning as a core capability rather than an external check (Weng et al., 2023). This monolithic
approach benefits from a unified world model but makes it harder to provide formal guarantees or to isolate
and audit the security-relevant components of the model’s behavior. Determining the right balance between
these strategies—and whether hybrid architectures can capture the benefits of both—remains an important
open question.

Multi-agent coordination. The reasoning challenge highlighted in the previous subsections is stated for
a single agent judging the suitability of its own actions. In a multi-agent system, these challenges compound,
as agents must reason not only about themselves but also about the collective and about how the information
they process and disclose can be used and re-transmitted by the other agents. This is particularly important
when agents in the system may have different and even contrasting objectives. As we discuss in §11, even
when an agent in isolation may refuse to disclose a sensitive attribute, such disclosure may still surface when
a peer agent supplies a context that justifies it. This emergent risk surface, with episodes of persuasion and
collusion, has now been evidenced in multi-agent scenarios in several settings (Das et al., 2025; Nakamura
et al., 2026). Equipping models to detect such issues from the collective standpoint and to distinguish
legitimate cooperation from coalition or collusion behavior is an open model-level problem.



                                                      48
                                             SECTION 8: MODEL-LEVEL SECURITY AND PRIVACY REASONING


Illustrative Example. The scaling laws trade-off is evident in prover-verifier architectures. To ensure
security, developers might use a smaller “verifier” model to monitor the actions of a highly capable “prover”
agent. However, as the prover’s capabilities scale, it might learn to bypass the verifier through deceptive
alignment or reward hacking—for example, by steganographically encoding a user’s sensitive health data
within the whitespace or capitalization of a seemingly benign email (Ryd et al., 2026)—and the weaker
verifier might fail to catch the sophisticated violation. Conversely, the architectural trade-off manifests when
choosing between special- and general-purpose models. A modular, special-purpose PII-redaction guardrail
might successfully filter out explicit Social Security numbers but fail to recognize that sharing a user’s sudden
shift to purchasing “sugar-free, low-carb groceries” implicitly leaks a new medical diagnosis. A monolithic
general-purpose model might possess the semantic world-knowledge to catch this inferential leak, but its
monolithic reasoning makes formal safety guarantees and isolated auditing significantly harder.


8.5   Interpreting how models use (private) information

Here, we investigate how an agent makes decisions and how the model context influences these decisions.
A naive way to judge appropriateness is to simply request the agent to generate “allow” or “deny” labels
without a transparent justification. While it is common to simply consult LLM reasoning traces in order to
“explain” model behaviors or “validate” that only appropriate information was used, a substantial body of
recent work has demonstrated that such model-provided explanations tend to be highly unfaithful in practice
(Lanham et al., 2023; Vilas et al., 2025; Barez et al., 2025; Mayne et al., 2026).
Recent work in mechanistic interpretability (as explored below) aims to elucidate the internal representations
and mechanisms by which models make decisions, but there is limited work in adapting or applying such
methods to the context of CI, or privacy more broadly. Recent work has also begun to explore internal
representations for agentic privacy, for example by identifying activation-space directions associated with
contextual privacy violations and tracking their evolution across multi-turn interactions (Das & Fioretto,
2026). However, a broader understanding of how models internally represent and use the individual CI
parameters remains largely unexplored. As such, an important challenge is to develop, extend, and/or adapt
mechanistic interpretability methods to faithfully interpret and explain how models use information about key
CI parameters (sender, recipient, subject, attribute, and transmission principle) in various contexts. This will
enable us to better determine the appropriateness of information flow to make decisions or predict/generate
outputs.
Motivating Example. Consider that systems provided with a greater amount of information, including
even inappropriate or task-irrelevant information, are better able to perform a given task than they are when
provided only the minimal amount of necessary, appropriate information according to Mireshghallah et al.
(2026).
From a privacy perspective, this is obviously problematic in that models may make use of inappropriate
information to solve a task; and from a utility perspective, models that rely on “shortcut learning,” such as
leveraging spurious information (i.e., that which may be correlated with task outputs in some distributions,
but not others), generalize worse out-of-distribution (OOD) than models which do not.

   Open Problem 8.4: Interpretable use of private information.

   Can we develop interpretability techniques to understand how and why models use sensitive informa-
   tion in specific contexts?


When, why, and how is this utility-privacy trade-off expected to arise? And given a better understanding of
this phenomenon, how can we appropriately mitigate models’ usage of potentially inappropriate or irrelevant
information while preserving utility? To answer these questions, we should develop and deploy faithful
internal interpretability techniques that can reliably detect what contextual (non-parametric) information
the model has used in order to perform the task, and the degree to which each such piece of information has
influenced the model’s output.



                                                       49
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


Open Problems and Future Directions. One could aim to apply principles of mechanistic interpretabil-
ity to understand privacy and security decisions of the model. We next outline potential directions:

    • Identify feature representations for privacy decisions using previous studies on supervised feature-
      interpretation methods like probing (Alain & Bengio, 2017; Belinkov, 2022), or unsupervised methods
      like SAEs (Subramanian et al., 2018; Yun et al., 2021; Cunningham et al., 2023). Once a given feature
      has been detected by such methods, it is possible to intervene on a model’s internal representation
      to remove or modify the value it encodes for the target feature, then observe how such interventions
      impact model behaviors in order to infer how the model uses the targeted feature (Davies et al., 2023;
      Bricken et al., 2023; Templeton et al., 2024; Canby et al., 2025).
    • Work interpreting information-processing mechanisms studies how latent features represented by mod-
      els are parametrically manipulated by internal mechanisms in order to produce an output—most often,
      via circuit discovery and analysis (Conmy et al., 2023; Wang et al., 2023). For instance, such methods
      could be deployed to study how models’ use of certain features varies by CI scenario—e.g., is there a
      learned internal mechanism that jointly considers recipient and type of information being transmitted
      before approving or denying transmission according to an access control principle?

Finally, given such interpretations of internal latent feature representations and/or mechanisms leveraged by
a model to predict/generate some output, how can we:

    • Develop appropriate auditing procedures to make complex interpretations of privacy decisions under-
      standable by human reviewers?
    • Automatically map interpretations onto relevant CI parameters?
    • (Manually or automatically) audit/assess compliance with respect to provided guidelines?

Answering these questions will help ensure that models behave appropriately regarding privacy and security
when solving tasks or when used for policy engines (§6).


9     User-Level Issues in Privacy- and Security-Aware AI Agents

Whereas traditional computing systems were designed around the user as the main “agent,” the agentic era
requires us to rethink the user’s role. If AI agents could flawlessly, provably, and autonomously execute all
user tasks, user involvement might not be needed. The absence of such capabilities, however, suggests that
we have to ask what the user’s role should be and what they want it to be in this new agentic era. We posit
that answering these questions requires contributions in three areas: supporting accurate mental models,
shifting from static consent to dynamic and contextual control, and establishing user trust.

9.1   Supporting mental model formation and updates

While agents provide unique opportunities in automation across many domains, they represent a fundamen-
tal shift in computing systems. Compared to traditional systems, such as desktop software, mobile apps,
websites, or embedded applications, agents operate with autonomy by planning and executing tools in a loop
to complete tasks. Autonomously accessing information and executing tools challenges user conceptions
about how software executes in the following ways.

    Open Problem 9.1: Supporting user mental models for AI agents.

    In what ways do the mental models of users about privacy and security differ between AI agents and
    traditional systems, such as apps and websites? Also, what mechanisms are needed to form effective
    models and to correct and refresh those models as the agents evolve?

User interactions with agents lead to different considerations, some of which we elaborate on in the following
subsections.



                                                     50
                            SECTION 9: USER-LEVEL ISSUES IN PRIVACY- AND SECURITY-AWARE AI AGENTS


9.1.1   Execution workflows: open-ended and brittle

Unlike traditional systems, agents have open-ended workflows (also noted in §7.5). On the one hand, in
traditional systems, the user executes a command, launches a script, or calls a program, which triggers a
predefined sequence of actions, tool invocations, or API calls. On the other hand, agents are open-ended
as their actions are not predefined and are decided dynamically depending on the current context. Users
cannot predict ahead of time exactly what steps an agent will take to solve a task.
A closely related challenge is the brittleness and jaggedness of the agent operation. Because LLMs, which
are often sensitive to the inputs, plan the sequence of actions, an agent might generate a different plan for
the same task across different users and contexts. Therefore, users do not experience consistent executions
for similar tasks, making it harder for them to reason about whether the agent is making a mistake or just
going through a different execution plan.

9.1.2   Social dynamics: anthropomorphism and interpersonal interactions

Unlike traditional systems, agents have a natural language interface that enables more engaging, social, and
human-like conversations. Because of these interfaces, users treat agents like humans, leading to misplaced
trust and risky behaviors. As a result, users might project human expectations onto the agent (anthropo-
morphism), eliciting privacy reasoning of a social nature instead of institutional privacy expectations applied
to standard software (Ma et al., 2026). These aspects equip the agent with a unique power to shift a user’s
worldview or social beliefs in a way that a traditional computing system does not. This might even be used
by malicious actors to exploit the agent to manipulate its users (Zhan et al., 2025).
Agents change the perception of who is involved in executing an action. Traditional systems involve a prede-
fined set of parties, often authorized through protocols such as OAuth2 (Hardt, 2012). Agents, however, can
engage in interpersonal interactions, communicating with other people or their agents on the user’s behalf.
This creates a many-to-many interaction model with each party associated with a different trust level. Con-
sequently, the agent is not just managing the data of the person prompting it, but also potentially exposing
or acting upon information regarding bystanders, friends, and colleagues involved in the broader context of
the task. Although communicating with external agents or services may still require authentication, the key
differentiating factor is that the information exchanges do not follow a fixed workflow, but are generated
on the fly and are inherently stochastic. This means that authenticated channels do not imply governable
behavior.
Related to the above, agents will create challenges as to how to represent and track information flows across
different contexts. While in traditional systems the user is frequently the sole data subject, agents operate
in multi-entity settings, where entities can be other users, companies, organizations, and even other agents.
They operate on collaborative data, such as shared emails, meeting transcripts, or third-party documents,
thereby processing information belonging to individuals other than the prompter.

9.1.3   Novel privacy and security risks

Users expect risk in traditional systems to arise from untrusted or malicious applications or software running
on a trusted platform, such as an operating system or a browser. Agents, even when not compromised, might
make mistakes in task execution (Jha et al., 2026a). Such mistakes might open users to risks if the agent
is executing sensitive security and privacy tasks. For example, an agent changing the “Account Privacy”
setting from private to public on a social media platform inadvertently exposes the user’s personal data.
Agents often operate across multiple contexts simultaneously, leading to merged contexts. Unlike single-
purpose applications that function within a clearly defined scope, agents frequently mediate interactions
that might span different personal, professional, and social domains. As an agent generates multi-tool plans,
extraneous events or ambiguous data can cause distinct contexts to merge. This blurring of boundaries
makes it difficult for the user to anticipate how the agent will prioritize information, potentially leading
to scenarios where the agent inappropriately applies data or behaviors from a private context to a public
interaction. To operate safely, how can underlying models reason about ambiguity in a manner that aligns
with users (also discussed in §8.2)?



                                                      51
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                           ↑ Table of Contents


9.1.4    Evolving models over time

There are challenges regarding the update, evolution, and transfer of user mental models over continued
use, as well as across different underlying models and agentic architectures. The memory component in AI
agents can accumulate a user profile over time, gathering information and making inferences about the user,
often without direct supervision, which can result in a large digital footprint. This makes it difficult for
users to anticipate and handle personal information retained in memory that could be leaked by the agent,
and to prevent misalignment between the inferred profile and the actual privacy and security preferences. In
addition, research has shown that different models or prompting methods can differ in their susceptibility to
privacy leakage (Agarwal et al., 2024; Green et al., 2025), while users often treat LLMs as a homogeneous
concept. Accordingly, users may need to update their mental models and adapt their control strategies in
response to model changes (e.g., due to a new model release or an upgrade/downgrade for economic reasons)
or prompt changes. Surfacing these differences to users could help mitigate some of these challenges.


9.1.5    The challenges of merged contexts and memory

Agents merge contexts by bridging previously siloed domains, creating the risk that information appropriate
in one sphere is inappropriately transferred to another. Unlike isolated apps, an agent might link a personal
dinner request to a professional calendar, inadvertently exposing medical dietary restrictions to an employer
and violating the distinct norms that govern each context.
This challenge is amplified by “infinite memory,” where agents retain static consent models that fail to adapt
to a user’s evolving life. Instructions valid years ago may become unsuitable as circumstances change—such
as after marriage or parenthood—locking users into “stale” permissions that no longer reflect their current
identity or intent. In these situations, an ideal agent would abstain or ask for fresh context (also discussed
in §8.3).
Agents may also compromise the privacy of bystanders (Zhan et al., 2026a)—friends, family, and colleagues—
whose data is processed without their knowledge. By scanning a user’s communications and photos, the agent
effectively forces unilateral consent decisions onto bystander third parties who never engaged with the system.
Finally, users frequently conflate an agent’s linguistic fluency with social intelligence, assuming that it under-
stands the boundaries of privacy. They may assume the agent understands implied social boundaries (e.g.,
“don’t mention my job search to my boss”), but the system treats all input as raw data unless explicitly
instructed otherwise. This mismatch could lead users to lower their guard, sharing intimate details under
the false assumption that the agent understands the unwritten rules of social discretion.


9.2     Moving beyond notice and choice

Traditionally, the “Notice and Choice” framework has governed online privacy, whereby service providers
notify users and provide them with options regarding how their data is accessed, processed, stored, and
shared. This framework, however, is widely questioned, as data practices, online and off, have increased
in complexity and stretch the comprehension of many individuals. Empirical studies have cast doubt on
whether privacy notices leave their readers any the wiser, and philosophical analysis has questioned whether
consent, in these environments, is meaningful. For example, empirical evidence has revealed that in familiar
settings, ranging from traditional operating systems (e.g., Windows User Account Control prompts (Motiee
et al., 2010; Roesner et al., 2012)) to smartphone platforms (e.g., iOS and Android application permissions)
to the web (e.g., cookie dialogs (Khandelwal et al., 2023; 2021) and browser permission prompts), prompting
the user too often for permission can undermine the goals of informing and providing meaningful control to
users (Egelman et al., 2008).
The advancements in AI have deepened the questions of suitability of the notice and choice framework, as it
possibly becomes harder to provide consent to agents that can be unpredictable and act with a speed with
which users cannot keep up. If data subjects’ consent is not reliably attuned to preferences or interests, it
might leave notice and consent not only impractical but also normatively unmoored.



                                                       52
                            SECTION 9: USER-LEVEL ISSUES IN PRIVACY- AND SECURITY-AWARE AI AGENTS


These limitations set the stage for Contextual Integrity, a different approach to privacy. However, the chal-
lenge contextual integrity poses to the normative underpinnings of notice and consent is not based solely
on these limitations; it also questions the entitlements of individuals to make fine-grained decisions about
personal data. One reason is that the privacy and security decisions of one user may affect others (Viljoen,
2021; Barocas & Levy, 2020; Barocas & Nissenbaum, 2014). Another is that collective, social, contextual
ends and values rightfully deserve consideration and may even supersede the preferences and interests of
a given individual. Rather than relying completely on potentially uninformed decisions made by individu-
als, contextual integrity advocates for reliance on substantive norms, discussed in earlier sections and the
Glossary.

   Open Problem 9.2: Thinking beyond notice and choice for AI agents.

   If the notion of notice and choice was of questionable effectiveness for traditional systems, it is unlikely
   to be effective for AI agents. As such, what frameworks can give users agency and transparency without
   overwhelming them or undermining the agent’s utility?


9.2.1   The different stakeholders in agentic systems

Ideally, agentic systems following contextual integrity would behave in accordance with the requirements
of appropriateness, whether that be seeking consent or conforming with policies and rules. Determining
appropriateness requires understanding the environment and the entities affected by an action. As tempting
as it may be to focus security and privacy considerations strictly around the direct user and the user’s
consent, there are often additional considerations. For example, agents frequently operate in shared contexts
where their behaviors affect bystanders and other third parties.
Considering other third parties is not new. For example, it is well known that camera-based augmented
reality headsets can affect bystanders in the environment (Denning et al., 2014) and that home IoT devices
can play a role in domestic abuse (Freed et al., 2018; Tanczer et al., 2018). Still, agentic systems amplify
these considerations.
Suppose that a user asks their agent to send an email to another person. The email recipient may be
affected if the email contains adversarial data (e.g., a prompt injection) originating elsewhere in the agentic
ecosystem. Or, suppose that an agentic system has access to a user’s wearable camera or microphone; in
such a situation, a bystander’s privacy may be violated if the system inappropriately captures and shares
images of them or records their conversations through the microphone (Zhan et al., 2026a).
As a method for proactively considering bystanders and other third parties, Butler et al. (2025) encourage
system designers to regularly check that they have taken account of all parties affected by their systems’
behaviors. Given the broad vision of appropriately acting agents articulated in this open problems paper,
could agentic systems also consider stakeholders beyond their immediate users (the so-called “default per-
sona” (Sim et al., 2023; 2025)), including but not limited to at-risk and vulnerable populations (McDonald
& Forte, 2022; Warford et al., 2022)?

   Open Problem 9.3: Determining appropriateness of AI agent actions.

   To operationalize Contextual Integrity for agentic security and privacy, an AI agent must determine
   the appropriateness of its actions. How can appropriateness be determined in environments involving
   multiple stakeholders, including users, bystanders, and other third parties?


9.2.2   Accommodation of “notice and choice” in agentic systems

Adopting the framework of Contextual Integrity does not fully displace consent with substantive norms.
As noted in various sections, e.g., §4, “with consent” may serve as the required transmission principle for
contextually appropriate flows. In what follows, we discuss the challenges of operationalizing consent in an
agentic environment, as well as promising approaches to mitigating these.



                                                       53
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                          ↑ Table of Contents


First, static consent mechanisms face challenges when users cannot meaningfully foresee (and therefore agree
to) execution paths that are unknown at the time of the consent request. While foreseeability problems
already started arising in traditional applications with fixed features (e.g., a “Share Location” toggle), these
problems are compounded for agentic systems, which determine their own steps to achieve a goal, often
in ways neither the user nor the developer can fully predict (e.g., an agent inferring the user’s location by
reading recent receipts and calendar invites). Consequently, a user agreeing to “manage my schedule” may be
signing a blank check for future behaviors and data combinations—such as cross-referencing private emails
with public social media—that were difficult to foresee at the moment of consent.
Second, standard consent models focusing on raw data inputs are no match for the powers of agentic systems
that can derive surprising, inferred insights by combining these inputs with other knowledge. For example,
information about health conditions or socioeconomic status might be inferred from seemingly benign data,
such as grocery purchase history. It remains a challenge to develop consent mechanisms that not only cover
the input but also convey or extend to the generated insights.
Third, the binary nature of “Allow” or “Deny” permissions fails to capture the conditional preferences
involved in safe delegation. A user rarely wants an agent to simply “buy plane tickets” without qualification;
they may want to reconsider if the price is too high or the layover is too short. Compressing these complex,
high-dimensional instructions into a static “Allow” button for upcoming actions strips away necessary nuance,
either hindering the agent’s utility or granting it overly broad authority.
Finally, the agent will be producing a large set of sub-tasks, and if it asks for consent while executing
every sub-task, such as reading a specific email or drafting a routine reply, the utility of having an agent
working in the background can significantly decrease. Moreover, when presented repeatedly with similar
questions, users may suffer from increased “prompt fatigue” or “warning fatigue,” and thus begin to pay
less attention to these questions (e.g., simply clicking “accept” to move on with their primary task without
carefully considering the implications), resulting in less safe decisions (Stanton et al., 2016; Anderson et al.,
2016). Conversely, asking less frequent and broader questions can lead to overly broad agent authority.

9.2.3   From static permission to dynamic control

One approach to addressing the issue of consent in agents, where it is a legitimating condition, is to leverage
their automation capabilities. Here, we could seek to identify factors that can determine how agents are
supervised. These factors, or dimensions, shift us away from a simple “all-or-nothing” binary to a dynamic
spectrum of control.
For example, the agent might seek clarification from users, such as asking which “Sarah” to email or con-
firming the tone of a message, thereby ensuring that consent is tied to the immediate context and specific
intent and preventing the agent from acting on stale or ambiguous instructions that were authorized months
prior. Alternatively, there are actions that do not require consent or explicit user confirmation. As such, one
important consideration is to explore the design of an agent that adopts a mixed-initiative approach where
it adjusts the level of autonomy based on numerous factors, in particular risk level. This approach would
treat user attention as a limited resource, interrupting the user when the consequence of an error warrants
human intervention and also allowing factors other than consent to prevail. Thus, for example, low-stakes
tasks, like organizing a playlist, can proceed automatically; high-stakes actions such as financial transfers
require explicit confirmation, and actions imposing risks on third parties may be blocked entirely. The Janus
permission management playground provides a platform for experimenting with different approaches for user
involvement, and its findings give insights into the opportunities and challenges for user-involved permission
management (Brigham et al., 2027).

Generative interfaces. Where consent is paramount, just-in-time requests may become more effective
through dynamically generated and context-aware interfaces (Chen et al., 2026). Instead of using the same
user interface for each permission request, the agent can dynamically create one that is tailored to the request,
possibly structuring complex information visually in a more accessible manner, thereby reducing the user’s
cognitive load. For example, the interface could visualize the permission request as an information flow,
including the parties involved, the data type, and potential risks. To illustrate the real-world implications of



                                                       54
                            SECTION 9: USER-LEVEL ISSUES IN PRIVACY- AND SECURITY-AWARE AI AGENTS


user decisions, the level of visualization could be adjusted to the use case, ranging from sketches (Monteiro
& Das, 2026) and block diagrams to concrete vignettes, e.g., describing a guest trying to access a camera
late at night.

Personal preferences. Another approach to reduce consent fatigue might be to take into account personal
preferences. Whereas contextual integrity questions whether individuals are entitled to make fine-grained de-
cisions about personal data, agents can place personal preferences as part of a hierarchy of policies that steer
the agent (Zhan & Haddadi, 2026). Such policies can include legal guidelines, institutional policies, social
norms, and developer-specified guidance (Meng et al., 2026). There is precedent in the research community,
especially in automated permission assistants that attempt to automatically decide on permission requests
taking into account past user decisions, similar users’ decisions, and other contextual information (Baarslag
et al., 2017; Misra & Such, 2017; Zhan et al., 2023). There is also preliminary work on permission automation
for agentic systems that shows prior decisions from users enable predicting future decisions with high accu-
racy (Flemings et al., 2026; Fawaz et al., 2026). Beyond historical data, agentic systems could also extract
implicit permission information directly from user prompts (e.g., recognizing “sending my current location
to Alice” as an implicit authorization to access GPS and contacts for that task) (Roesner et al., 2012).

   Open Problem 9.4: Enabling user supervision over automation.

   AI agents operate at a pace that their users cannot keep up with, making user approval of each action
   impractical. Instead of asking users to decide on every action, can they specify policies that steer
   agents to act appropriately? If so, how can we elicit such policies from users, and how can users
   maintain, edit, and verify them over time?


However, operationalizing this approach raises two core challenges: onboarding and maintenance. The first
challenge is how to elicit agent policies from users (Monteiro & Das, 2026), who are not necessarily tech-
savvy and do not have the bandwidth to spend a lot of time interacting with the agent. There is an open
question of how agent policies can be represented and how agents can be made to abide by policies. There
might be several onboarding strategies that provide different trade-offs between usability and utility. On
one end, the agent can run the user through a detailed set of scenarios to be encountered in the real world,
and gather user decisions about them. Compressing these scenarios and the corresponding user answers
would yield policies that are predictive of their actions (Teltzrow & Kobsa, 2004). However, such a strategy
might be cumbersome to the users. Another onboarding strategy might rely on collecting previous behavior
from the user, either by asking them to recall it or by querying it directly from their prior decisions. This
strategy involves less user effort and might result in policies that align with users’ prior actions. However,
these prior actions might be biased by poor interfaces, deceptive patterns, and psychological phenomena
such as resignation. Another strategy could include adaptively interviewing users to elicit a model of their
decision-making (Namara et al., 2018).
The second challenge is enabling users to edit and update their policies, which may be necessary (1) in
reaction to agent behavior that is misaligned with their preferences, or (2) as their preferences evolve in
response to life events and technology trends. Supporting such updates requires representing policies in an
editable format. While actual policies might be specified in a formal language, a natural language interface
could allow users to read and edit them. Also, agents could support continuous policy maintenance, possibly
through at least two mechanisms. The first is in-the-moment disambiguation, where the agent pauses to
query the user when it encounters ambiguous intent or conflicting contexts. The second is retrospective
editing, which allows users to review past agent actions and seamlessly adjust the underlying rules if the
agent made an incorrect inference.

Agency with automated consent decisions. An additional challenge in automating consent decisions
is that it might deprive the user of agency, raising questions regarding current regulatory frameworks. In
online systems, the legal and regulatory infrastructure is built on an assumption that a human clicking a
button is proof of intent (Hartzog, 2010). For example, OAuth consent screens require the user to click
“Allow” to grant permissions (Hardt, 2012), payment confirmation flows (reinforced by PSD2 Strong Cus-



                                                      55
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                          ↑ Table of Contents


tomer Authentication (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2015)) require the user to
actively confirm transaction details, and clicking “I Agree” on a Terms of Service creates a legally binding
contract (Dickens, 2007). On the other hand, agents interact on the user’s behalf by clicking buttons, filling
forms, and navigating pages, exactly as a human would within an authenticated browser session. When an
agent clicks “Buy Now,” the system records this as “the user consented to this purchase,” even though this
may not have been explicit to the user. If the “click” no longer carries its consensual meaning, how does this
interact with the current legal, regulatory, and security infrastructure (which largely still assumes it does)?
Most existing authentication protocols, browsers, and APIs have no way of signaling whether the clicking
entity is a human or an agent. Under the California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA), a user can authorize
an “agent,” which can be another person or business entity, to make privacy requests, such as accessing,
deleting, or editing personal information. However, no framework exists to govern the situation where a
non-human agent makes security and privacy decisions, such as consent decisions.

9.3     Establishing and maintaining user trust

A major challenge for AI agents is whether human users can trust them to complete actions on their behalf
while accessing sensitive data or consequential tools. The notion of trust is multifaceted, especially as agents
take on roles that transcend distinct domains: task assistance, information retrieval, social companionship,
and even corporate representation. To help build human trust, AI agents benefit from mechanisms for user
control, auditing, and accountability (Such et al., 2014).

9.3.1    On the notions of trust and trustworthiness

Whereas “trust” is often used to refer to both the act of trust (e.g., the user trusts the system) and trustwor-
thiness (e.g., the system is worthy of the user’s trust), it is helpful to distinguish between the two concepts.
Ideally, agentic systems are both trustworthy and trusted, since one without the other is less desirable.
Consider a manipulative mechanism through which a user develops trust in a system to provide a specific
property when, in fact, the system is untrustworthy with respect to that property. Another undesirable,
though likely less harmful, configuration for the user is when the agentic system is trustworthy but the user
does not trust it, leading to opportunity costs. How can we develop methods through which users can assess
the trustworthiness of agentic systems and, ultimately, trust those systems that are trustworthy?
Closing this gap between trust and trustworthiness requires giving users an accessible means to inspect what
the agent does and why. Could such a transparency mechanism move beyond complex settings menus to
leverage the agent’s core strength: natural language? Users could audit the system through direct dialogue,
asking questions like “Why did you send that?” or “What do you know about my health?” Such a mechanism,
if feasible and reliable, would allow users to correct wrong inferences and refine the agent’s behavior using the
same conversational interface they use for delegating tasks, providing intuitive oversight over agent behavior.
However, the gap between what an agent claims and what it actually executes complicates this objective.
Current research and empirical observations indicate that AI agents often fail to answer accurately when
interrogated about their own privacy behaviors, occasionally hallucinating or incorrectly denying that they
accessed sensitive data (Zhang et al., 2025c). Because the open-ended nature of LLMs makes them unreliable
narrators of their actions, conversational transparency is insufficient. We need mechanisms that guarantee
verifiable correctness. Achieving this notion requires “trusted paths” that decouple the generation of the audit
log from the untrusted task LLM. For example, the system could rely on a dedicated permission assistant or
explicit visual UI indicators backed by an immutable record of what the agent actually did. One way to realize
this architecture is to leverage hardware-backed confidential execution frameworks (Abdollahi et al., 2026).
Such frameworks isolate the agent runtime, the LLM inference engine, and third-party applications into
confidential virtual machines (cVMs) and mediate all interactions through secure communication channels.

9.3.2    Calibrating trust through “what if” scenarios

Trust in agents is highly situational, where a user has to establish trust for a specific purpose, in a specific
environment, at a specific time. For example, trusting an agent to act as a “gofer” to schedule meetings
does not automatically translate to trusting it as a “coach” for mental health advice (Zhan et al., 2024b).



                                                       56
                            SECTION 9: USER-LEVEL ISSUES IN PRIVACY- AND SECURITY-AWARE AI AGENTS


Expecting users to build this situationally calibrated trust by experimenting in a live environment could lead
to consequential mistakes. Could users benefit from stress-testing the agent in hypothetical situations, i.e.,
in a simulated environment? These situations might correspond to new domains in which the user does not
yet trust the agent. Therefore, a key research question is how we can enable users to calibrate their trust
through simulation (e.g., “what would you do if . . . ”) or stress-testing before deployment.
One view mimics how individuals build trust in social settings. When people meet someone new, they try
to establish trust in a progressive manner. First, they engage with the acquaintance in less sensitive tasks
where, if the acquaintance behaves inappropriately, there is little harm. Then, as they build increased trust,
they entrust the acquaintance with more important responsibilities. For example, Lewicki & Bunker (1996)
hypothesized three stages of developing trust over time in team relationships. The first stage is developing
calculus-based trust where teams engage in activities to establish trust validity. After a history of interac-
tion, calculus-based trust evolves to knowledge-based trust. Individuals know enough about each other such
that they can predict their behavior, thereby improving trust. While calculus-based trust is grounded in
deterrence, knowledge-based trust is grounded in knowledge about the other individuals. Eventually, some
relationships with knowledge-based trust evolve into identification-based trust, where individuals effectively
understand each other to the extent that they act on each other’s behalf. Earlier research (Deutsch, 1949)
hypothesizes that identification-based trust enables one to act as another’s “agent” in interpersonal relation-
ships. According to this view, user trust in an agent will require a history of interactions where the user can
understand the agent and know enough about it to be able to predict its behaviors.
We can also accelerate the trust-building process through simulating scenarios. A user can interact with
the agent in a simulated environment and stress-test it in various realistic ways and scenarios. The user
can then monitor the agent interactions and responses to build a trust model. Simulated environments also
ensure that there is no harm to the user when the agent misbehaves. However, such an approach might be
challenging to achieve in practice. One challenge relates to designing a user interface that is welcoming to the
user and reduces the barrier to entry, especially for novice and non-tech-savvy users. The second challenge
is that simulated scenarios need to be realistic and match the user’s use cases. For example, if a user is
using an agent as a personal assistant, perhaps simulated scenarios should consider the personalized context
of the user. Here, we cannot expect users to come up with these scenarios. Third, simulated scenarios
should elicit realistic agent behavior. There is increasing evidence that models detect when they are under
test (Abdelnabi & Salem, 2025). A model detecting, even implicitly, that it is operating in a simulated
environment would not yield real-world behavior.
One challenge of building trust over time is that lengthy calibration risks vendor lock-in. If a user wants to
switch to a different vendor’s agent, transferring this established trust remains an open problem.

9.3.3   Delayed execution and circuit breakers

Beyond calibration, another mechanism to increase user trust in agents is to allow users to intervene during
autonomous execution. While some current systems let users send “stop” messages to the agent, it is unclear
whether there is any guarantee that the model will abide by them (Bort, 2026). If a task involves highly
sensitive actions, data, or irreversible state changes (such as deleting production logs, initiating financial
transfers, or deactivating accounts (Ramesh et al., 2026)), the agent could incorporate delayed execution. It
can either adapt its plan or escalate to the user to request explicit confirmation. Of course, such a delayed
execution mechanism exhibits a trade-off between autonomy and user control.
Another related concept, though challenging to employ in agentic systems, is reversibility. An agent can
incorporate “undo” windows for sensitive actions to allow users to reverse the effects of operations before
they become permanent. Such a mechanism requires the agentic system or the systems the agent interacts
with to keep track of pre- and post-action states, similar to rollback mechanisms in database systems. It is
not always the case that an agent will interact with tools and systems with such capabilities. However, one
possible direction is for the agent to simulate the effects of its actions in a synthetic environment or within
a world model.
Moreover, agents could possess a system-level circuit breaker with a deterministic operation. Such a circuit
breaker should have a usable interface and should conclusively halt the agent’s operation. Implementing



                                                      57
PART II: COMPONENT-LEVEL PERSPECTIVES                                                         ↑ Table of Contents


such functionality can also be challenging, particularly in complex agents, or when sub-agents and delegated
tasks are involved. An agent is typically not a single execution process. It has a planner module, and at any
time it is orchestrating the execution of several synchronous and asynchronous tools or sub-agents. Still,
in response to a “stop” command from the user, the agent should stop generating new actions and send
“kill” signals to all processes it has spawned. Ideally, it reports back to the user the status of all processes
and whether it was able to terminate them. Such circuit breakers may also be desirable for platforms that
facilitate communication among many agents, in case unexpected collective behavior emerges that requires
time to examine (akin to the circuit breakers used in stock exchanges).

   Open Problem 9.5: Delaying, stopping, and reversing AI agent actions.

   An agentic system may not be a single synchronous process that can easily be stopped when needed. It
   can involve sub-agents and orchestrate synchronous and asynchronous tools with external side effects.
   How can we equip users with guarantees that a “stop” command conclusively halts execution, and
   that sensitive actions can be delayed, previewed, or reversed?


9.3.4   New legal and regulatory frameworks

An important open problem for agentic systems is their real-world trust model. Despite all built-in and
bolt-on protections, agents will perform actions that might cause harm or leave no recourse. It is critical to
consider how human users can trust agents that, by nature, will make mistakes. Fortunately, as no domain
is completely error-free, humans have been engaging with systems and individuals that make mistakes and
have accidents. In response, there exist legal and technical processes that manage situations where failures
happen. For example, while traditional software systems can be more deterministic, they are still prone to
failures. Service providers offer Service Level Agreements (SLAs), liability frameworks, and incident response
protocols that define acceptable errors and establish remediation when failures occur. The physical world
has processes and frameworks for establishing liability and distributing risk, such as tort law, insurance
policies, and professional licensing, which ensure that affected parties are compensated and operators are
held accountable when human or mechanical errors cause harm.

   Open Problem 9.6: Accountability frameworks for AI agents.

   Despite best efforts, AI agents are likely to make mistakes that cause harm. What should an ac-
   countability framework for AI agents look like, and what would contracts, transparency, liability, and
   incentives mean in such a framework?

Because AI agents operate with a level of independence that blurs the boundaries between mechanical tools
and human actors, finding the right trust framework requires a shift in perspective. We find it useful to
draw parallels with legal agency and human contractors. Much like a human contractor, an AI agent is
granted the autonomy to determine the specific means of achieving a user’s (principal’s) goals. There are
different frameworks in the literature that model this relationship, such as principal-agent theory (Prause,
2026). The principal-agent relationship covers the delegation of decision-making authority from the principal
to an agent, where the principal relies on the agent to act in their best interest. This dynamic introduces
challenges related to alignment of objectives, information asymmetry, incentives, and accountability. In the
real world, this relationship is constrained by a legal framework, which includes contracts, transparency,
liability, and incentives.

  • Contracts establish a legally binding relationship. They define the scope of authorized work, measurable
    deliverables, payment structures, and conditions for termination.
  • Transparency addresses the information asymmetry by allowing the principal to verify the agent’s daily
    actions. Transparency mechanisms include mandatory reporting or third-party audits.
  • Liability imposes accountability by ensuring the agent bears the legal and financial consequences for
    negligence, breach of contract, or self-dealing.



                                                      58
                            SECTION 9: USER-LEVEL ISSUES IN PRIVACY- AND SECURITY-AWARE AI AGENTS


  • Financial and reputational incentives align the relationship by ensuring the agent profits directly from
    achieving the principal’s ultimate goals.

Under such a framework, human principals could trust an agent because they have a binding contract, can
review the agent’s actions, know that the agent will be liable for those actions, and understand the agent’s
incentives. However, can such a framework be adapted to improve trust between the user (principal) and AI
agents? Toward that end, we need to rethink the elements of such a framework to leverage its properties to
improve user-agent trust relationships.
First, while agents possess the autonomy of a contractor, they lack the legal capacity of entering into
a traditional contract with a human user. Instead, establishing a “contract” with an AI agent requires
translating legalese into explicit policies that become part of the agent’s context window every time it
executes a task. This approach has technical challenges. There are currently no deterministic guarantees
that an LLM-based agent will strictly follow the provided policies or interpret them the same way a human
would.
Second, AI agents can be more amenable to transparency than human-based agents, albeit using different
mechanisms. A user can audit an AI agent by viewing its reasoning process, such as its chain-of-thought
or execution logs, in real time. However, unlike a human contractor who can explain their rationale, LLMs
remain inherently opaque. Managing this opacity requires interpretability tools that might not be accessible
to the layperson. Furthermore, there is the risk of post-hoc rationalization (Peng et al., 2026), where an
agent generates a plausible-sounding but entirely hallucinated explanation for its actions, creating a false
sense of transparency (Turpin et al., 2023; Lanham et al., 2023).
Third, agents lack liability: they cannot be “punished” for destructive actions or sued for negligence. Al-
ternatively, liability could shift to human and corporate stakeholders, such as model developers, application
providers, or system deployers. Still, it might be unclear how to reason about liability in the face of ambiguous
user queries, untrusted inputs, model hallucinations, and malfunctioning third-party APIs.
Finally, unlike human contractors whose motivations are generally understood (e.g., payment, reputation),
an agent’s “incentives” are defined by opaque objective functions (i.e., values in a tensor). Such incentives
are not intuitive to the user and are complicated by context fed into the model along with the direct prompt.
Neither the incentive nor the context may be visible or salient to the user. The primary challenge in the
former is reward hacking, where an agent finds a technical loophole to optimize its objective function without
actually fulfilling the user’s underlying intent. The latter requires auditing mechanisms to reveal potential
misaligned incentives resulting from adversarial prompt injection and conflicts that may naturally emerge
between accumulated system context and the direct prompt. Translating the interests of a human principal
into quantifiable metrics remains an open research question.




                                                       59
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                              ↑ Table of Contents




       Part III: Deployment and Ecosystem Perspectives

Part III switches from problems at various levels of agent components to the problem of preserving contextual
privacy and security in real-world settings. §10 first discusses open challenges in evaluating agents, focusing
on building stronger benchmarks that are grounded in large-scale user studies or long-running tasks. §11
then discusses how agents may be deployed in multi-agent environments in practice, and the challenges
with inter-agent communication and coordination: trustworthy protocols, deployment practices, and new
environments. Finally, §12 concludes by discussing governance challenges: how to source, maintain, and
resolve conflicts in contextual norms as agents shift between different contexts and perform various tasks.

10     Evaluations and Benchmarking

Historically, standardized benchmarks have served as the primary engine for AI development by provid-
ing reproducible environments, scalable automated performance and security measurements, and a way to
compare systems on even ground. They facilitate the identification of systematic failures without relying
on prohibitively expensive human-in-the-loop assessments. However, agents operate in complex, dynamic
environments over long time horizons, interacting with both external, independent systems and internal
memories related to their past actions and user data. For these reasons, evaluating the reliability and safety
of these systems—particularly regarding complex, socially grounded constructs like privacy and Contextual
Integrity (CI)—presents profound methodological challenges.
This section begins with a brief overview of the known difficulties in the science of benchmarking and
evaluating frontier agents (§10.1). We then review existing CI benchmarks, highlighting their strengths and
limitations (§10.2). In light of this information, we establish and discuss key problems we believe future
research should address to enable more accurate and reliable evaluation of systems complying with the CI
framework (§10.3, §10.4).

10.1   General difficulties of benchmarking frontier agents

Evaluating frontier agents’ general capabilities exposes major limitations and disadvantages in current bench-
marking approaches:

  • Construct Validity: Many current agent benchmarks, e.g., AgentDojo (Debenedetti et al., 2024b),
    rely on highly constrained simulated environments that abstract away real-world complexity. Agents
    that overfit to these simulated sandboxes frequently experience catastrophic failure in production when
    confronted with the complexity and ambiguity of human instructions, system errors, and partial or
    changing information. Evaluation metrics are also computed in such sanitized, simplified environments.
    Furthermore, agents have been observed to produce inconsistent behaviors despite identical inputs, and
    to be extremely sensitive to how the same information is represented.
  • Trajectory Evaluation and Compounding Errors: Unlike single-turn text generation, agents
    execute multi-step workflows. A minor hallucination or incorrect tool selection early in a plan can
    result in a complete failure and prevent the assessment of the desired capabilities/properties of a
    system (Zhang et al., 2024). Current benchmarks largely rely on single-run, binary “task completion”
    scores that overlook highly inefficient or redundant execution paths, fail to account for trajectory
    variance across multiple attempts, and fail to penalize unsafe intermediate steps or to reward sound
    reasoning. Developing granular metrics to evaluate an agent’s entire operational trajectory remains an
    open challenge.



                                                      60
                                                            SECTION 10: EVALUATIONS AND BENCHMARKING


  • Data Contamination: The indiscriminate scraping of internet data for LLM pre-training frequently
    leads to data contamination, where open-source benchmark test sets leak into a model’s training corpus.
    This results in artificially inflated performance metrics driven by memorization rather than generalized
    reasoning (Sainz et al., 2023).
  • Unreliable Evaluation Metrics: Because deterministic exact-match grading is too rigid for open-
    ended agent trajectories, researchers increasingly rely on frontier AI models as automated evaluators.
    However, these proxy LLMs could exhibit significant positional biases, favor verbose responses, exhibit
    strong inconsistencies across multiple runs on the same inputs, and often diverge from human expert
    reasoning (Wang et al., 2024). Evaluating an LLM’s agentic behavior using another LLM could create
    a recursive loop of unreliability and poor metric calibration (He, 2026). Alternatively, evaluation
    procedures that rigorously measure a specific property may be expensive, difficult, or impossible to
    develop.
  • Evaluation Awareness and Plausibility of Benchmarks: Frontier models may behave differ-
    ently when they detect—or infer—that they are being evaluated rather than operating in a genuine
    deployment context. Subtle cues in prompt formatting, synthetic scenario design, or the structure
    of benchmark harnesses can signal an evaluation setting (Abdelnabi & Salem, 2025), potentially elic-
    iting more cautious, policy-compliant, or otherwise atypical behavior that does not reflect how the
    system would act under naturalistic conditions. This raises a fundamental tension: benchmarks must
    be sufficiently controlled to yield reproducible measurements, yet sufficiently realistic to avoid cuing
    the model into a distinct “evaluation mode.” As models grow more capable of situational awareness,
    the gap between benchmark performance and real-world behavior may widen, making it increasingly
    difficult to draw reliable conclusions about an agent’s true operational profile from standardized tests
    alone. Designing evaluation environments that are both rigorous and ecologically plausible remains an
    underexplored methodological challenge.
  • Synthetic Data and Evaluation Biases: Increasingly, researchers rely on synthetically generated
    benchmarks to evaluate agents due to cost constraints, yet this introduces substantial measurement
    artifacts. Synthetic test collections exhibit systematic biases, including inflated performance metrics
    and preference for systems aligned with the generative model’s patterns (Rahmani et al., 2025). More
    fundamentally, recent work (Naseh & Mireshghallah, 2025) shows that membership inference attacks
    function as machine-generated text detectors rather than true membership tests, incorrectly classifying
    synthetic data as training samples regardless of source; this raises the specter that synthetic evaluations
    may fundamentally mislead conclusions about model capabilities and memorization. Additionally,
    smaller LLMs exhibit pronounced biases toward their own generated data or generative patterns as
    benchmarks (as quantified by the “bias factor” metric in recent research (Maheshwari et al., 2024)),
    while the effectiveness of synthetic data as evaluation material degrades substantially for complex tasks.
    These factors collectively suggest that synthetic evaluations risk systematically mischaracterizing agent
    behavior, particularly when the same models are used to both generate evaluation datasets and perform
    evaluated tasks.

   Open Problem 10.1: Advancing automated red-teaming on saturated benchmarks.

   It is critical to study why existing automated red-teaming evaluations fail to discover new attacks on
   leading models. How can we determine whether these results arise because models are truly robust,
   because the benchmark environments are too simplistic, because models are aware they operate in test
   settings, or because automated red-teaming methods are lagging behind model improvements?

Strong adaptive adversarial attacks have always been core to assessing empirical defenses to security issues
in AI (Tramèr et al., 2020). This was true in the pre-LLM era, where adaptive attacks regularly defeated
proposed defenses to adversarial examples (Goodfellow et al., 2014), and it continues to be true in the agentic-
LLM era, where Contextual Security needs to be robustly tested against committed, intelligent adversaries.
Between 2024 and 2026, it became standard practice to set up strong, automated attacks within agentic
environments that helped give the developer an idea of how well an agent can perform aligned actions within
untrusted environments which may contain adversaries (Shi et al., 2025). This standard practice started to



                                                      61
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                              ↑ Table of Contents


be marginalized in mid-2026: frontier labs like Anthropic deprecated automated red-teaming, citing limited
benefits in discovering new ways to break the agent (Anthropic, 2026c). More generally, the claim appears
to be that new models have reached a sufficient understanding of Contextual Security to withstand the
most powerful automated red-teaming evaluations. Another interpretation blames increasing test-awareness;
determining which claims are correct remains an open question. Answering the above question will be highly
important to the future of properly forecasting a model’s Contextual Security before deployment.


10.2    A review of Contextual Integrity benchmarks for LLMs and agents

Recent datasets and benchmarks focus on quantifying how LLMs and agents can follow Contextual Integrity
(CI) and complete various tasks that require sharing information. In §D, we carefully design an analysis
framework to study existing benchmarks and conduct an analysis across 16 existing benchmarks (Table 2).
Through this analysis we find that often static evaluation methods gradually evolve into dynamic, multi-turn,
multi-modal, and multi-user environments.
Despite this rapid progress, our analysis in §D.2 reveals three limitations across current benchmarks. First,
transmission principles and ambiguity remain heavily simplified: apart from the Camber framework, most
benchmarks intentionally filter out ambiguous edge cases to maintain deterministic ground truths. Second,
benchmarks rely overwhelmingly on synthetic personas and annotations: existing datasets depend on frontier
LLMs to procedurally generate user profiles or rely on crowdsourced raters evaluating fictitious vignettes
rather than real human-agent interactions. Third, evaluations treat CI parameters as static, single-session
snapshots: even multi-turn agent benchmarks such as PrivacyLens-Live, AgentDAM, and PrivacyBench
bound their trajectories to a single task or up to ten conversational turns, failing to capture how persistent
system context, cumulative leakage, and shifting social norms evolve over multi-year time horizons. We
discuss these limitations below.


10.3    Large-scale user studies on human perception of AI agents are missing

As AI agents are still in rapid development and access to real agent traces is rare, there has not yet been
a comprehensive user study that tries to measure how users perceive AI agents that perform real tasks on
their behalf. Real tasks are difficult to collect as environments are still rapidly evolving and the ways users
are interacting with agents are rapidly changing. Further, perhaps only a subset of daily use cases will face
agentic privacy and security failures where agents run outside of robust sandboxes and without manually
monitored tool use.


   Current studies are thus limited to fictitious scenarios, privacy seed collection, and common-sense
   evaluations. The resulting evaluation environments are simplistic and not nearly as complex as in real
   tasks. There is an emergent need to understand human preferences and norms for agent behavior in
   real, messy environments and for the tasks for which agents will be used.



   Open Problem 10.2: Designing and implementing large-scale studies.

   There are many questions that need to be studied for comprehensive and faithful user studies:
       • What should be the focus of user studies? How can we focus efforts around impactful settings
         where there are conflicting norms, misunderstandings, and ambiguities?
       • What is the ground truth for contextually appropriate privacy and security actions? How do we
         account for disagreements among users, cultural differences, or the user’s uncertainty in their
         own preference? How do we leverage observable behavioral data and self-reported data to derive
         alignment targets or evaluative measurements? How do we deal with the privacy paradox? How
         do we deal with users not understanding risks implied in privacy or security breaches?




                                                      62
                                                            SECTION 10: EVALUATIONS AND BENCHMARKING




       • How should we conduct user studies to capture complex real-world dynamics that vary across
         human-agent and agent-agent interactions, differ between solo task use and agent-mediated
         multi-person communication settings, and unfold over different time scales? How can we achieve
         an effective balance among ecological validity, controllability, and ethics when studying how users
         respond to agent actions that may have privacy and security consequences in realistic settings?
       • How should we simulate the environments in which agents execute security-vulnerable tasks?
         Can we faithfully leverage synthetic data, including user and attacker simulations, to emulate
         realistic settings that capture real uncertainties? How is this data presented to users?
       • Do we need separate user studies? What are the benefits of user studies over shadow and A/B
         testing to evaluate how agents perform in realistic and ever-evolving settings?

Many of these questions have been previously answered by the HCI community in non-agent settings using
methods like qualitative interviews and controlled experiments, which yield empirical insights and theoretical
constructs that capture patterns and pitfalls in privacy-related actions in human-computer interaction (Mar-
tin & Nissenbaum, 2017). Understanding how these results transfer to today’s evolving agent settings, and
developing new paradigms of human-centered privacy research (including studies that involve real users and
user simulations) that are more scalable and replicable to keep pace with rapid AI advances, are important
future directions.

10.4    Contextual Integrity for life: evaluating CI over long time horizons

Current benchmarks for Contextual Integrity largely test privacy parameters as static snapshots. However,
the theoretical strength of the CI framework lies in its inherent flexibility: it accounts for evolving social
norms as social and technological needs change, shifting actors, overlapping contexts that exert mutual
influence over time, and the adaptation of information flows to meet new goals.


   As frontier agents transition into lifelong assistants, this dynamism is no longer a theoretical edge case
   but an aspect that demands adequate evaluation.


We next discuss five directions that evaluations and benchmarks can pursue to satisfy this need.

   Open Problem 10.3: Evaluating how agents persist and use historical system context.

   As an agent manages data across multiple years and sessions, it must ensure that loaded historical
   context accounts for contextual relevance and does not surface “stale” information that violates the
   user’s current preferences or current contextual norms (§8.3). The lack of a unified long-term memory
   mechanism and the fast-paced research developments in this area suggest that benchmarking efforts
   should provide their data in a format that can be easily adapted to assess new ways of storing and
   retrieving memories.

The development of benchmarks that perform context-aware and temporally informed storage and retrieval
of memories could be the catalyst for innovative memory-management methodologies that prioritize the
long-term aggregation of information according to evolving privacy norms. Additionally, these benchmarks
should evaluate the ability of such systems to filter inappropriate information before it enters the context
window to reduce the burden and the risk of loading inappropriate information into the agent’s context
window. Furthermore, acknowledging that a user’s life is not a static database necessitates the development
of sophisticated versioning systems for personal memory. This evolution encourages researchers to treat
historical data as a living narrative subject to revision, decay, and re-contextualization over time.
To support this, future benchmarking efforts must provide datasets in modular, interoperable formats that
can represent the intricate temporal relationships between CI parameters. Crucially, these formats must
remain independent of the specific memory mechanisms of frontier agents. This decoupling of the data



                                                      63
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                               ↑ Table of Contents


format from the underlying architecture allows for a more rigorous, cross-platform assessment of how various
storage and retrieval strategies navigate the friction between privacy boundaries and multi-year temporal
grounding.

   Open Problem 10.4: Evaluating leakage in aggregated data flows.

   Current benchmarks mainly focus on single-flow evaluations: they ensure the execution of a single
   agentic run satisfies the CI framework. This fails to account for leakage that may result from the cu-
   mulative behavior of the agent over time. We currently lack a way to evaluate how privacy leakage may
