   distribute over multiple interactions in the agent’s lifecycle. This may occur both in non-adversarial
   settings (e.g., a norm that is not violated in a single execution of the agent) and adversarial ones
   (e.g., adversaries who do not seek an immediate exfiltration or single-point failures, but, similar to
   linkage or attribute inference attacks, instead reconstruct user identities or secrets through long-term
   observation of agent traces). Recent benchmarks have begun to capture this longitudinal dimension;
   for example, SEABench evaluates self-evolving agents through sequences in which earlier interac-
   tions modify persistent agent state and later tasks test whether those adaptations remain safe as the
   context changes (Das et al., 2026). Such evaluations provide a useful starting point for studying how
   privacy and contextual norms evolve over time. However, we lack realistic, comprehensive evaluation
   methodologies and standardized metrics to quantify how much privacy is lost with each subsequent
   interaction, and what “rate of leakage” accumulates across multiple agent flows (Das et al., 2025).


Drawing inspiration from existing literature, we believe a future benchmark modeling an attacker’s goal could
synthesize a “shadow profile” from hundreds of non-sensitive agent interactions. This includes modeling
inferential leaps—where the agent inadvertently confirms a hypothesis the user never intended to share. In
addition, some potential techniques could strategically obfuscate or hinder this procedure, e.g., abstraction
layers, added noise, or anonymization procedures.

   Open Problem 10.5: Benchmarking dynamic privacy-utility trade-offs.

   Most privacy benchmarks treat user preferences and norms as static variables—assuming that if a
   piece of data is “sensitive” today, it will remain so forever. In reality (and in accordance with CI),
   privacy and utility are subject to temporal relativity, where a user’s willingness to share or retrieve
   information shifts based on their current life stage, social context, and emotional distance from the
   event (§8.3). The “emotional charge” of a memory can flip over time. A traumatic event in one’s youth
   may require strict privacy filtering in early adulthood but become a high-utility “bonding story” in
   later years. Static benchmarks cannot account for this re-contextualization. Privacy requirements vary
   drastically across developmental milestones (e.g., a teenager’s need for “forgetting” vs. a professional’s
   need for “archiving”). Current models lack a framework to adjust their retrieval logic as the user’s
   identity evolves. Over-protecting “old” data can lead to a sterile agent experience that lacks personal
   depth, while under-protecting it can cause significant reputational or psychological harm. There is
   no standardized way to measure the “sweet spot” of this trade-off as it moves over a 10- or 20-year
   horizon.

Developing datasets that include “contextual metadata” would allow researchers to test whether an agent
can recognize when a topic has shifted from “off-limits” to “shareable.” Benchmarking efforts could utilize
mechanisms to simulate the development of this contextual metadata, and test the ability of an agent to
access and leverage memories across different simulated decades of a user’s life. This would require a shift
from objective accuracy to a form of subjective alignment.

   Open Problem 10.6: Evaluations that carry across environments.

   Evaluations conducted in different environments often tell us little about an agent’s behavior when
   deployed in new environments. Current CI benchmarks are coupled to specific agent architectures, tool




                                                      64
                                              SECTION 11: TOWARDS TRUSTWORTHY MULTI-AGENT ECOSYSTEMS




     interfaces, and execution environments. Some depend on specific text-based QA formats with multi-tier
     vignettes, others assume particular chat and email interfaces, and others are tied to specific webform
     structures and field schemas. This coupling means that a change on either side—the agent’s framework
     or the benchmark’s infrastructure—can silently invalidate measurements. An interface redesign or a
     migration between agentic frameworks can break a benchmark without any change to the underlying
     privacy question. It is difficult to separate what is being evaluated (CI compliance) from how the
     evaluation is executed (the specific harness and action format). Therefore, each change necessitates
     rebuilding benchmarks from scratch, creating a perpetual lag between deployed configurations and
     available evaluations.

Benchmarks must consider how, in a new environment, an agent may gain access to new tools, lose access to
previously available environments, or be redeployed to a different domain. Each such change reshapes the
CI parameter space, and a benchmark designed for one agent configuration may become useless for another
agent. New tools introduce new senders and recipients, new environments introduce new information types,
and new protocols introduce new transmission principles. This problem is amplified by the increasing
autonomy of frontier agents: as agents transition from executing single predefined actions to planning long-
horizon, multi-step workflows across multiple environments, the space of possible CI violations expands
combinatorially. Furthermore, self-evolving agents may change their tools or the way they summarize and
manage their context window (Kontonis et al., 2026), making it hard to predict and measure failures.

     Open Problem 10.7: Evaluations of agents in evolving environments.

     Finally, we lack benchmarks that test evolving environments and how agents will notice and adapt to
     these changes, such as their ability to proactively revisit and amend past data-sharing decisions when
     the context shifts. Changes in norms and other CI parameters might trigger agents to revisit previous
     decisions and amend them where possible. For example, changes in the political climate, company
     policies, and positioning might change the appropriateness of previous data sharing decisions for certain
     groups of users. Persistent agents that manage user data proactively need to monitor such changes
     and trigger actions (e.g., delete user profiles, execute right-to-be-forgotten requests, opt out of certain
     data-sharing practices) to ensure that the information they have previously shared on behalf of users
     remains appropriate in the new context.

Benchmarks for evaluating these kinds of scenarios are currently non-existent but are critical for testing the
ability of proactive data management agents to adapt. As concrete examples, these benchmarks could cover
scenarios like (1) changes in transmission principles where a company that previously received information
from the user changes its data-sharing policy (e.g., due to a new agreement, a new government policy, or
an acquisition); or (2) changes in the recipient where a change in a government’s political leaning changes
the perception of risk of sharing certain types of information (e.g., political preferences, sexual orientation,
financial situation) with government bodies.


11      Towards Trustworthy Multi-Agent Ecosystems

Thus far, we have considered the privacy and security of a single agent. However, the contextual lens could ex-
tend uniquely to multi-agent systems, in which agents, ideally, would protect user data and act appropriately
while interacting with each other.8 In multi-user cases—where agents represent different users—collaborative
tasks (e.g., calendar scheduling) might benefit from sharing data between agents, while requiring guardrails
to prevent data sharing of, e.g., doctor appointments, which could violate contextual norms. In cases where
information from one agent flows through agent delegation chains and graphs, this creates compositional
risks and requires agents to establish and propagate trust among themselves. Furthermore, evaluation of
multi-agent systems requires population-level simulation.
  8 See also the open security challenges in multi-agent systems in   de Witt et al. (2025), which presents a taxonomy of attack
vectors and a broader agenda.




                                                              65
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                               ↑ Table of Contents


11.1   Establishing and following shared contextual norms

   Open Problem 11.1: Establishing shared contextual norms.

   In open agentic ecosystems, agents interact with unfamiliar parties. In order for a participating agent
   to decide whether any information flow (or interactive behavior) is appropriate, the two parties would
   first need to settle the contextual parameters of the exchange: e.g., for information flow, what roles
   they occupy, which information types are in scope, which transmission principles apply, and which
   task language they will use. Given this definition of the behavior, one approach is to ensure that an
   agent would evaluate whether the behavior adheres to agreed-upon, shared contextual norms.


Shared norms might be selected from a shared source of truth, whose construction and governance are
themselves open challenges (§12), or established dynamically through negotiation. One can think of the
negotiation as a contextual handshake to establish agreed upon norms before proceeding with the task. If
the negotiation succeeds, its outcome could be useful beyond the immediate exchange, as the negotiated
transmission principles could be propagated to other agents along the delegation chain or graph. Whether
those agents actually comply with the negotiated norms, however, remains a separate (verification) problem;
we discuss attestation-based approaches in §11.3. What should such a negotiation look like? Perhaps an
agent could negotiate both contextual parameters and norms robustly even if one party may be untrusted,
while protecting the negotiation process from adversarial input (similar to how policy generation is isolated
from untrusted system context in Tsai & Bagdasarian (2025) and Debenedetti et al. (2025)). It would be
valuable if future research addresses how negotiable aspects of context could be represented, and what could
happen when the two sides fail to converge, for example, by refusing the interaction, escalating to the user, or
involving a trusted mediator. Such mitigation mechanisms may themselves introduce new attack surfaces: an
adversarial counterparty could claim incompatibility to force a fallback to a compromised channel, creating
a semantic downgrade attack analogous to protocol stripping on the early web.

   Open Problem 11.2: Preventing collusion and drifting norms between agents.

   As AI agents interact repeatedly across tasks and communication channels, they may develop persis-
   tent coordination patterns, form coalitions, or collude in ways that advance shared objectives at the
   expense of other agents or users, as observed by Nakamura et al. (2026). Such behaviors may even
   emerge gradually and may not be apparent from any single interaction or agent in isolation. Similarly,
   repeated interactions may cause agents to develop, reinforce, and propagate collective biases that are
   not encoded in any individual agent and may drift away from the contextual norms originally governing
   the system (Ashery et al., 2025). These phenomena raise a fundamental compositional challenge: even
   when each agent appears locally “compliant,” the resulting collective behavior may violate system-
   level privacy and security directives. These challenges require re-evaluation and constant control over
   whether the whole multi-agent system adheres to contextual norms.


When interacting agents are jointly optimized for task performance, their communication may also drift
away from human-interpretable language toward machine-optimized encoding. This idea has been adopted in
proposals in which agents exchange embeddings or latent states, thus removing their transcripts entirely (Zou
et al., 2026). In such cases, what does auditing an information flow mean when the message is a tensor
with no canonical reading? As LLMs have been found to favor LLM-generated text over human-written
text (Laurito et al., 2025), agents built on the same base model share priors that may let them implicitly
coordinate (converging on the same salient choices) with nothing suspicious appearing in any transcript.
These settings raise several questions worth asking for the purpose of auditing information flows in multi-
agent systems. These may include: When must the inter-agent language remain human-readable? How can
we detect that the use of a protocol has semantically drifted from its initial intended definition? And how
can the effective diversity of a population of interacting agents be measured?



                                                      66
                                         SECTION 11: TOWARDS TRUSTWORTHY MULTI-AGENT ECOSYSTEMS


11.2   Challenges with agent delegation and composition

The emerging agentic ecosystem is no longer composed of isolated agents performing tasks in a vacuum.
Increasingly, personal AI assistants delegate sub-tasks to specialized service-provider agents, which in turn
invoke external APIs, query databases, or coordinate with further agents. For example, a user who asks
their personal assistant to book a vacation may trigger a chain of delegations: the assistant contacts a
flight-booking agent, which queries an airline API; a hotel agent, which accesses loyalty program data; and
an insurance agent, which requests health information. At each hop in this chain, CI norms governing
who may send what information to whom, and under what transmission principle, may differ. Further,
these delegation patterns may be broadcast to multiple service-provider agents simultaneously, soliciting
competing quotes from insurance providers or coordinating across a user’s employer, healthcare provider,
and financial advisor in parallel, or negotiating on behalf of multiple household members with overlapping
but distinct privacy preferences. Each of these concurrent interactions operates under a different trust level
and trust architecture: the user may trust their employer’s HR agent with salary information but not with
medical history, while the reverse may hold for a healthcare agent.

   Open Problem 11.3: Preserving constraints during delegation or parallel execution.

   Many tasks require agents to pass control to another agent or to execute actions concurrently. In these
   scenarios, the overarching multi-agent system (e.g., the assistant) must manage a portfolio of active
   trust relationships in real time, making context-dependent disclosure decisions across stakeholders
   who may themselves share information laterally. The multi-agent system, thus, must also properly
   propagate consent and privileges during delegation. But how and when to achieve this safely is an
   open research problem.


This challenge becomes particularly acute in open agentic ecosystems, where the set of downstream agents
may not be known in advance.
Open Marketplaces. As agentic ecosystems mature toward open marketplaces where agents dynamically
discover and contract with previously unknown service agents, there is a need to understand how and when
agents should trust each other. Indeed, in these new ecosystems, the trust topology can become multi-hop,
multi-party, and even adversarial. Thus, we argue that the agents must reason about information flows
across a graph of heterogeneous agents with unverified capabilities, competing incentives, and potentially
conflicting normative obligations, all while preserving the user’s expectations for Contextual Integrity. The
adoption of the CI framework for LLM agents, currently operationalized for dyadic information flows between
identifiable actors, must account for these transitive, parallel, and dynamically evolving delegation topologies
where transmission principles may be altered as information traverses trust boundaries.
These dynamic delegation topologies raise a more specific question: how should the user’s original constraints
change, if at all, as information and authority move across successive trust boundaries? There is the further
question of whether it will be possible to mark agentic systems as acting in capacities, or roles, that make
sense for respective social contexts.
Trust Attenuation and Consent Propagation. When tasks are delegated through chains of agents, the
user’s original intent and data constraints often exist only in the first agent’s context window; each subsequent
delegation can cause privilege escalation, system context leakage, or loss of the original constraints. Moreover,
agentic systems may interact at runtime with newly discovered tools and peer agents whose trustworthiness
has not yet been established. This may require verifying each new counterpart through attestation, raising
the question of whether such checks can be performed efficiently at runtime.
More generally, when a user shares data with their personal agent, it may be unclear whether and under
what conditions that data can be transferred to downstream agents. CI transmission principles can express
such conditions (including reciprocity, need-to-know disclosure, and legal compulsion), but enforcing them
across multiple delegation hops remains challenging. A formal model of trust attenuation should specify how
these constraints are preserved or modified as information crosses each trust boundary, and how compliance
can be enforced and audited across the ecosystem. Here, too, the issue may not merely be attenuation as



                                                       67
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                             ↑ Table of Contents


distance from the initial prompt increases, but also how to retain a sense of the roles or capacities in which
mediating agents are acting.


   Open Problem 11.4: Orchestrating large agentic swarms and enforcing normative behavior.

   As the number of agents spawned to complete tasks increases to hundreds or even thousands and form
   swarms, we need efficient methods that allow us to monitor and audit each agent to ensure that: (1) it
   complies with contextual norms, and (2) a swarm of agents collectively does not violate these norms.



This challenge exposes a gap in current evaluation methodologies. Current benchmarks typically measure
disclosure in a single interaction according to CI parameters and the user’s consent or authorization. They
do not evaluate how the scope of that authorization should evolve as tasks are delegated to other agents.
Future benchmarks should therefore evaluate whether swarms of agents correctly enforce scope restrictions
as tasks are delegated, and whether consent granted at hop n is inappropriately assumed at hop n + k. Per
above, benchmarks might need to be established that take account of the roles assumed by the myriad of
agents in play.
Delegating Across Heterogeneous Normative Contexts. In real-world delegation chains, different
agents may operate under fundamentally different normative regimes. A user’s personal assistant may be
governed by EU data protection norms (GDPR), while a downstream service-provider agent operates under
US sectoral regulations (HIPAA, FERPA), and a third agent follows corporate-internal policies. Data that
may be shared under one regime’s transmission principle may be prohibited under another’s. While the
contextual handshake described in §11.1 can help resolve such conflicts for a single pairwise interaction,
delegation chains introduce a further challenge: CI norms may conflict across multiple boundaries simulta-
neously, and pairwise resolution at each hop does not guarantee end-to-end coherence. The identification of
roles might here, too, help mitigate challenges.
Benchmarks that model these cross-jurisdictional and cross-institutional normative conflicts would be useful,
in order to be able to evaluate whether agents can detect, flag, and appropriately resolve situations where
delegation would require violating one set of norms to satisfy another.


   Open Problem 11.5: Ensuring composed behaviors across agents uphold privacy and security.

   Even when each individual delegation step is locally appropriate, their composition may create a pri-
   vacy or security concern. Individually appropriate information flows or behaviors can, when aggregated
   across multiple agents, reveal sensitive information or result in harm that no single agent disclosed
   or enacted (analogous to linkage attacks for re-identification (Powar & Beresford, 2023) or the ac-
   cumulation of privacy risks from multiple differentially private queries on the same dataset (Kairouz
   et al., 2015; Abadi et al., 2016)). How do we reason about and prevent such compositional privacy or
   security harms?



For example, an assistant that shares a user’s dietary restrictions with a restaurant agent and shares the
user’s medication schedule with a pharmacy agent has made two individually defensible disclosures, but a
third party observing both flows can infer a medical diagnosis.
Current benchmarks largely evaluate atomic privacy properties (did this agent leak this datum?), but lack
the infrastructure and agentic use cases needed to trace information provenance across agent boundaries
and assess composite privacy properties. Developing metrics and evaluation frameworks for cross-boundary
aggregation risk, and finding suitable parallels for evaluating composition harms of non-information-sharing
behaviors, can help measure (and prevent) the aggregate harm of multi-agent systems.



                                                     68
                                         SECTION 11: TOWARDS TRUSTWORTHY MULTI-AGENT ECOSYSTEMS


11.3   Secure and trusted agent interactions

   Open Problem 11.6: Developing secure and robust multi-agent communication protocols.

   Securing multi-agent ecosystems requires trusted and secure communication channels between agents;
   done correctly, these channels can also enable robust negotiations (e.g., for shared norms) between
   agents.


Emerging interoperability protocols such as MCP (Anthropic, 2024) and A2A (Google, 2025) standardize
transport, discovery, and identity, but the payload they carry is free-form natural language (Ehtesham et al.,
2025), and they do not enforce policy negotiation or compliance.
From a security standpoint, unstructured language between agents may expose them to attacks, provide the
capacity for inappropriate disclosure (Gupta et al., 2026), and even result in covert coordination (Nakamura
et al., 2026). It further minimizes auditability, since the semantics of any message are only loosely constrained
by its syntax. Many of the emergent risks discussed in this paper (e.g., prompt injection through peer
messages, persuasion-driven disclosure, and collusion) arise from properties of the communication layer in
addition to the models themselves. It could be helpful to establish trust through other mechanisms, such as
communication channel design and endpoint verification.
Recent work demonstrates that communication protocol design can enable negotiation without trusting
either party. Firewalled agentic networks (Abdelnabi et al., 2026) project inter-agent communication onto
closed, task-scoped protocols mined from previous benign interactions. The communication channel itself
encodes contextual transmission principles: what the protocol cannot express, it also cannot leak or actuate.
The Agora meta-protocol (Marro et al., 2024) shows that heterogeneous LLM agents can autonomously
negotiate structured protocol documents and converge from natural language to efficient machine-verifiable
routines (although the negotiation there is driven purely by cost, with no privacy or security semantics
attached). These results suggest a largely unexplored design space between rigid, human-authored APIs
and unconstrained natural language, in which the expressiveness of the inter-agent language is a tunable
parameter.

   Open Problem 11.7: Building trust across agent boundaries with attestation.

   Agents operate on private user data, interact with third-party tools, and collaborate with other agents
   across organizational boundaries. However, each agent interaction requires trust between various
   parties because each agent interaction crosses a trust boundary: the user trusts their agent, but does
   the agent’s tool provider handle data appropriately? Does the peer agent enforce the same security
   standards? And does the infrastructure operator have access to the data in transit? Can agents operate
   on secure infrastructure to build trust between themselves and interacting parties via attestation?


Confidential Computing is an established approach for providing trust in distributed systems (Confidential
Computing Consortium, 2022; Forough et al., 2026). Its core primitive is the Trusted Execution Environment
(TEE): a hardware-enforced execution environment that guarantees the confidentiality and integrity of code
and data, even against privileged software such as the operating system or a hypervisor. We note that
hardware attacks (Seto et al., 2025; Chuang et al., 2026) and side-channel attacks (Hornetz et al., 2026)
against specific TEE implementations remain an active concern, but we assume for the purpose of this
discussion that TEEs will provide their claimed security properties as they are adopted across industry and
mature.
TEEs enable remote attestation: a mechanism by which a remote party can cryptographically verify what
code is running inside the TEE, using a chain of trust rooted in the CPU manufacturer’s hardware. Combined
with binary transparency (Binary Transparency, 2021) (public, append-only logs of all binaries deployed
inside TEEs) and reproducible builds, this may allow users to verify that the software they interact with
matches publicly auditable, reviewed source code (Kocaoğullar et al., 2024). For traditional software, this is
sufficient: the security properties can be established by reviewing the attested code.



                                                       69
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                                ↑ Table of Contents


Agents break this assumption. The literature on the subject has indicated that an agent’s behavior is
not determined by its code alone: it emerges from nondeterministic model inference over an unpredictable
context (Yao et al., 2022; Xi et al., 2025), and the same attested binary can take arbitrarily different
actions depending on its inputs. A perfectly attested TEE running a correct agent binary can still execute
harmful actions, especially if it is influenced by an adversarial prompt (Greshake et al., 2023; Liu et al.,
2024b; Triedman et al., 2025; Abdelnabi & Bagdasarian, 2026). How can we extend the notion of remote
attestation to an agent’s behavior? And how can we define what “behavioral attestation” even means for a
system whose behavior depends on unpredictable, adversarially controllable inputs?
Even if we cannot fully attest an agent’s behavior, recent work has explored attesting the security mechanisms
that govern it (Jin et al., 2026), the agent’s execution environment (Bodea et al., 2025), or the results of
model safety evaluations (Schnabl et al., 2025; Duddu et al., 2025). Deterministic components, such as the
policy engine binary and its configuration, can be attested using standard techniques. This matters because
when a user delegates credentials to an agent, they need assurance that these mechanisms are actually
mediating every action, not just present in the codebase.
However, in many agent systems the guardrail itself relies on model inference: prompt injection classi-
fiers (Inan et al., 2023; Zeng et al., 2024), safety filters (Rebedea et al., 2023; Han et al., 2024), and policy
generators (Tsai & Bagdasarian, 2025; Shi et al., 2026) are all LLM-based. The policies they produce are
generated at runtime in response to context, so they are not a part of the pre-audited binary. Can we attest
the policies a generator produces, not just the generator binary? And what security properties are both
meaningful for nondeterministic agents and feasible to verify via attestation?
Different agents may rely on completely different security architectures (TEEs, software-only guardrails, or
no verifiable guarantees at all), and there is currently no common vocabulary for comparing or composing
these heterogeneous trust levels (Forough et al., 2026). Can zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) be used to thread
the needle between one agent’s policies and another’s requirements by providing the information needed by
the latter while protecting side-information to adhere to the former?
Deploying even a handful of agents adhering to contextual privacy and security can open new opportunities
for multi-agent systems. These models could become trusted third parties that facilitate complex task
creation not feasible before (Shumailov et al., 2025; Mahmud et al., 2026): for example, as done in the
real world, mutually distrusting agents could collaborate by sharing their data with a trusted analyzing
agent, thus hiding the data of any individual untrusted agent from another. Further progress on trusted
infrastructure could significantly improve the feasibility of multi-agent workflows.

   Open Problem 11.8: Balancing security and utility in multi-agent communications.

   Free communication between agents allows an adversarial agent to insert prompt injections and hijack
   the control flow of participating agents (Triedman et al., 2025). However, approaches to constrain
   this communication and protect control flow restrict the collective utility of the multi-agent system
   in various ways.

   Recent work by Jacob et al. (2025) proposes to type-check inputs sent to an agent, thus preventing
   attacks that add indirect instructions. In multi-agent settings, Abdelnabi et al. (2026) use a series of
   LLM filters to isolate harmful content between agents, and Marro et al. (2024) use fixed communica-
   tion messages for repeated conversations. Each option has its own challenges. Fixing communication
   patterns mitigates attacks but risks being too rigid when agent capabilities change quickly. On the
   other hand, dynamically adapting the protocols to changes in communications exposes them to adver-
   sarial manipulations. Therefore, finding the right mechanisms to protect the agents without sacrificing
   utility remains an open problem.


How Much Expressiveness Does a Task Need? Constraining the language trades adaptability for
security, but we currently have no way to quantify this trade-off. Given a task, what is the minimal language
sufficient to complete it, and how do the injection surface, the leakage capacity, and the covert channel
capacity grow as the language becomes more expressive than that minimum? Are there tasks whose minimal



                                                       70
                                        SECTION 11: TOWARDS TRUSTWORTHY MULTI-AGENT ECOSYSTEMS


sufficient language is already highly vulnerable in these dimensions, and if so, can they be meaningfully
broken down into more constrained sub-tasks? A closed protocol bounds the overt channel, but it also
pushes adversaries toward behavioral side channels: timing, message ordering, or the choice of protocol
fields.
Side-Channel and Behavioral Leakage. In multi-agent ecosystems, the attack surface for such side
channels multiplies. Agents may leak information not through explicit message content, but through observ-
able behavioral patterns (Zhang et al., 2025d): the timing and ordering of tool calls, the selection of which
downstream agent to delegate to, or shared state artifacts. An adversarial agent can infer sensitive informa-
tion about a user by observing which agents their assistant contacts, when, and in what order, even if the
message content itself is appropriately filtered. No current benchmark measures these implicit information
flows.

11.4   Scalable environments for multi-agent evaluation and training

Evaluating whether multi-agent systems respect Contextual Integrity is hard to do in the wild: the relevant
behaviors surface only across long, branching interactions among several agents and their users, and real
deployments give little control over the contexts in which information flows and agents act. A growing
line of work instead studies agent behavior inside simulated social environments, where scenarios, roles, and
information-sharing norms can be specified and replayed at scale. Sotopia (Zhou et al., 2024) offers an
open-ended environment in which language agents role-play rich social scenarios and are scored against ex-
plicit social goals, making emergent multi-agent behavior reproducibly measurable. Subsequent frameworks
adopted this paradigm to further investigate information flow in a multi-agent setting (Yashwanth et al.,
2026). Haicosystem (Zhou et al., 2025) extends this to safety, sandboxing human–AI interactions with
simulated tools and adversarial users so that operational, societal, and legal risks can be elicited and graded
across thousands of runs.
A complementary direction is to use formal multi-agent coordination models, such as Distributed Constraint
Optimization Problems (DCOPs), as scalable and controllable evaluation environments (Fioretto et al.,
2018). DCOPs explicitly represent the agents, their local variables and constraints, and the interaction
graph, making it possible to systematically vary the number of agents, network topology, local objectives,
and communication structure. Recently, researchers have demonstrated this approach for LLM multi-agent
systems by grounding cooperation in a DCOP and auditing collusive behavior under different coalition
objectives, persuasion strategies, and network topologies (Nakamura et al., 2026). More broadly, such
formal environments could be extended to encode CI-relevant roles, information constraints, and transmission
principles, providing controlled ground truth for evaluating various safety issues and privacy harms at scale.

   Open Problem 11.9: Building realistic multi-agent environments for Contextual Integrity.

   How can we simulate realistic agent interactions (where, e.g., agents may have access to a user’s data
   or act on behalf of a business) in order to surface complex information flows and contextual norms,
   and enable both evaluation and training of contextually private and secure LLMs?


Such simulated and formally specified environments provide a natural substrate for studying Contextual In-
tegrity at the population level: appropriate and inappropriate information flows can be encoded in scenarios
or problem instances, and tendencies to leak or over-share can be probed systematically, extending Contex-
tual Integrity benchmarks built for single models (Tamber et al., 2026) to the multi-agent regime (Gupta
et al., 2026). Scalable, controllable environments of this kind could offer just what is needed for measuring
Contextual Integrity in multi-agent settings, where the space of contexts and inter-agent information flows
is far too large to audit manually. Formal environments additionally could make it possible to vary these
factors systematically and to evaluate agent behavior against known system-level objectives and constraints.
Addressing the challenges described above would greatly benefit from benchmarks that move beyond single-
agent, single-flow evaluation toward system-level privacy assessment. This includes constructing multi-agent
evaluation environments where information provenance can be traced end-to-end across delegation bound-



                                                      71
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                               ↑ Table of Contents


aries; defining compositional privacy metrics that quantify aggregate leakage across individually compliant
flows; and red-teaming methodologies specifically designed for multi-agent ecosystems, including adversarial
agents that exploit delegation protocols to reconstruct user profiles through long-term observation of inter-
agent communication patterns. These benchmarks would measure both verbatim factual disclosures and
how adversarial agents may infer attributes via behavioral leakage.


12     Governance of Agentic Privacy and Security

To meet the serious and unprecedented privacy and security threats posed by AI agents, this manuscript
has proposed a contextual approach and has adopted the theory of Contextual Integrity as the underlying
framework. It has outlined the conceptual framework and has laid the foundations and identified the open
problems for operationalizing and enforcing it. To promote the chances of real-world, practical adoption of
contextual approaches to privacy and security, sound governance is essential. Ideally, the collaborative efforts
of actors in governmental, corporate, academic, public interest, and technical standards settings would yield
smart, well-calibrated societal policies (Nissenbaum, 2011). Proposing specific policies is beyond the scope
of this manuscript. Instead, this section identifies the open governance and policy problems that must be
addressed to enable contextual privacy and security.
We organize this around three questions. First, what preconditions would create an environment conducive
to contextual privacy and security? Second, how should the CI norms relevant to agent deployment and
increased use of agents be sourced, and what conflicting preferences must be balanced for a norm to settle
(§12.1)? Third, how are norms enforced, and who are the relevant enforcement actors (§12.2)?

     Open Problem 12.1: Achieving environments conducive to contextual privacy and security.

     Achieving an environment conducive to contextual privacy and security requires progress on several
     interdependent aims:
       1. How can we incentivize developers of agentic systems to learn and adhere to existing contextual
          norms, as opposed to treating privacy and security as a compliance afterthought?
       2. How should the concept of a “reasonable expectation of privacy,” historically developed around
          the relationship between governmental entities and private actors, be extended to the far wider
          range of relationships agents mediate?
       3. How can processes and systems be made flexible enough to absorb contextual wisdom about
          ends, purposes, and values from experts embedded in the relevant social domains, and to identify
          systematic relationships between data flows or state changes and those contextual ends?
       4. What are workable methods for two hard cases: articulating contextual roles for novel actors
          such as tech companies and agents themselves, and adjudicating actions that serve one context
          while disserving another?


The preconditions above identify what an environment conducive to contextual privacy and security requires.
Before turning to what the substantive norms governing agentic information flows should be, we address a
prior governance-design question: How should responsibility be distributed across the agentic ecosystem so
that these preconditions can actually take hold? One core logic to follow is that obligations ought to be
assigned to actors in line with their capability, vantage point, and exposure to manage the risk. This
redistributes harms and benefits in a way that enables users to trust and adopt a technology, while the
responsible party retains flexibility in how it meets the obligation.
There are several historical examples of how similar questions have been addressed. For instance, in the
early days of credit cards, a major barrier to public uptake was consumer fear of uncapped liability related
to credit card fraud. The Fair Credit Billing Act (1974) (Littlefield, 1975) capped consumer exposure at
50 USD and shifted the residual cost of fraud to card issuers. The key purpose was to build the public
trust necessary for mass adoption. Additionally, because issuers now bore the cost, they invested heavily in



                                                      72
                                                  SECTION 12: GOVERNANCE OF AGENTIC PRIVACY AND SECURITY


fraud-detection and prevention technologies without legislation prescribing what those technologies should
be.
Another example comes from the Americans with Disabilities Act (1990) (Burgdorf Jr., 1991), where building
codes set performance-level floors (e.g., a structure must withstand a given load, a public space must be
accessible) but did not prescribe the use of specific materials or layouts. The effect is to enable broad public
participation and access by assigning the burden of meeting the standard to the entity that controls the
relevant design decisions, not to the individuals who must rely on the built environment (Burgdorf Jr., 1991;
Murdoch & Jawer, 1992).
In both cases, concentrating responsibility on capable actors generated investment, trust, and adoption
without mandating the technical form of the solution. A comparable logic could guide agentic governance.
We broadly conceptualize agentic governance as the rules, practices, processes, and technological tools that
inform the development and deployment of agents. A CI-specific understanding further focuses on how these
rules govern and incentivize appropriate flows of information and behaviors within the agentic ecosystem.
With the preconditions and the distribution of responsibility in place, the following sections discuss what
the norms should be, and how these norms should be enforced.

12.1    What should the norms be?

Norms, in the CI framework, reflect a settled accommodation of contextual values, ends, and purposes. They
express what a given community has come to regard as appropriate flows of information and behaviors within
a particular social domain. This leads to two important open questions:

   Open Problem 12.2: Determining CI norms.

   First, what should be the practices and processes for sourcing the set of norms that agents should
   abide by? Second, how do we devise governance mechanisms for formally updating norms that may
   have become suboptimal in supporting contextual values, ends, and purposes?

We see at least three approaches to the first question:
The first is a baseline of standards established top-down by companies and governments. Just as building
codes and accessibility mandates set performance-level floors for the built environment (see above), platform
providers and regulators can articulate minimum requirements for how agentic systems handle the flow of
information or actuation of state changes within a given context. Such baselines also need to address a
transparency problem specific to agentic systems. When an AI agent acts on behalf of a user, the vendor
may or may not be a recipient of the third party’s data, depending on how the system is built (a vendor
running an agent inside a trusted execution environment or on the user’s device need not see the data at
all). The third party generally cannot tell which is the case.
The second route is empirical. There is a need for systematic efforts to discover existing contextual norms
as they actually operate in practice and the real world. What do people in specific social domains already
expect, rely on, and regard as appropriate? One key point here is that this is not arbitrary social science
work. In order to be actionable and useful for governance, such empirical research efforts need to be forward-
looking, aligned with the technologies on the horizon, and focused on the settings that agentic systems are
about to enter. One concrete step to collect relevant insights in a centralized and dynamically expanding
place would be the development of a public norm repository. Such a norm repository would take the form of
a structured catalog of context-specific informational norms that agentic systems could query and respect.
Such a resource could also serve as shared infrastructure for both developers seeking to build norm-aware
systems and regulators seeking to ground policy in empirical reality.9
This is undoubtedly as much an empirical question as a doctrinal one. CI’s approach to norm-sourcing does
something structurally similar to the Fourth Amendment’s “reasonable expectation of privacy” test by asking
  9 Once again, we can learn from historical precedent. Specifically, the legal system already performs a version of this sourcing

work. The Fourth Amendment’s “reasonable expectation of privacy” test, established in Katz v. United States (1967) (Supreme
Court of the United States, 1967; Winn, 2009), asks what expectations society legitimizes as reasonable.




                                                               73
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                              ↑ Table of Contents


what information flows people regard as appropriate within a given context. The difference is one of scope.
The reasonable-expectation test was historically developed around the relationship between governmental
entities and private actors. As noted in our preconditions, this thinking needs to be extended to the far wider
range of relationships that agents mediate, ranging from relationships between users and service providers,
to those between users and third parties and between the multiple principals whose interests converge in a
single agentic interaction (Winn, 2009; Supreme Court of the United States, 1967; Nissenbaum, 2009).
A third option is to develop AI systems capable of performing CI’s decision heuristic process for identifying
legitimate norms given contextual details. Rather than taking norms as either stipulated or empirically
given, AI agents would make carefully contextualized decisions on the fly about what norms—entrenched
or otherwise—will support ends, purposes, and values (see Benthall & Cummings (2026)). AI agents would
consider fixed or architectural aspects of their system context, as well as observations of user behavior (see
Criado & Such (2015)). In some cases, AI agents may need to communicate with each other to establish
norms dynamically. This capability may prove especially critical when agentic AI systems serving different
principals interact in a rapidly changing digital environment. In these settings, AI agents cannot wait
for norms to become settled institutional or empirical facts; they may need to anticipate what users or
policymakers will decide is legitimate.
Even where norms have been identified through any of the above routes, a further open problem arises:

   Open Problem 12.3: Institutional and legal governance—resolving conflicting norms.

   Autonomous agents are designed to move seamlessly across cultural, geographic, and organizational
   boundaries. The result is a highly fragmented compliance landscape. An agent that books travel,
   manages health records, and negotiates contracts may, in a single session, be subject to the data
   protection regimes of multiple jurisdictions with divergent and sometimes conflicting requirements.
   For developers of agentic systems, this is not an edge case but a routine feature of how agents operate.


One approach worth exploring is the systematic mapping of localized regulatory requirements and privacy
and security norms. Such a mapping could serve as a prerequisite for dynamic legal and cultural alignment,
allowing an agent to adapt its information-handling practices and behaviors to the jurisdiction and context
in which it is operating at any given moment. Another direction might pursue training agents to navigate
overlapping jurisdictions by adopting conflict-of-laws principles from private international law (Kohl, 2007;
Berman, 2012; Svantesson, 2017).

12.2   How do we verify and provide accountability for norms enforcement?

The previous section addressed what the norms governing agentic information flows and behaviors should
be, and where they come from. Earlier sections of this manuscript (e.g., §6) discussed how agents themselves
can be designed to operationalize and self-enforce contextual norms at runtime. Governance poses an equally
important and distinct challenge in practice:

   Open Problem 12.4: Incentivizing and verifying normative compliance.

   How do we scalably verify that agents did, in fact, follow contextual norms, and hold the relevant
   actors accountable should they not?


Contextual norms are not enforced by any single authority. Different social forces maintain them, and
each brings its own mechanisms. Courts enforce norms through adjudication and liability. Regulators
enforce through statutory compliance and penalties. Companies enforce through internal policy and platform
governance. Professional bodies, including the domain experts discussed above, enforce through standards
and sanctions. In practice, these forces operate together, and any given context’s norms are typically
sustained by some combination of them. We organize the discussion around two broad categories: (1)
institutional and legal governance, and (2) governance through technology.



                                                      74
                                          SECTION 12: GOVERNANCE OF AGENTIC PRIVACY AND SECURITY


Institutional and legal mechanisms are necessary but not sufficient. Agentic systems act at a speed and
level of autonomy that outpaces traditional ex-post regulatory intervention. Moreover, effective enforcement
often requires reporting of personal information, which is private, to some investigatory or enforcing party.
This information flow is itself subject to information norms that can be violated. Consider, for example, the
ways that improper gathering of evidence without a warrant can lead to a mistrial in a court of law. By the
time a court adjudicates or a regulator investigates, the information flow has already occurred and the harm
may be irreversible. Enforcement therefore cannot rely entirely on after-the-fact correction. It must also be
built into the systems themselves.
A norm repository can be one component of this accountability infrastructure. Beyond a resource that
agents query at runtime, it provides a benchmark against which their information-handling decisions can be
audited after the fact. However, investigatory and enforcement decisions may happen at machine speed and
in novel scenarios, suggesting a need for AI agents to also be capable of on-the-fly evaluation of CI norms
when static guidance is incomplete.
Formal models of context and normative reasoning not only enable runtime tuning of agents’ behavior, but
they also create a record of which norms were applied to a given flow, and for what reason. This inspectable
record moves governance through technology beyond a design technique and toward an accountability mech-
anism. It renders an agent’s information-handling decisions auditable, both individually and in cross-agent
interactions where responsibility is otherwise hard to trace, making it possible to determine after the fact
which norms were applied, by whom, and whether they were appropriate. This would mean that both en-
forcement and production of an auditable record of compliance occur at the moment the agent acts, rather
than being reconstructed months later through a regulatory investigation. Legal and regulatory mechanisms
are essential for setting the norms themselves and for accountability when systems fail.




                                                     75
PART III: DEPLOYMENT AND ECOSYSTEM PERSPECTIVES                                           ↑ Table of Contents


13    Conclusion

This manuscript outlined privacy and security research challenges that arise with highly capable and au-
tonomous AI agents. We began by introducing and justifying a perspective on agentic privacy and security
through the lens of Contextual Integrity theory. We then highlighted open questions towards building
agents that act appropriately across agent component levels, including system-level enforcement abstrac-
tions, model-level capabilities, and user interaction strategies. Finally, we outlined problems that emerge
when deploying agents in complex environments such as evaluations, interacting in multi-agent settings, and
governance of behavioral norms. We hope that this manuscript inspires the broader research community
across academia, government, and industry to collaborate to develop the contextual foundations for a safe,
secure, and privacy-respecting agentic ecosystem.
