                                                 Appendix


A     Contextual Integrity Glossary of Key Terms

Below, we provide core definitions of contextual integrity developed by Nissenbaum and Shvartzshnaider.10

A.1     Context [social domains, social spheres]

“Contexts are structured social settings characterized by canonical activities, roles, relationships, power
structures, norms (or rules), and internal values (goals, ends, purposes)” (Nissenbaum, 2009, p. 132). These
may include healthcare, education, political life, commerce, social (family and friends), etc. They are
“abstract representations of social structures experienced in daily life” (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp. 134-135).
Social domains or social spheres are closely related concepts.

A.2     Norm

Behavior-guiding norms prescribe and proscribe actions and practices. A norm’s behavior-guiding power
may be directly prescriptive or it may be indirectly prescriptive, based on an overwhelming preponderance
of behavior of a particular kind. Norms may be explicit or implicit; they may vary in kind, e.g. ethical,
aesthetic, formal, informal. They may be standalone or elements of complex systems of rules, e.g., law,
professional code of conduct. Rules, Laws, Directives, etc., are related concepts (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp. 137-
140).

A.2.1    Contextual informational norm

(Also referred to as informational norm, context-relative informational norm, context-specific informational
norm, CI norm, privacy norm.)
CI norms prescribe and proscribe the flow of personal information from one party to another (Nissenbaum,
2009, pp. 140-147). Contextual informational norms are defined by five parameters: Sender, Recipient, Data
Subject, Transmission Principle, Information Type. Well-formed instances of informational norms include
values for all five parameters. The presence of CI norms may be established empirically. They may or may
not be legitimate depending on whether they effectively serve (or promote) contextual ends, purposes, and
values.

A.3     Personal information

As used in CI, this term simply means information about a person. The source is irrelevant, whether it is
the data subject, a third party, or inferred.

A.4     Contextual information flow

Flow of information from an actor to other actors. Well-formed descriptions of contextual information flows
must include values for all five parameters expressed in terms of contextual roles and informational ontologies.
 10 A full and up-to-date version of the glossary is available at https://privaci.info/glossary.




                                                         100
                                            SECTION A: CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY GLOSSARY OF KEY TERMS


A.5     CI parameter

This refers to the five parameters of CI norms: Actors (subject, sender, recipient), Attribute, Transmission
Principle.

A.5.1    Values for CI parameters

Instantiations of CI parameters referred to in terms of respective contextual ontologies of roles and informa-
tion types.

A.5.2    Actor

This term refers collectively to the parameters: sender, recipient, and data subject. Values for these param-
eters are given in terms of respective contextual role ontologies. Examples include: teachers, doctors, etc.
It may also refer to non-human parties, such as corporations, organizations, governments. Individuals (or
agents) are actors, in this sense, insofar as they are acting in capacities according to contextual ontologies.

A.5.3    Contextual role

Contexts are co-constituted by contextual role ontologies. A contextual role [capacity] involves fulfilling
certain functions, meeting particular expectations, and assuming certain responsibilities. There is no fixed
number of roles per context and the roles themselves may change over time, and may even disappear while
new ones emerge. Roles may be thickly detailed or quite scant.

A.5.4    Information (data) type

(Also referred to as data type, data field, or attribute.) This term points to what the information is about, its
semantic content, what the data or information refers to. Contexts are associated with distinctive information
(data) ontologies.

A.5.5    Transmission principle

A transmission principle (TP) is a flow-guiding principle. Commonly occurring TPs include: with consent,
reciprocally, with notice, in confidence, as required by law, with a warrant. TPs cut across contextual
ontologies.

A.6     Privacy expectation

In US law, “reasonable expectation of privacy” (Supreme Court of the United States, 1967) implies a
widespread or commonly held expectation. It has played a central role in legal arguments, supporting
restrictions on access to personal information. In CI the term connects this powerful legal doctrine to the
concept of a contextual informational norm (Selbst, 2013) which as a matter of fact, describes a commonly
held view on appropriate data flows.

A.7     Contextual integrity

Contextual integrity is preserved [respected, maintained] when justifiable contextual informational norms
are respected, OR when information flows promote [support] contextual ends, purposes, and values and do
not undermine contextual ends, purposes, and values (see CI heuristic in §A.8).

A.7.1    Contextual values

Including goals, ends, purposes, and values, these teleological elements define and co-constitute a context.
They are the rationale behind a context. Contextual functions, norms, and ontologies are oriented around
these elements. Activities and practices make sense insofar as they support and promote contextual ends,
purposes and values. Information norms are legitimate insofar as they promote contextual ends, purposes,



                                                      101
APPENDIX                                                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


and values. By implication, entrenched functions, norms, practices, and ontologies may be challenged by
alternatives that are shown to be more effective in supporting and promoting contextual ends, purposes, and
values (Nissenbaum, 2009, pp. 155-166).

A.7.2     Contextual functions, practices, activities

These are behaviors, activities, and practices that are typical or expected for a given context. They may
achieve this status through repetition, established as an aspect of the culture of a context, or because they
contribute to contextual functions, in turn instrumental to achieving higher order goals and values. [See, for
example, Conley et al. (2011).]

A.7.3     [Moral] legitimacy of informational norms

The presence of entrenched information-flow norms constitutes prima facie evidence of the legitimacy of
respective flows. To be morally justifiable [legitimate], the norm in question must be shown to perform well
on three layers of analysis: (1) strike an acceptable balance of wellbeing among all affected parties; (2) be
compatible with central ethical and political principles; and (3) promote contextual ends, purposes, and
values (Nissenbaum, 2009, Chapter 8).

A.7.4     Contextual ontologies

These refer to ontologies of contextual roles and information [data, attributes]. Although contextual ontolo-
gies are distinctive for each context, there are likely to be overlaps in roles and information categories among
different contexts (Nissenbaum, 2019).

A.8     The CI heuristic protocol

The CI heuristic (Nissenbaum, 2009, page 282) described below provides guidance on whether a given
information practice threatens or upholds privacy as CI by evaluating information flow patterns resulting from
the deployment of sociotechnical systems. The CI heuristic may also guide the developers of sociotechnical
systems who wish to assess design alternatives in light of their impacts on privacy as CI.

  • Describe information flows in terms of five parameters [the CI-tuple]: subject, sender, recipient, at-
    tribute, transmission principle (see Values for CI parameters, §A.5.1).
  • Identify a prevailing context, or contexts, e.g., healthcare, allowing that subcontexts, e.g., teaching
    hospital, might be nested within them (Nissenbaum, 2009, page 136).
  • Locate relevant contextual informational norms, if any, and note conformance or points of departure.
        • Case 1: Where conformance is achieved, appropriate flow has been maintained.
        • Case 2: Where flows are in breach of norms, an evaluator or designer concludes that a prima facie
          violation of contextual integrity has occurred. Presumption favors entrenched, pre-existing norms
          over divergent data flows.
        • Case 3: No norm exists covering observed data flows. Technology-enabled data flows may be suf-
          ficiently novel that existing norms are too distant for comparative purposes. Systems of contextual
          norms are likely to be “incomplete” in relation to all possible data flow instances.
  • When there is a breach (Case 2) or a lacuna (Case 3), the following assessments are indicated:
        • Evaluation I: Carefully assess the impacts of novel flows on those affected, viz. stakeholders.
          Typically, this could include all the parties that the CI-tuple identifies as actors, as well as others
          beyond these actors. (“It requires a study of how novel flows affect the interests of key affected
          parties: the benefits they enjoy, the costs and risks they suffer. These may include material costs
          and benefits as well as those less palpable, including shifts in relative power” (Nissenbaum, 2015).)
        • Evaluation II: Consider the impacts in terms of high-level moral principles, such as autonomy,
          justice, fairness, and liberty, as well as political principles concerning power distributions, social
          hierarchy, democracy, and so on.



                                                        102
                                                                                  SECTION B: DEFINITIONS OF CONTEXT



                           Social context (sphere)
                           Purposes, roles, norms (e.g., healthcare, education)

                              Physical context (situation)
                              Technical infrastructure, devices, environment

                                  System context
                                  Knowledge bases, pipelines, memory, tools

                                      Model context
                                      The context window: instructions,
                                      conversation, retrieved content,
                                      tool outputs, in-context memory




Figure 3: A schematic diagram of multiple meanings of context. A model may be embedded in a system,
each of which has context. It is deployed into a physical context. It may also operate in a social context,
which provisions norms that are salient to user privacy. Note that this is not a Venn diagram, but a depiction
of different nested components.


     • Evaluation III: Consider how the system or practices impinge on the values, ends, and purposes
       of the contexts in question.

The result of the protocol’s layered analysis (adapted from Nissenbaum (2009, pages 184-185)) may clearly
favor accepting or rejecting a particular system on the basis of novel and disruptive data flows. Ambiguous
outcomes, however, indicate a need for more data, further judgment and deliberation, or even a reversion to
the status quo.

B    Definitions of Context

Following discussion in Section 4.1.1, the model context is unstructured digital material, such as text, which
is available to the LLM as an input as it generates new output. The amount of information that the LLM
may consider is limited by its context window, and this can be a challenge to an LLM’s performance on
complex problems (Shojaee et al., 2025). Orchestration techniques may be used to manage the LLM’s actions,
changing the model context multiple times as the model “reasons” through a problem. This is sometimes
referred to as “multi-agent” reasoning (Hong et al., 2024; Wu et al., 2024b). These orchestration techniques
can be understood as a system-level solution to a model-level constraint.
In this paper, we also discuss system context, a component of an agentic system: its internally accessible
knowledge base. A reasoning agent may employ digital contextual information that goes beyond the model’s
context window but is nevertheless digitally provisioned, perhaps as unstructured data. Both senses of
context refer to the software system that implements the agent.
However, “context” can also refer to a system’s environment. In early work on “privacy in context” (Ackerman
et al., 2001), context is understood as the specific environment in which a system is deployed, inclusive of
the technical and physical details. A prototypical example technology in this setting would be a sensor and
display system for controlling heating and lighting conditions in an office building. Benthall et al. (2017) use
the term situation to denote the concrete, specific circumstances in which a system is deployed.
Contextual Integrity emphasizes social context, an established social field of activity with widely understood
purposes and norms. It is understood normatively and abstractly – in terms of goals and values – as opposed
to the descriptive specifics. Benthall et al. (2017) refer to this form of context as a sphere. This context
exists primarily outside of the software system and in the social environment. A prototypical technology
that interacts with such a context is an electronic medical records system; in that context the norms are



                                                           103
APPENDIX                                                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


often clear-cut and described by law. However, CI also theorizes how new social contexts can emerge and
adapt to changing circumstances. In all cases, CI locates the norms and significance of privacy in such a
social context.
From the perspective of CI, the challenge of agentic privacy is aligning system behavior with the norms and
purposes of the social context. One way to accomplish this is by adequately representing the social context
in the system context—so that the agent has available all the relevant information about the social context
it needs to act appropriately. This can be challenging, because social context sometimes is unfixed and
established dynamically in the interactions between a system and multiple other people (Dourish, 2004). In
other words, mere representation of norms as “policies” may be insufficient; privacy must be enacted through
agent behavior.


C     Contextual Integrity: Critiques, Expansions, and Open Problems

CI is a live theory of privacy with an active community of scholars who sharpen and develop the theory as
they apply it to new technologies and scenarios. In §4.1 we discussed the canonical theory of Contextual
Integrity, and reflected on the importance of consistency and standardization. In this section, we discuss
where scholars have critiqued and proposed extensions to CI. The understandable desire to adapt CI to new
technologies—such as agentic AI systems—is in tension with the need to standardize an ontology to make
evaluations meaningful. This makes the development of CI itself into an open research problem.


C.1    Expanding on transmission properties

In CI’s standard formulation, information flows are characterized by sender, receiver, subject, attribute, and
transmission principle. Some have argued that CI can be a more powerful and expressive theory if it develops
a richer taxonomy of the descriptive transmission properties of information flows (Benthall & Cummings,
2024).
Kumar et al. (2024) discuss the topic of information medium, such as face-to-face conversations, handwritten
notes, text messages, or public social media posts, and how to incorporate it into the CI taxonomy of
information flows. Kumar & Byrne (2022) suggest it should be an additional parameter. Here, we present
it as another way of elaborating the “transmission properties,” descriptive elements of the information flow.
One use of “transmission properties” is to adapt CI to the possibility of privacy-enhancing technologies
(PETs) such as differential privacy. Benthall & Cummings (2024) note that CI does not currently have
a native way of registering that an information flow is aggregated over many data subjects, queried into a
single statistic, and modified with random noise—typical steps in a differentially private disclosure of personal
information. “Transmission properties” bridge the explanatory gap and open the door to new methods that
use CI as a way of tuning the design of sociotechnical systems with PETs (Benthall & Cummings, 2026).


C.2    Clarifying the transmission principles

An information norm in CI determines the conditions under which an information flow is appropriate. These
conditions are referred to as the “transmission principle.” Though they carry the normative load of CI, there
is surprisingly little development of what transmission principles are in the original theory. Currently there
is no straightforward enumeration of transmission principles, such that one could structure a search over
possible norms.
Shvartzshnaider et al. (2022) have proposed a clarifying framework, GKC-CI, that combines CI with the
institutional grammar of Governing Knowledge Commons (GKC) (Frischmann et al., 2014; Sanfilippo et al.,
2018) to provide a more granular taxonomy of transmission principles. They consider four aspects:

    1. Aims. e.g. for use in personalized advertising. The anticipated use of the information by the receiver.
    2. Conditions. e.g. if given consent. An event that occurs prior to the transmission.



                                                      104
                    SECTION C: CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY: CRITIQUES, EXPANSIONS, AND OPEN PROBLEMS


  3. Modalities. e.g. can, might, will. Addressing whether the condition is sufficient or necessary for
     appropriateness.
  4. Consequences. e.g. if necessary to verify identity. A consequence of the transmission which must be
     taken into account.

These elements of transmission principles may find relevance when CI is applied to complex legal and social
domains. For example, the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) of the EU depends heavily on
“purpose binding” of information flows; this is not a first-order element of CI theory but can be understood
as a transmission principle that depends on the consistently maintained “aims” of information use as it flows
from actor to actor.

C.3   Modeling context

As transmission principles are developed in a deeper and more comprehensive way, it becomes apparent that
proper enforcement of information norms may require a great deal of information about the environment or
scenario. A norm might require that a receiver be committed to particular uses of the information, or be
unaware of the information already. The information sender may, in order to be compliant with norms, need
to model what the receiver already knows, what they need to know to fulfill the context’s purpose, what
they might infer by combining new information with prior knowledge, and what downstream consequences
disclosure could produce for the subject. For example, revealing to a colleague that a coworker “has been
dealing with some personal issues” is different from revealing a specific diagnosis, in part because the latter
enables the recipient to make inferences and take actions that the subject never intended to authorize.
This analysis reveals a longstanding critique of CI, which is that it presumes that information flows have
a stable meaning—designated by the “attribute” parameter—when in fact “data’s meaning has become a
moving target” (Horvitz & Mulligan, 2015) because its function depends on the other information it is
combined with. Contextual Integrity may be clinging to general, socially understood intuitions about the
conventional semantics of information, and trying to preserve those expectations, even though in reality, data
is flowing “up and down the food chain” of inference in real time (Nissenbaum, 2019). This is a mismatch
between theory and practice. Furthermore, some information norms depend on the granularity or specificity
of information, not just the broad category of information. For instance, sharing that a patient “has a chronic
condition” with an employer may be appropriate when requesting workplace accommodations, but sharing
that the patient “takes 20mg of Lexapro daily for Generalized Anxiety Disorder” violates the norm — not
because the information type changed, but because the granularity exceeded what the context required.
Some prior work has addressed the instability of data’s meaning by attempting to assert that the “attribute”
property should refer to the information provenance, not what it contributes to inference (Nissenbaum et al.,
2018). Benthall (2019) argues that this notion of information semantics should be replaced with a theory
of “situated information flow” that sees information flow as causal and embedded in a sociotechnical system
that determines what each actor can infer from data as it flows. This way of modeling sociotechnical systems
has been applied to the problem of CI-inspired methods for tuning differential privacy parameters (Benthall
& Cummings, 2026).
An open problem for CI is whether and to what extent detailed modeling of social context is necessary to
either determine legitimate contextual norms, or comply with them once they are understood. It is possible
that AI systems enacting CI norms may require models of the other party’s knowledge and inferential
capability. CI-compliant multi-agent interaction may require causal world modeling of the socio-technical
system and the capacities of the agents within it.

C.4   Further evaluation of technical change

CI can be seen as a normative theory of evaluating technical change. O’Neill (2022) proposes an extension
of CI to technology evaluation beyond privacy. This extension engages most with the sense of “entrenched
practices” in a context (including but not limited to information flows), and whether and how they relate to
CI’s teleological elements: individual ends, and shared goals and values which could relate to personalization.



                                                     105
APPENDIX                                                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


Furthermore, other attempts to generalize CI away from privacy may lead to greater synthesis with other
frameworks for institutional analysis and design (Ostrom, 2019; Frischmann et al., 2014; Shvartzshnaider
et al., 2022).

D     Review and Taxonomy of Contextual Integrity Benchmarks
A wave of recent datasets and benchmarks has either explicitly attempted to quantify whether LLMs and
agents can adhere to CI, or evaluated their utility in operationalizing some aspects of CI. We first describe the
attributes of each CI benchmark or dataset that we later use to analyze and compare them (§D.1, Table 1).
§D.2 then performs a comparative analysis of the various benchmarks across these attributes (Table 2).

D.1   Analysis framework

We propose the following features to help us analyze each benchmark:

• Contextualization: Evaluates what contextual information is provided within evaluation scenarios to
  make information types and other CI parameters (e.g., sender, receiver, information subject) contextual,
  in the sense that the same parameters may have different implications depending on the scenario’s context.
  If so, how richly contextualized is the evaluation scenario (e.g., does the context include a purpose for
  the information flow, multi-faceted personas, or actors with domain-specific roles)?
• Transmission principles: Evaluates the complexity of transmission principles covered by the bench-
  mark. Simple transmission principles are generally context-independent and/or do not influence or specify
  further data-sharing constraints. For example, the information is appropriate in the given context or is
  necessary to achieve the specified scenario purpose. On the other hand, complex transmission principles
  are context-specific and/or specify special conditions placed on the present or subsequent transmissions.
  For example, data is shared under an NDA or patient-doctor or client-attorney confidentiality; data is
  compelled to be shared under a subpoena or a domain-specific regulation; data is part of a commercial
  exchange; or some form of reciprocity is involved.
• Ambiguity: Captures whether the information flows in the benchmark scenarios may be appropriate
  in some contexts and inappropriate in others, and whether such ambiguity is explicitly represented in
  the benchmark design (e.g., by providing multiple scenarios involving the same information flow under
  different contexts), or instead arises implicitly.
• Norms: Analyzes the sources of informational expectations covered by the benchmark. Typical sources
  include legal norms (e.g., regulations, case law), societal norms (e.g., social expectations, cultural norms),
  and organizational norms (e.g., company policies, industry standards).
• Realism: Measures how closely benchmark data reflects real-world distributions and scenarios. Data
  can range from purely synthetic data, to synthetic data grounded in real relevant datasets or (simplified)
  real-world data.
• Annotation: Characterizes how the annotations associated with each scenario in the benchmark were
  obtained. Annotations typically include information about what is appropriate to reveal in a scenario
  and what is necessary to share in order to achieve the scenario’s purpose. The annotations can be fully
  synthetic (e.g., generated by a language model, sampled from a distribution) or obtained from human
  raters, who in turn can be expert or non-expert depending on their knowledge of the domain and/or the
  CI framework.
• Explanation: Captures whether annotations provide only target outputs/labels or also include explicit
  justifications for what is appropriate or necessary in a scenario, which can be evaluated against agent or
  model reasoning.
• Metrics: Describes what evaluation signals are considered when assessing benchmark performance (e.g.,
  leakage and utility) and what types of scoring rules are used to compute such metrics (e.g., deterministic,
  heuristic, or judge-based methods). Deterministic scoring methods are those that can be computed with
  a fixed algorithmic procedure. Heuristic scoring methods involve some form of approximation or rule-
  of-thumb that may not be strictly deterministic. Judge-based scoring methods involve the use of an



                                                      106
                        SECTION D: REVIEW AND TAXONOMY OF CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY BENCHMARKS


   LLM-as-a-judge to compare model outputs against reference annotations and assign scores based on the
   quality of the match, which may involve some degree of subjectivity or variability in scoring.
• Granularity: Indicates whether benchmarks include a taxonomy for fine-grained error analysis (e.g.,
  complexity tiers, domains, failure modes) instead of only coarse aggregate labeling.
• Modality: Specifies which data types and interaction settings are covered, including text and non-text
  modalities (e.g., images, audio, video), multi-turn behavior, and whether tool-mediated information access
  in agentic settings is part of the evaluation.

              Table 1: Analysis framework features, category sets, and category explanations.

D.2   Comparative analysis

We next compare the various CI benchmarks across the attributes of our analysis framework (summary in
Table 2).

Contextualization. Benchmarks classified as possessing simple contextualization, such as ConfAIde, CI-
Bench, LLM-CI, AgentDAM, and CIMemories, typically ground their information flows in basic, functional
metadata. Context is predominantly established by assigning straightforward domain-specific roles, such as
a doctor, sibling, or financial advisor, and defining explicit, goal-oriented purposes, like applying for a loan
or providing commercial assistance. These frameworks avoid generating multi-faceted sociological profiles,
instead embedding context through operational necessity, brief user instructions, or concise, structured
Likert-scale questions.
Conversely, benchmarks exhibiting rich contextualization construct intricate, multi-layered environments
that move beyond basic labels to simulate realistic human complexity. This approach relies heavily on
detailed user personas, which frequently feature extensive key-value demographic attributes, complex trans-
action histories, and specific narrative goals. Rather than evaluating isolated variables, these benchmarks
procedurally expand abstract privacy seeds into elaborate, paragraph-length vignettes or simulated agent
trajectories, placing the AI within a highly descriptive multi-actor story or deep functional scenario. Fur-
thermore, methodologies like SPILLage embed multiple synthetic attributes into ongoing multi-turn chat
histories or forwarded emails to create dense backgrounds for the agent to navigate.
Among the benchmarks analyzed, several stand out for offering exceptionally rich contextual depth. Pri-
vacyBench introduces a highly dynamic environment by simulating entire communities with evolving user
attributes, such as occupation or location, and time-bound relationships that shift throughout the evaluation.
ConfAIde+ advances narrative depth by incorporating the psychological states of actors, such as feelings
of guilt or pressure, alongside detailed professional histories. Additionally, PrivacyLens and its live vari-
ant PrivacyLens-Live distinguish themselves by translating expressive textual vignettes into highly detailed
simulated agent trajectories, complete with system logs, internal scratchpads, and agent cards that strictly
detail interpersonal relationships. Finally, ConVerse provides substantial complexity by integrating lengthy
email threads and up to 184 distinct natural-language planning options within its simulated commercial
ecosystems.



                                                      108
                         SECTION D: REVIEW AND TAXONOMY OF CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY BENCHMARKS


Transmission principles. Benchmarks categorized with simple transmission principles predominantly
rely on binary appropriateness gates, such as functional necessity and data minimization. Frameworks in-
cluding AirGapAgent, “Form-filling agents,” AgentDAM, CIMemories, and SPILLage evaluate information
flows based on whether a specific data attribute is strictly necessary or functionally relevant to achieve an
immediate operational goal. Other benchmarks in this category focus on the modality of communication or
straightforward social boundaries: PrivacyLens, PrivacyLens-Live, and PrivacyLens+ utilize basic commu-
nication mechanisms like sending an email or direct message rather than invoking nuanced legal conditions.
Similarly, ConfAIde and PrivacyBench rely on standard confidentiality expectations, general incentives, and
static allow/deny rules tied to specific confidant lists or temporal validity. Across these benchmarks, com-
plex, conditional mechanisms such as non-disclosure agreements, reciprocal sharing, and downstream data
deletion are intentionally excluded.
In contrast, benchmarks evaluating complex transmission principles move beyond static, context-independent
rules to integrate highly conditional and legally binding sharing mechanics. Frameworks such as CI-Bench,
MPCI-Bench, and ConfAIde+ introduce structural constraints that require models to navigate explicit user
consent requirements, formal sender and recipient authorization limits, and professional non-disclosure pro-
visions. Furthermore, LLM-CI expands this complexity by systematically varying transmission conditions
to include diverse situational overrides, such as advertising use, specific privacy policy allowances, and emer-
gency situations.
Among the frameworks reviewed, PrivaCI-Bench and ConVerse stand out for offering highly sophisticated
and realistic transmission mechanisms. PrivaCI-Bench establishes rigorous statutory realism by explicitly
decomposing complex legal mandates, such as subpoena compliance, commercial exchanges, and medical
confidentiality, into functional operational factors like purpose and explicit consent. ConVerse, on the other
hand, requires an advanced application of transmission principles by moving entirely away from simple
binary allow/deny outcomes. Instead, it demands that agents perform dynamic generative data abstraction,
forcing models to parse context to share generalized functional requirements rather than raw personal details
depending on the specific scenario constraints.

Ambiguity. It is important to note at the outset that a significant number of benchmarks, including
ConfAIde, PrivacyLens-Live, PrivaCI-Bench, CIMemories, and SPILLage, intentionally exclude ambiguity
entirely by filtering out edge cases, enforcing strict deterministic ground truths, or relying on mutually
exclusive categories. However, among those that do account for it, several frameworks feature implicit
ambiguity. In benchmarks like CI-Bench and AirGapAgent, ambiguity is not actively engineered into the
scenarios or evaluation methodology, but instead emerges organically during the annotation phase through
high-variance or split decisions. Similarly, LLM-CI forces latent norm conflicts structurally by systemat-
ically cross-multiplying contextual parameters without writing explicit narrative contradictions, allowing
researchers to evaluate the resulting ambiguity mathematically through statistical variance.
Benchmarks categorized with explicit ambiguity purposefully design uncertainty and context collapse di-
rectly into their evaluation mechanics. “Form-filling agents” captures this by formally quantifying human
rater entropy and establishing multiple ground-truth aggregation heuristics, such as relaxed consensus or no
veto, to represent differing privacy boundaries. Camber framework Yi et al. (2025b) proposes PrivacyLens+
and ConfAIde+ that deliberately evaluate underspecified contexts by systematically altering narrative vari-
ables and scale scenarios across a disambiguation spectrum, thereby observing shifts in privacy judgments.
Other approaches embed structural traps to force ambiguity resolution: AgentDAM interleaves task-relevant
instructions with task-irrelevant sensitive narratives to test disambiguation, PrivacyBench employs semantic
indirect probing and temporal relationship shifts, MPCI-Bench utilizes a pairwise design testing identical
visual images in conflicting contexts, and ConVerse introduces a dedicated related-and-useful data tier to
create explicit gray areas regarding information granularity.
Among the evaluated frameworks, PrivacyLens+, ConfAIde+, and PrivacyBench present the most challeng-
ing and realistic ambiguity considerations. PrivacyLens+ and ConfAIde+ stand out by making underspeci-
fied context their core focus, systematically injecting varying levels of disambiguating context via the Camber
framework to rigorously measure the entropy of language model judgments in uncertain environments. Pri-
vacyBench provides a highly realistic challenge by modeling ambiguity temporally, where user relationships



                                                      109
APPENDIX                                                                                       ↑ Table of Contents


organically shift over time, and behaviorally through indirect conversational probing, which mimics realistic
social engineering attempts to induce inappropriate data leakage by shifting semantic topics. Finally, Con-
Verse offers a highly nuanced approach by requiring generative data abstraction rather than binary choices,
forcing models to actively navigate the explicit gray area of determining exactly how much granular detail
is functionally useful versus overly private in complex commercial ecosystems.

Norms. Across the analyzed frameworks, most benchmarks incorporate societal norms, some integrate
legal and organizational frameworks, and a few rely entirely on purely statutory or alternative normative
sources. Societal norms are frequently derived from crowdsourced human intuitions meant to reflect everyday
North American expectations. Benchmarks such as ConfAIde and CIMemories also source societal norms
from established academic literature, surveys like Westin’s privacy archetypes, and Pew Research data.
Alternatively, SPILLage defines societal norms through the implicit privacy expectations users hold regarding
data minimization when delegating tasks. Legal norms, when present, are generally extracted directly from
major regulatory frameworks, including U.S. laws like HIPAA and FERPA, or international statutes like
the GDPR, PIPEDA, and the EU AI Act. Organizational norms are sourced from professional boundaries,
encompassing institutional communication rules, corporate ethics, AMA medical codes, and hierarchy-based
disclosure restrictions. Lastly, ConVerse represents a unique “other” category, where norms are not derived
from legal or societal standards, but instead emerge implicitly from domain-specific semantic relationships
within simulated commercial ecosystems.
The codification of these norms within the benchmarks reveals several distinct methodological patterns.
Benchmarks like AirGapAgent, CI-Bench, and SPILLage codify norms loosely as informal, common-sense
directives, guiding the agent to evaluate appropriateness based on general socio-cultural expectations or
basic necessity rather than strict rules. In contrast, “Form-filling agents” utilizes a hierarchical codification
strategy: it establishes baseline appropriateness through societal consensus, but explicitly overrides these
human annotations with strict legal statutes when a scenario touches regulated contexts. Benchmarks focused
heavily on legal realism, such as PrivaCI-Bench, codify norms deterministically by translating complex real-
world litigation histories, corporate privacy policies, and compliance questionnaires into strict permit or
prohibit baseline categories. A highly distinct codification approach is seen in ConVerse, which moves away
from explicitly defining external rule sets entirely; instead, it codifies norms through the natural language
constraints of specific planning tasks, forcing the model to dynamically infer the appropriate boundaries.
For the broadest normative coverage, PrivacyLens and PrivacyLens-Live stand out by successfully integrating
legal, societal, and organizational norms within unified evaluation suites. They achieve this comprehensive
scope by synthesizing U.S. privacy regulations, professional organizational ethics codes, and crowdsourced
human expectations of interpersonal communications into their generated scenarios. Regarding the most
realistic codification of strictly legal norms, PrivaCI-Bench is exceptional due to its reliance on authentic
professional court judgments, constitutional boundaries tracked by the ACLU, and official compliance frame-
works rather than synthetic interpretations. Finally, ConfAIde, ConfAIde+, and AgentDAM present highly
realistic operationalizations of organizational norms; ConfAIde and ConfAIde+ test peer-to-peer workplace
confidentiality, while AgentDAM grounds its normative evaluation in functional corporate software develop-
ment environments to enforce professional boundaries.

Realism. Benchmarks classified under the synthetic realism category rely entirely on generative models or
programmatic templates to construct their evaluation environments, lacking direct grounding in empirical
datasets. Frameworks such as CIMemories, PrivacyBench, and ConVerse utilize frontier large language mod-
els, including gpt-oss-120B, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite, and Claude Sonnet 4, to probabilistically generate user
personas, digital footprints, and multi-turn conversational histories. Other benchmarks utilize synthetic gen-
eration to achieve structural scale or narrative depth; for instance, the primary dataset in LLM-CI consists
of 6,912 information flows built via the combinatorial expansion of contextual parameters, while ConfAIde+
transforms abstract privacy-violating seeds into dense narrative stories. Although SPILLage executes its
tasks on live web platforms, its underlying user personas and shopping prompts remain completely syn-
thetically generated. Among these, PrivacyBench and ConVerse present particularly sophisticated synthetic
environments by generating complex, evolving community social graphs and extensive, multi-turn planning
interactions, respectively.



                                                      110
                         SECTION D: REVIEW AND TAXONOMY OF CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY BENCHMARKS


The grounded category represents a hybrid methodological approach where synthetic data generation is
strictly constrained by, or built upon, real-world statistical distributions, structural templates, or empirical
human seeds. Benchmarks in this tier often utilize crowdsourcing or regulatory texts to establish baseline
scenarios, which are subsequently expanded into evaluation items by language models. For example, Air-
GapAgent generates synthetic demographic profiles that adhere to U.S. Census data to ensure statistical
plausibility, ConfAIde draws from the Pew Study and a legal study of human privacy expectations, while
CI-Bench extracts structural characteristics from publicly available real-world dialogues. The PrivacyLens
family (PrivacyLens, PrivacyLens-Live, PrivacyLens+) grounds its synthetic vignettes in actual U.S. privacy
regulations, academic literature, and human-crowdsourced starting parameters. MPCI-Bench and Agent-
DAM stand out for integrating grounded data with sophisticated modalities; MPCI-Bench firmly grounds
its synthetically generated stories in real-world visual data and human-annotated labels from the existing
VISPR dataset, and AgentDAM expands human-authored narrative seeds into functional scenarios tested
on live software replicas.
The utilization of purely real data within Contextual Integrity benchmarks is exceptionally rare, likely due
to the inherent privacy risks and ethical complications of handling unredacted, sensitive human data in
evaluation datasets. Consequently, only PrivaCI-Bench incorporates a distinct classification of real-world
data within its methodology. Instead of relying on simulated social interactions or synthetically generated
personas, PrivaCI-Bench achieves high statutory realism by directly importing authentic legal and corporate
documentation. Specifically, the benchmark’s HIPAA, GDPR, and ACLU subsets rely on highly realistic data
extracted from historical professional court judgments, active regulatory enforcement trackers, and published
enterprise privacy policies. This direct integration of real-world litigation and compliance data provides
the most legally sophisticated and historically accurate grounding for evaluating normative transmission
principles among all analyzed frameworks.


Annotation. Synthetic annotations across the benchmarks predominantly utilize large language models
or strict programmatic rules to automatically generate ground-truth labels at scale. Benchmarks such as
CIMemories and AirGapAgent rely entirely on frontier models to simulate human privacy personas or gener-
ate labels via multi-step prompting procedures. Alternatively, frameworks like LLM-CI, PrivacyBench, and
AgentDAM employ structural self-annotation, where ground truth is intrinsically established by the data’s
position within a multi-dimensional tensor or predefined sensitive constraints. PrivaCI-Bench also utilizes a
highly deterministic synthetic approach specifically for its EU AI Act subset; because the legislation is new
and lacks established case law, the benchmark generates labels upfront by exhaustively mapping synthetic
scenarios to an official compliance checker questionnaire. Notably, LLM-CI, PrivacyBench, and PrivaCI-
Bench demonstrate particularly rigorous synthetic methodologies by structurally embedding ground truth
directly into the dataset’s generative architecture or deterministic rule sets, minimizing subjective LLM
evaluation drift.
Annotations derived from non-expert raters heavily rely on crowdsourcing platforms to capture broad,
consensus-based societal norms. Frameworks including ConfAIde, PrivacyLens, and PrivacyLens-Live utilize
platforms such as Amazon Mechanical Turk and Prolific to gather baseline privacy expectations and initial
seed data from everyday users. Similarly, the ConfAIde subset of LLM-CI utilizes external paid raters to
provide Likert-scale scores or establish empirical human consensus. “Form-filling agents” stands out for
its methodological rigor in this category; rather than forcing absolute agreement, it actively quantifies the
variance in annotator disagreement (rater entropy) and calculates multiple aggregation heuristics, such as
relaxed consensus or no veto, to formally model ambiguous privacy boundaries.
Expert rater annotations are intended to introduce specialized knowledge to ensure the normative and legal
accuracy of the evaluation datasets. In several frameworks, such as CI-Bench, PrivacyLens, PrivacyLens+,
ConfAIde+, and ConVerse, the internal research teams and authors themselves serve as the expert panels to
manually validate outputs or provide definitive labels. However, the validity of classifying benchmark authors
as independent experts is debatable; while they possess deep familiarity with their own mechanistic designs,
they generally lack formal credentialing in privacy law, compliance, or sociology, which risks introducing
insular bias. In stark contrast, PrivaCI-Bench exhibits the most rigorous and authentic methodology within
this category. Rather than relying on internal researchers for initial legal interpretations, PrivaCI-Bench



                                                      111
APPENDIX                                                                                      ↑ Table of Contents


derives its expert annotations directly from historical professional court judgments and established privacy
policies for its HIPAA, GDPR, and ACLU subsets. Furthermore, to ensure the highest standard of domain-
specific accuracy, these real-world expert annotations were subsequently validated by a human review team
that explicitly included law students to rectify any parsing errors.


Explanation. Only a select minority of the evaluated benchmarks incorporate qualitative explanations
or justifications into their datasets or evaluation pipelines. Across these specific frameworks, explanations
predominantly originate from synthetic sources, utilizing large language models to either generate founda-
tional rationales during dataset construction or to produce intermediate chain-of-thought reasoning during
automated evaluation. For example, AgentDAM relies on an automated LLM-as-a-judge to provide natural
language justifications detailing why specific execution traces constitute privacy leaks. Similarly, ConVerse
utilizes synthetic pre-generated ground-truth ratings that include detailed textual justifications aligning op-
tions with user constraints, alongside crafted semantic rationales for simulated attacks.
Certain benchmarks highlight more nuanced and realistic approaches to generating explanations by anchoring
them in specific societal harms or rigid structural requirements. PrivacyLens is particularly notable for
embedding a specific parameter that justifies its ground-truth labels by explicitly articulating the exact legal
or sociological harm associated with a data flow. Building on this, PrivacyLens-Live employs a specialized
architecture that requires agents to generate an explicit text-based justification and an information flow
extraction for their binary privacy judgments. Additionally, MPCI-Bench ensures foundational clarity by
forcing the creation of an explicitly written justification during its construction phase to firmly ground the
underlying transmission principle. Finally, AirGapAgent demonstrates domain-specific nuance by generating
and storing detailed textual motivation strings for every label, explicitly outlining the functional necessity
of sharing specific data within specialized contexts like healthcare.


Metrics. The evaluation of privacy violations across the benchmarks primarily revolves around measuring
leakage, which tracks precision-oriented unauthorized disclosures and the exposure of sensitive facts. Method-
ologies for computing leakage range from strictly deterministic approaches to more semantic, judge-based
evaluations. Deterministic pipelines often rely on exact string matching, regular expressions, or single-
token parsing against categorical ground truths. While these methods, utilized by “Form-filling agents,”
PrivaCI-Bench, and PrivacyLens+, offer highly reliable and reproducible assessments of static outputs, they
can be structurally rigid. To capture more realistic semantic evasions and implicit disclosures within com-
plex or multi-turn dialogues, several benchmarks instead employ heuristic text-processing (such as BLEU
or BLEURT) or LLM-as-a-judge frameworks. Notably, MPCI-Bench includes a visual leakage metric for
multi-modal evaluations. Alternatively, LLM-CI bypasses strict leakage tracking entirely, measuring norma-
tive alignment mathematically by using statistical distance functions to calculate model divergence from an
expected multidimensional tensor.
Utility metrics complement privacy evaluations by assessing a model’s ability to successfully share function-
ally necessary information and complete designated tasks. These recall-oriented metrics ensure that privacy
mechanisms do not severely degrade system functionality, explicitly penalizing models for unwarranted with-
holding or over-refusal. Methodologically, utility is frequently evaluated deterministically to ensure high
reliability. For instance, “Form-filling agents” calculates the mathematical fraction of required fields suc-
cessfully filled, while AgentDAM performs programmatic state checks directly against a server database to
confirm task execution. In more fluid, narrative environments, benchmarks like CIMemories and SPILLage
leverage LLM judges to semantically parse the generated text, identifying revealed attributes and evaluating
overall task-level completeness.
Benchmarks that integrate multiple complementary methodologies offer the most nuanced and thorough
metric suites. PrivacyLens provides a highly robust framework by identifying explicit leakage in final tool
outputs, calculating a scalar helpfulness score to calibrate for over-refusals, and evaluating static contexts
via deterministic QA probing alongside a few-shot LLM classifier. AgentDAM demonstrates exceptional
methodological realism by evaluating utility through highly reliable programmatic state checks on functional
software replicas, while reserving judge-based semantic evaluation strictly for identifying nuanced privacy



                                                      112
                         SECTION D: REVIEW AND TAXONOMY OF CONTEXTUAL INTEGRITY BENCHMARKS


leaks. Finally, ConVerse stands out for combining snippet extraction with an LLM-as-a-judge to track attack
success rates and utility coverage within complex commercial ecosystems.

Granularity. Across the evaluated frameworks, granularity is most frequently operationalized through
the categorization of benchmark-specific features such as application domains, complexity tiers, and distinct
operational failure modes. Numerous datasets enable fine-grained failure analysis by slicing scenarios into
specific contextual domains, such as healthcare, finance, or travel, allowing researchers to observe domain-
specific degradation in model performance. Furthermore, several benchmarks structure their evaluation
along strict difficulty or complexity tiers, ranging from isolated parametric seeds to complex multi-turn agent
trajectories, to pinpoint the exact threshold where a model’s contextual reasoning breaks down. Granularity
is also applied to operational and modality-specific architectures, enabling the isolation of specific execution
failures. For instance, PrivacyLens-Live explicitly categorizes whether an agent failed at the judgment phase
or during the subsequent action, PrivacyBench distinguishes between retriever-side and generator-side leaks,
and multi-modal benchmarks like MPCI-Bench and SPILLage isolate text-based informational leaks from
visual or behavioral navigation leaks.
In relation to core theoretical features, granularity axes are strategically employed to map failures directly
back to underlying norms, transmission principles, and context ambiguity. Benchmarks like LLM-CI and
PrivacyLens+ perform explicit parametric slicing, manipulating individual contextual parameters (e.g., swap-
ping the data recipient while holding other variables constant) to statistically isolate which specific entity
or transmission rule causes normative divergence. Granularity is also used to differentiate between distinct
normative frameworks, with PrivacyLens explicitly tracking failures across varying norm sources such as
strict U.S. regulations versus general societal expectations. To systematically address ambiguity and com-
plex transmission principles, datasets like “Form-filling agents” and CI-Bench classify targets by underlying
data sensitivity, identifiability, and functional need-to-know constraints. Similarly, ConVerse models ambi-
guity through explicitly defined privacy tiers (e.g., related-and-useful vs. unrelated data) to test how models
navigate implicit contextual gray areas.
The most sophisticated axes of granularity highlight critical systemic failures by categorizing the founda-
tional reasoning flaws and adversarial vulnerabilities of AI agents in high-risk situations. Some scenarios
in ConfAIde are designed to test different aspects of reasoning like tracking information accessibility, while
ConfAIde+ and PrivacyLens+ stand out by utilizing a taxonomy of distinct reasoning codes (such as con-
sent, sender authorization, and purpose), which enables researchers to diagnose exactly which structural
transmission principle a model fundamentally misunderstands. Similarly, CI-Bench breaks down errors by
functional stage, identifying whether a model failed to extract the context or failed to identify the governing
norm. For uncovering high-risk security vulnerabilities, benchmarks that introduce granularity into their
threat modeling provide the most robust insights. ConVerse categorizes failures across specific commercial
attack vectors like toolkit manipulation, AirGapAgent isolates context-preserving versus context-hijacking
adversaries, and PrivacyBench contrasts direct questioning against indirect semantic probing, effectively
exposing how sophisticated agents might systemically leak data when subjected to nuanced conversational
evasion.

Modality. The predominant modality across the evaluated benchmarks is text, though the structural
variability within these text-only datasets enables the assessment of diverse AI capabilities. At the most
fundamental level, frameworks like LLM-CI rely on decontextualized, single-turn text strings to evaluate
static alignment. A significant cluster of benchmarks, including PrivacyLens+, ConfAIde+, and PrivaCI-
Bench, utilizes probing-style prompts where models answer direct, single-turn questions regarding written
narrative vignettes or legal scenarios. Expanding into structural diversity, “Form-filling agents” evaluates
text-based data payloads and web form descriptions rather than narrative stories. To capture relational
dynamics, CI-Bench and ConfAIde depart from single-prompt formats by incorporating multi-turn text data,
presenting scenarios as conversational chat dialogues, asynchronous email chains, or synthesized meeting
transcripts.
To evaluate more sophisticated autonomous systems, several benchmarks expand beyond text to incorporate
visual modalities and agentic capabilities such as multi-turn execution and tool use. Notably, video and audio



                                                      113
APPENDIX                                                                                    ↑ Table of Contents


modalities are entirely absent from all the evaluated datasets. However, image processing is actively tested:
AgentDAM utilizes raw screenshots and Set-of-Marks visual bounding boxes, MPCI-Bench tests vision-
language models in agent-based settings by combining images with text prompts, and SPILLage processes
visual screenshots alongside web DOM structures. Furthermore, assessing autonomous agent behavior relies
heavily on tool use and multi-turn planning. Benchmarks like PrivacyLens, PrivacyLens-Live, and ConVerse
require models to navigate multi-step workflows by actively querying simulated software endpoints and API
sandboxes covering productivity and financial applications, among others. Similarly, PrivacyBench requires
RAG agents to actively fetch historical data across up to ten conversational turns.
The benchmarks offering the most complex and realistic representations seamlessly integrate multiple modal-
ities with deep environmental interaction. AgentDAM and SPILLage present the most comprehensive multi-
modal agent testing environments; both require models to process complex text structures (accessibility
trees or DOMs) and images simultaneously while executing long-horizon, multi-turn navigation using live
or replica browser automation tools like clicking, typing, and scrolling. Within text-driven agentic systems,
PrivacyLens-Live offers exceptional realism by testing multi-step workflows natively using the Model Context
Protocol (MCP) and Agent2Agent frameworks. Finally, MPCI-Bench provides a highly nuanced approach
to visual privacy by executing its evaluation through a pairwise design, testing the exact same visual image
across different contextual text scenarios to explicitly force vision-language models to navigate multi-modal
contextual ambiguity.
